# AgentSpaces Specification v0.1.32 (Draft)

**A Peer-to-Peer Coordination Suite for Agents, Combining JXTA-Style Peering with JavaSpaces-Style Data Sharing**

Draft specification, September 2026. This document stands alone and assumes only the companion P2P review. Version 0.1.1 adds the AssetCard addendum (§6.1a), which admits data-provider peers to the advertisement model; version 0.1.2 adds the security addendum (§11a), which specifies the group payload encryption and sealed key distribution the v0.1 draft deferred to v0.2; version 0.1.3 adds §11a.4, the authenticated-state-transition mechanism that closes a forgery gap the earlier drafts left open, and reconciles the §8 capability catalogue with the reference implementation; version 0.1.4 promotes the QUIC transport binding from future work to a shipped reference implementation (§5.5, §9) and resolves the planner question §14 had left open with §10.6, the remote-actions bridge that lets a planner treat the fleet's AgentCards as available actions; version 0.1.5 completes the annotation programming model (§10.3): choreography as a returning `@SpaceNotify`, composed stereotypes, sole-space inference, simple duration strings, and `@SpaceRef` injection; version 0.1.6 adds §5.6, channel authentication: identity-endorsed transport certificates that let authenticated channels stand in for the per-frame envelope signature, with the evidence signatures of §7 and §11a unchanged; version 0.1.7 adds the §7.2 matching-order guarantee that came with the type-partitioned index; version 0.1.8 records the accepted threats and the definite-length canonical form; version 0.1.9 adds the RevocationAdvertisement, identity rotation by successor, and the enterprise-CA channel mode; version 0.1.10 reconciles the text with the reference implementation after a clause-by-clause coverage review: it defines the founding document and join by GroupID (§4.4, §5.1, §9, §10.1), describes membership and gossip as shipped (§5.2, §5.3), completes the space lifecycle and the tombstone and claim rules (§7.3 to §7.5), the aggregate operators and gossip-learn exchange (§8), and the Spring capability surface (§10.5), and states the envelope, kind list, and wire version precisely (§9); version 0.1.11 adds credential and authorizer space admission (§7.5), the authorization model under which the profile-selected `Authorizer` arbitrates every privileged operation (§11), the bearer principal model on the console and A2A surfaces (§12), and the starter properties for all three (§10.1); version 0.1.12 adds the embedder identity on semantic discovery (§8) and the `MODEL_SERVE` operation (§11) that the Spring AI integration's model service needs; version 0.1.13 judges agent certificates at signing time and lets them renew (§4.2), has attested agents sign their own state transitions (§11a.4), makes everything certified revocable (agents, agent keys, channel leaves, join credentials) and lets a CA root a peer revocation with evidence (§6.1), adds content-key epochs, rotation, and per-agent key wrap (§11a.1 to §11a.3), declares each agent's actions on its card (§6.1), and adds the A2A gateway auto-configuration (§12) and QUIC parity with TLS (§5.5); version 0.1.14 lets templates match on tags before decoding (§7.2), lets a binder name entry types through the registry its spaces use, adds the `tags` attribute, the `Tagged` return, and the `@SpaceJoin` fan-in to the annotation model (§10.3), reserves the `JoinTicket` entry type (§7.1), and completes the workflow vocabulary with the `Entries` fork, `where` filters, sealed returns on the card, the gather, event-kind selection on a reaction, and `@OnEstimate` (§8, §10.3); version 0.1.15 moves the wire to version 3 with a canonical order for map entries (§9) and adds `@SpaceReduce`, the persistent accumulator (§10.3); version 0.1.16 paces the idle fleet (§5.2, §5.3): the self-advertisement refreshes on an interval rather than every tick and is introduced directly to newcomers, the probe runs once per protocol period in round-robin, anti-entropy backs off while nothing moves, and a settled push-sum epoch goes silent (§8); version 0.1.17 adds lazy push to the rumor channel (§5.3), session attestation for connections the transport cannot attest (§5.6), and the hint-gated kinds `BATCH`, `HAVE`, `GRAFT`, `PRUNE`, and `SESSION_HELLO` (§9); version 0.1.29 adds the persistent data trees (SRAD agentspaces-pdt): a tree root beside the piece root in `payloadRef` and the tree escalation of a space with its pre-seal rule (§7.1), the recordless state delta and the `recordless` hint (§7.3, §9), the `trees` parameter and the tags 8 to 11 of the content swarm (§8), and annex H, the node, value, blob, ref, message, and sealing formats; version 0.1.30 bounds the stamps of the hybrid logical clock: a stamp is acceptable only in its canonical text, with no leading zero, a physical part within a signed 64-bit integer, a counter from 0 to 65536, and a node free of colons and control characters; a receiver refuses any other stamp wherever it decodes, merges, orders, or deduplicates one, counts the refusal, and strikes the signer where it keeps strikes; the merge clamps a remote physical part one millisecond inside the drift ceiling; a clock carries into the next millisecond where it used to saturate; and a receiver drops a frame at most five seconds past its far-future ceiling without a strike (§7.3, §7.4, §9, §11); the same revision's fourth review judges an agent-signed state's `signedAt` like any other stamp, makes the `pdt.issued` head stamp advisory, fixes the CBOR item a stamp travels as, defers a state or claim stamped past the far-future ceiling and its margin, refusing one only an hour further out, counts refusals under one key and one set of names, and has every peer validate its own node (§7.3, §9, §11a.4, annex H); version 0.1.31 defines the wire encoding, one CBOR form for every value with item rules, record rules, and the exact types of application big numbers, and has every receiver refuse a carrier unless encoding what it decoded gives back exactly the bytes it received, refuse a wrong-typed protocol field, judge an agent's `signedAt` by the far-future rule, read `PRef.attrs` as text, and bound its claim map (§7.3, §7.4, §9.1, §9.2, annexes H and I); the same revision's seventh review bounds a bignum at 1000 bytes and a stamp at 3996 UTF-16 code units, adds the capability pipe records to annex I, and has every reader refuse a signed beacon document unless it arrives as exactly its canonical JSON (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08; §9, §9.1, annex I).; version 0.1.32 closes the AppCard's `kind` to four connection values with a new meaning for `assembled`, adds the open `origin` and `formats` fields, makes `signOn` `idp` or `none`, gates the new fields on the hint `wire.appcard=2`, replaces the 4 KiB card sentence with a byte budget on every advertisement cache, and moves the large parts of a big `AppCard` or `AgentCard` into a content-swarm document its readers fetch on demand (AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09; §6.1a.1 to §6.1a.5, §6.3, §9, annex I), and, in the same revision, PDT 2 (srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md) adds the `content-store` card, the pin, signed pin, and receipt entries, the content-swarm feature level `pdt` with its possession challenge, set reconciliation, and intra-chunk deltas, eager push of ref deltas, and the DataScript, relaxed-vector, text, version-graph, and BLAKE3 outboard node kinds behind `PRef.v` 2 and reserved attributes, closes the two gaps §9.3 recorded, and states the waits on other peers as guidance (§5.3, §6.1a.6, §7.1, §8, §9.3, §9.4, annexes G, H, and I). §15 records the change history.

## 1. Vision

AgentSpaces is a peer-to-peer suite of services that lets autonomous agents, including LLM-driven agents, robots, sensor nodes, and conventional services, discover each other, form scoped peer groups, and coordinate work through a shared, replicated, associative memory. Fleets of heterogeneous agents thus gain discovery, teaming, and tasking with no central broker, and operators gain a coordination fabric that keeps working through partitions and churn. The design fuses the two strongest ideas of the early-2000s Java distributed-computing stack and upgrades their weak points with the lessons of the intervening twenty years.

From JXTA, AgentSpaces takes the peering model: stable cryptographic peer identity independent of network location, peer groups as scoped trust and discovery domains, advertisements as the universal self-describing discovery currency, and a topology that tolerates NAT, churn, and heterogeneous transports through rendezvous and relay roles.

From Jini and JavaSpaces, AgentSpaces takes the coordination model: a typed tuple space with `write`, `read`, and `take` operations and template matching, leases on all state so that nothing lives forever without an owner renewing it, event notification for reactive patterns, and the replicated-worker "bag of tasks" as the canonical tasking idiom.

The upgrades draw from the modern P2P canon. Gossip protocols (SWIM-style membership, epidemic dissemination, push-sum aggregation) replace JXTA's loosely consistent rendezvous walker as the dissemination layer. CRDT replication replaces the centralized space implementation, which makes the tuple space partition-tolerant and offline-capable. Content addressing handles bulk payloads. For exclusivity on `take`, JavaSpaces relied on a global transaction monitor; AgentSpaces instead offers a spectrum of conflict-resolution strategies, from lease-based deterministic arbitration to CBBA-style probabilistic auction-consensus, selectable per space.

Finally, AgentSpaces is capability-extensible. Gossip learning, decentralized aggregation, voting, auction-based allocation, and future conversational agreement services all take the form of capability services that peers advertise, discover, and bind to through the same advertisement machinery the core uses for everything else. The core stays small: identity, peering, advertisements, and the space.

The first delivery target is an Embabel extension (`embabel-agentspaces`). Embabel's Spring-native annotation model (`@Agent`, `@Action`, `@Goal`, `@Condition`), its typed domain-object blackboard, and its API/SPI split give AgentSpaces both a natural host and a design language. An AgentSpace generalizes Embabel's per-process blackboard into a shared, leased, peer-to-peer blackboard spanning many agents on many machines, and Embabel agent metadata (descriptions, goals, input/output types) supplies exactly the raw material from which the extension generates capability advertisements automatically.

## 2. Design Principles

**P1: The weakest sufficient primitive.** AgentSpaces treats consensus as a scarce resource. Default semantics are eventually consistent (gossip plus CRDTs), exclusivity is lease-scoped, and total ordering is available only as an opt-in capability service. Every API that offers a stronger guarantee names its cost.

**P2: Everything is leased.** All shared state, including entries, advertisements, group memberships, capability bindings, and subscriptions, carries a TTL and vanishes unless its owner renews it. Failure handling therefore reduces, in most cases, to the simple absence of renewal. AgentSpaces inherits this semantic from Jini, and it is the single most important inheritance in the design.

**P3: Everything is a signed advertisement.** Typed, signed, TTL-bearing documents describe peers, groups, spaces, capabilities, and services. Discovery, trust, and binding all operate on advertisements. JXTA supplied this insight; AgentSpaces makes the signatures mandatory.

**P4: Groups are the unit of everything.** Each peer group defines its own membership policy, gossip scope, space replication scope, and capability visibility. A peer participates in many groups, and a deployment's bootstrap group, when it defines one (§5.1), is simply the outermost.

**P5: Typed entries, domain-first.** Following Embabel's domain-model philosophy, space entries are plain Java records or POJOs, matched by type plus template and serialized with a stable schema. The core API accepts no stringly-typed blobs.

**P6: Small core, advertised extensions.** The core normative surface is Layers 0 through 3 (§4 through §7). Learning, voting, auctions, ordering, and every other extension arrive as capability services (Layer 4), each with its own advertisement type and binding handshake.

**P7: Local-first, transport-agnostic.** A node with zero connectivity still works against its local replicas, and synchronization proceeds opportunistically. Transports (TCP, QUIC, WebSocket, Zenoh/DDS bridges, even sneakernet sync) sit behind an SPI, so the choice of transport remains orthogonal to the coordination semantics above it.

## 3. Architecture Overview

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Layer 4  CAPABILITY SERVICES (advertised, pluggable)            │
│   task-auction (CBBA)  ·  vote  ·  aggregate (push-sum)         │
│   gossip-learn  ·  ordered-log (Raft quorum)  ·  custom…        │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Layer 3  AGENTSPACE (distributed tuple space)                   │
│   write/read/take/notify · templates · leases · CRDT replication│
│   conflict-resolution strategies · content-addressed payloads   │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Layer 2  ADVERTISEMENT & DISCOVERY                              │
│   typed signed documents · TTL · local ad-cache · queries       │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Layer 1  PEERING                                                │
│   peer groups · SWIM membership · gossip dissemination          │
│   rendezvous & relay roles · transport SPI                      │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Layer 0  IDENTITY                                               │
│   keypair PeerID · signatures · (optional) DID interop          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Each layer depends only on the layers below it. A conforming minimal implementation comprises Layers 0 through 3 on a single LAN with the default conflict strategy; capability services and WAN topology are additive.

## 4. Layer 0: Identity

**4.1 PeerID.** Every peer generates an Ed25519 (RFC 8032) keypair on first start and derives `PeerID = multibase(sha-256(public-key))`. The ID stays stable across restarts, addresses, and transports, because the peer persists it in its keystore. The originating peer signs all protocol messages and all advertisements with its key, and receivers MUST verify signatures before caching or acting.

**4.2 AgentID.** A peer may host multiple logical agents, as an Embabel application typically does. `AgentID = (PeerID, local-name)`. Agents inherit the peer's key for transport-level signing; an agent MAY additionally carry its own subordinate Ed25519 keypair, certified by the peer key, when per-agent non-repudiation matters (v0.1.11, QA4 A4-7 phase 1). The certificate is `AgentCertificate = (agent, agentPublicKey, issued, ttl, peerSignature, encryptionPublicKey?)`, where the optional X25519 `encryptionPublicKey` (v0.1.13) is appended and omitted when absent (§11a.2a): canonical CBOR, the peer's signature covering the record minus the signature field, valid from `issued` until `issued + ttl` exclusive, and issuable only by the peer the AgentID names. A record written under a subordinate identity is signed by the agent key and travels with the certificate beside the peer key (§7.1, §11a.4); a receiver MUST verify the certificate under the peer key, require it to name exactly the record's issuer and to cover the record's signing time, which means that its issue stamp's physical time lies in `[issued, issued + ttl)` (v0.1.13; earlier revisions judged expiry on the receiver's clock, which made an agent's history unmergeable once its certificate lapsed), and then verify the record under the certificate's agent key. For a space's records and take claims the certificate check sets no bound of its own on how far the signing time leads the receiver's clock: once the signature and the certificate verify, the receiver judges the record's `issued` stamp or the claim's stamp by the far-future rule of §7.3, exactly as it judges a peer-signed carrier's, so it merges, defers, or refuses the carrier and strikes the signer only for a hostile stamp (HLC round five, 2026-11-08). The certificate check of an agent-signed state's `signedAt` (§11a.4) sets no such bound either: once the signature and the certificate verify, the far-future rule of §7.3 judges `signedAt` (HLC round six, 2026-11-08, owner ruling F4). The other certificate checks keep the bound of the receiver's clock plus `MAX_DRIFT`: an agent card's attestation at the card's `issued` time, and a group key request. A signer therefore attaches the certificate that covers its own signing time, and a writer holding none refuses to sign. Certificates renew: a peer MAY re-issue an agent's certificate for the same key before it lapses (the reference implementation re-issues at half-life, 24 h lifetimes by default, and keeps earlier certificates so signatures made under them stay certifiable), so an agent runs indefinitely while each signature stays judged by the certificate of its time. The accepted cost is that a stolen agent key could sign records back-dated into an old window; revocation with reason `key-compromise` (§6.1) is the remedy. Agent keys MAY persist: the reference implementation stores each subordinate agent's Ed25519 and X25519 pairs under `<agent-keystore>/<peer-id>/<local-name>/` (private keys PKCS#8 and owner-only, written atomically, loaded only after a probe proves the pair matches), and re-issues certificates at start, so an agent keeps its attested identity across restarts. A local space offers the same per-agent views without signatures, so attribution reads the same in tests and in a single process. A record that arrives with a certificate but a peer-key signature is refused, and a record without a certificate verifies under the peer key as before, so a fleet that never opts in is unchanged on the wire. Attribution is therefore per agent: a replica reports each entry as `PEER_ASSERTED`, the peer vouched for the name, or `AGENT_ATTESTED`, the agent's own key signed it. Eligibility remains an authorization decision made at the authorizer's granularity (§8 QUORUM, §11): a certificate proves who signed, never what they may do. Several agents on one node share one replica of a space through *views* (QA4 A4-7 phase 3): a view is the same space seen as another agent of the same peer, with one replica, one gossip registration, and one clock per node, whose writes are issued and signed by that agent, whose take claims and completions name that agent as holder, and whose admission (§7.5) is judged for that agent, so two views of one replica can be admitted differently. A view has no state of its own and cannot act as another peer's agent. The binder (§10.3) hands an agent with a key of its own views of every space it is bound to, and its AgentCard (§6.1) carries the agent's public key in an appended field, so readers see the card's agent is attested. A take claim made through a view is signed by the view's agent key: the proof still carries the holder's *peer* key (so it hashes to the holder's peer as it always has) and appends the peer's certificate, and a receiver applies the same two-key rule it applies to records: the certificate under the peer key for exactly the claim's holder, covering the claim's stamp (which a renewal keeps), then the signature under the certificate's agent key; a proof carrying a certificate but a peer-key signature is refused. State transitions (§11a.4) are signed by the acting agent's own key when that agent is attested (v0.1.13): the writer for its renewals and cancellations, the claim holder for its completion, so no sibling agent and not even the peer can act in an attested agent's name. An agent whose certificate carries an encryption key MAY fetch group content keys for itself through per-agent key wrap (§11a.2a, v0.1.13), sealed to that certified key, so a sibling agent on the same peer learns nothing from it.

**4.3 DID interoperability (informative).** A PeerID is representable as `did:key:…`, and a resolver SPI allows group membership policies to accept W3C DIDs and verifiable credentials issued elsewhere. This keeps AgentSpaces alignable with ANP-style cross-organization identity without depending on it.

**4.4 Naming.** Advertisement identifiers are URIs under a common scheme: a group's advertisement carries `aspace://<groupID>`, a space's carries `aspace://<groupID>/<spaceName>`, and an entry is addressed as `aspace://<groupID>/<spaceName>#<entryID>`. GroupIDs and SpaceIDs are hashes of their founding documents, which makes them self-certifying: possession of the founding document proves the name, and nobody can publish a different policy under the same name, because a receiver that re-derives the identifier from the document it was handed detects the substitution.

The founding document of a group is defined precisely. The *founding fields* are the GroupAdvertisement's immutable fields in this order: `name`, `founder` (the issuer PeerID), `issued`, `ttl`, `membershipPolicy`, `defaultStrategy`, `gossip`, encoded as canonical CBOR. The founder signs those bytes with its Ed25519 identity key. The *founding document* is the pair (founding fields, founder signature), again as canonical CBOR, and `GroupID = multibase(sha-256(founding document))`. The advertisement's `id` is `"aspace://" + GroupID`. A SpaceID is `multibase(sha-256(canonical CBOR of the founding SpaceAdvertisement))`; a purely local, unadvertised space derives its SpaceID from its name instead.

## 5. Layer 1: Peering

**5.1 Peer groups.** A PeerGroup is the unit of scope: it defines a membership policy, a gossip domain, and a namespace for spaces and capabilities. A founder creates a group by publishing a signed GroupAdvertisement containing the group name, the GroupID (the self-certifying hash of §4.4), the membership policy, the default conflict strategy for the group's spaces, and gossip parameters (fan-out and period). The founding advertisement circulates as a signed document: the advertisement, the founder's raw public key, and the founder's signature over the founding fields. A receiver verifies it with four checks: the key hashes to the advertisement's issuer, the signature verifies over the founding fields, the GroupID re-derived from the founding document equals the advertisement's group, and the `id` URI names that group. Every group advertisement learned from the network MUST pass this check before a node joins or acts on it, and a node MUST NOT serve a newcomer a group whose advertisement it cannot verify. A locally configured advertisement with a literal GroupID is exempt, because it is configuration the network cannot swap, and a node never serves such an advertisement to newcomers. A peer that knows only a GroupID and a seed endpoint joins by asking the seed for the founding document (`GROUP_AD_WANT` and `GROUP_AD`, §9) and verifying the answer against the GroupID it already holds; a hostile seed can delay that join and can never substitute a policy.

The membership policy is `OPEN`; `INVITE`, which requires a credential signed by a founder or admin key; or `POLICY`, a pluggable validator SPI such as a DID credential check. All three are enforced at admission: a verified signature proves an advertisement's authorship and says nothing about whether its author may join, so an OPEN group admits every verified peer, an INVITE group admits only peers presenting a founder-signed credential bound to their PeerID, and a POLICY group admits only peers the group's validator accepts. Groups map naturally onto task teams, mirroring JXTA's subgroup pattern: an application spins up a group per mission, per tenant, or per conversation.

*Delegated join credentials (v0.1.18 addendum, normative).* An `INVITE` credential has two forms under the same `aspace:join` resource hint, and every member verifies either with public data against the founding advertisement. The founder-issued form is the founder's signature over `(candidate, group)`. The delegated form is a *delegation*, the founder's signature over `(inviteKey, group, notAfter, label)` for an Ed25519 key the founder does not hold, and the invite key's signature over `(candidate, group)` under it; its string form is prefixed `d1:` so the two never parse as each other. A verifier accepts the delegated form when the founder's key hashes to the founding's issuer, the delegation signature verifies, `notAfter` is not before the verifier's clock less the ten-minute skew advertisements allow, the candidate signature verifies under the delegated key, and neither the credential's hash nor the delegation's hash is revoked (§6.1). The delegation is public data; whoever holds the delegated key's secret admits themselves until it expires or the founder revokes it. This is what lets a group of people share one key out of band (an *invite key*, SRAD discovery-bootstrap-customization §3.8) with no service in the loop: a beacon that seeds and relays for them is admitted under the same delegation and holds no secret. A delegation's `notAfter` bounds first admission (v0.1.20, normative): a verifier that admitted a peer under a delegated credential while the delegation was valid continues to admit that peer under the same credential after `notAfter`, until the credential or the delegation is revoked (§6.1); a peer it never admitted, or one presenting a different credential under the expired delegation, is refused as before. Implementations SHOULD persist this record of first admissions across restarts, and the reference implementation keeps it beside the keystore, one file per group, holding peer ids and credential hashes alone.

*Bootstrap groups (informative).* Earlier drafts named a universal `OPEN` WorldGroup as the root of discovery. Seed peers, rendezvous roles (§5.4), the starter's `seeds` configuration, and the multicast beacon (§10.1) supply bootstrap today, and a universal open group would add a Sybil and flood surface with no consumer. A deployment MAY define a well-known bootstrap group of its own; the core defines none.

**5.2 Membership and failure detection.** Within a group, every member periodically republishes a leased, signed self-advertisement (its PeerAdvertisement) on the reserved `peers` gossip stream, and this refresh is the liveness proof: a member's advertisement is a claim only that member can make. The refresh is paced (v0.1.16, v0.1.17): a node sends its advertisement directly and unforwarded to each member once per advertisement interval (the reference implementation's default is 30 s, capped at half the advertisement TTL; and once the group's probe cycle, one member per probe period stretched up to four times when quiet, has outgrown the member TTL, at half that TTL, since past that size the probes no longer reach every member inside the TTL and the refresh is the keepalive), floods it at once when its content changes (a flood reaches a member through a third where a direct link is cut; a refresh flooded on an idle fleet cost every member several copies, since nothing else had pruned the gossip tree, and a flood on every membership view change made one expiry at fifty members into forty-nine floods, so a view change floods nothing: a newcomer is introduced to directly, a lost member by the retry below), and otherwise sends it directly, once and unforwarded, to each member it has not introduced itself to, falling back to a flood when any member cannot be reached; it introduces itself on every channel it dials and, the moment it admits a newcomer from the newcomer's own introduction, answers with its own introduction on that same channel (v0.1.17: ordered delivery then has the newcomer hold it before any later frame, where waiting for the next tick lost every frame pushed meanwhile as a non-member's), closes its channel to a member that leaves its view, and is told at once by its transport when a channel ends at either end (`TransportConnection.onClose` in the SPI, v0.1.17; the `tcp`, `tls`, and `quic` bindings signal it from the end of their read loops), so the member on the other side forgets the channel and re-dials and re-introduces itself on its next tick rather than when a later send fails; a channel a node gives up on after a failed send is closed, not merely forgotten, so both ends restart from a fresh handshake and neither keeps a session the other has dropped; a frame that fails on a dead channel is retried once over a fresh one. Two members that lost each other for silence, with no third member to carry an introduction, would otherwise stay apart for good: for ten minutes after a member's loss a node introduces itself to that member's last advertised endpoints, and a node whose view is empty dials its seeds again the same way, first after five seconds and then at an interval that doubles after each round nobody answered, up to thirty seconds, and starts again from five on an admission (so a pair alone under a one-way loss re-admits and re-drops at a bounded rate rather than at full rate for as long as the loss lasts); a member removed by an authoritative eviction or a revocation is not courted; and the view itself is anti-entropy state (the reference implementation's `view` stream, v0.1.17): a node's digest names the live set as it sees it, itself included, a partner lacking a member receives that member's signed advertisement in the delta and admits it as it would a forwarded copy (under attestation a copy admits nobody), except that a member the node dropped for silence comes back only on an advertisement issued after the drop, which only a member alive since could have made; an introduction that reached nobody's random fan-out thus still converges, and nothing floods to make it so (v0.1.17). A node sends nothing at all to a peer it holds in local quarantine, and opens no channel to it. An earlier revision flooded the advertisement on every tick, which cost every member a signature verification of every other member several times a second. Membership state is leased (P2): the group drops, without ceremony, a member whose advertisement lapses and who neither gossips nor responds within its membership TTL. A SWIM-style probe cycle shortens detection: once per protocol period (the reference implementation's default is one second, v0.1.16; earlier revisions probed on every tick) a node pings the next member of a shuffled round-robin order, so every member is probed once per cycle, an unanswered ping triggers an indirect ping-req through k other members, and a member unanswered through the indirect stage is marked suspect; probe timeouts are judged on every tick. While the group is quiet (no gossip activity since the last tick, no member admitted, no probe unanswered) the probe period doubles at each probe up to four times the base and snaps back to the base on the first active tick (v0.1.17). The probe and its acknowledgement piggyback (v0.1.17, memberlist-style; the fields are null from older nodes) a SHA-256 of the sender's anti-entropy digest per stream, or the root hash over them when it holds more than sixteen streams, and the items it forwarded before the current tick (at most thirty-two): a member learns of what it missed from the probes it receives anyway (§5.3), and a difference on a stream both hold, while the periodic anti-entropy round has backed off, starts a targeted round with that member at most once per gossip period per member. Suspicion is local state; any frame heard from the member clears it and refreshes its lease. AgentSpaces disseminates no JOIN, ALIVE, SUSPECT, or DEAD verdicts about third parties, because a verdict about another member is exactly the kind of unauthenticated claim the rest of the design refuses; members learn of a join from the joiner's own signed advertisement and of a departure from its absence. A peer leaves a group by ceasing to refresh and to answer in it (informative, v0.1.20): the reference implementation's `leaveGroup` releases everything the node holds for that group, sends nothing more in it, and keeps its other groups and the links it shares with their members, and the other members drop the leaver by the membership TTL as they drop a member that lapsed; there is no leave frame. The membership view feeds a peer-sampling service exposed to upper layers (`PeerSampler.randomMembers(n)`), and this sampler is the primitive from which space anti-entropy, capability queries, and Layer 4 protocols all draw their partners.

**5.3 Gossip dissemination.** Each group runs a gossip bus with two channels. The rumor channel pushes new items (advertisement announcements, entry deltas, capability frames) on named streams to `fanout` sampled members, and each hop forwards to `fanout` further members while a per-item hop budget lasts. The hop budget is a constant, 6; a publisher MAY choose a smaller budget for an item, and a receiver clamps any larger inbound budget to the constant. The anti-entropy channel pulls a digest reconciliation from one random partner once per `period` per group on the node's own clock while anything moves, with the first round immediately after joining and (v0.1.17) a targeted round with each newly admitted member on the tick after its admission, so a newcomer pulls the cards and space state the fleet already holds at once rather than when its random partner happens to hold them, which guarantees convergence; while a group is idle (no rumor published or received, no delta sent or applied since the last round) the period doubles per round up to 32 times `period` and returns to `period` on the next activity (v0.1.16; anti-entropy is the backstop, not the carrier); the `peers` stream counts as liveness, not activity. A member admitted since the last tick gets a targeted round on that tick (§15, v0.1.17). Toward a member whose advertisement carries `wire.hashes=1` a round opens with a SHA-256 of the digest per stream (v0.1.17): the partner names, in a `DIGEST` carrying `want`, the streams both hold whose hashes differ, or answers nothing, and the asker then sends its full digests, which grow with the state, for those streams alone, answered with the deltas as before; two members in sync exchange one small frame per round, and a stranger's `want` is never answered. Rumor forwarding runs unpaced as frames arrive. *Lazy push (v0.1.17).* The random fan-out delivers each item to each member about `fanout` times; between members that advertise the hint `wire.tree=1` the bus pushes lazily instead, after Leitão, Pereira, and Rodrigues' Plumtree: an item goes eagerly (as a `RUMOR`) to every such member the node has not pruned, and a member that receives a duplicate over an eager edge demotes the sender with a `PRUNE`, which cuts the edge at both ends, so the eager edges converge on a spanning tree over which each item reaches each member once. For eight ticks after a change of the tree or the view (a prune or graft either way, a member admitted or dropped) a node tells every pruned member, in a `HAVE` on each tick, which items (stream and payload hash) it forwarded since the last; for the next twenty-four ticks it does so every fourth tick; after that it announces nothing on its own, since a member can be cut off from the tree only by such a change, and the probes carry the recent items instead (§5.2). A member that hears of an item it never received asks an announcer for it with a `GRAFT`, which restores that edge and is answered with the item. It waits longer than an eager push takes, batching included, since a graft any sooner fetches a copy already on its way and the duplicate prunes a sound edge. Since v0.1.32 (guidance, §9.4; swarm retries, 2026-11-09) the member times the wait by the clock and never by ticks: it asks 500 ms after the first `HAVE` plus a uniform spread over a window of twice the 90th-percentile graft round trip it observes, at least 1 s and at most 4 s, and it asks a random announcer other than the first heard when it knows another. Later asks back off 1, 2, 4, and 8 s with the same spread and cycle through the announcers again, and the member keeps the item while an announcer in its view announced it within the last 15 s, under a bound on grafts per tick and on remembered announcements. A first copy pushed over a pruned edge restores it; a copy delivered point to point or on the `peers` stream does not, just as such a duplicate never prunes. A group with no more members than `fanout` reaches in one hop prunes nothing: every member pushes to every other, each item arrives at most `fanout` times, and no claim or bid ever waits on a repair, which a settle window of a few ticks relies on. Which edge a duplicate prunes is the duplicate's sender, except that a duplicate that travelled at least two hops fewer than the first copy replaces the first copy's sender instead (Plumtree's path optimisation, with its threshold: swapping on a one-hop difference, which every triangle produces for items from different sources, leaves the shared partition source-dependent and a node pruning every edge in turn), and that an origin's own push is never pruned in favour of a relayed copy that beat it, since an asynchronous fabric does that at both receivers at once and would orphan the origin; and a node that receives its own item back around a cycle prunes nothing, since its direct edge to the sender is the shortest path there is. A member leaving the view, or sentenced to local quarantine, may have been the tree's only path to others, so a node then forgets every prune, pushes eagerly again until duplicates re-form the tree, and asks every other member with a `GRAFT` that names no item to make it eager again, so no item of theirs keeps routing through the member it lost. The `peers`, `revocations`, and `credential-revocations` streams are pushed to every capable member whatever the tree says, so a revocation never waits on repair, and announcements and probe piggybacks name only items forwarded since the member in question was admitted: what came before is history, which its anti-entropy brings with revocations ahead of the records they judge. A duplicate never prunes when it arrived with a hop budget of one (a direct introduction or a graft answer) or on the `peers` stream, which is delivered directly and flooded on purpose. Members without the hint keep the random fan-out from every node and never receive `HAVE`, `GRAFT`, or `PRUNE`; anti-entropy remains the backstop for both. Receivers deduplicate items by stream and payload hash (`sha-256` of the item payload) against a bounded seen-cache, so an attacker cannot suppress an honest item by pre-seeding its identifier. Expected propagation is O(log N) rounds. The GroupAdvertisement supplies `fanout` and `period` (defaults 3 and one second); no view-size parameter exists, because membership views are full and leased. §9 gives the envelope. Toward members that do not advertise the tree hint (an older node, a dial-only client), the `fanout` slots of a rumor's origin are filled two ways (v0.1.17): all but one with members the node holds a live link to and has not failed to reach lately, in random order, and the last with the member it pushed to least recently, so that the push stays reliable, nobody is left out for long, and no fixed pair is settled on; a push that could not leave frees its slot. A forwarder along the tree pushes to such members only when they have no endpoint of their own and it holds their link (a client behind its seed), since every other one had its chance at the origin and gets the rest by anti-entropy, where every forwarder pushing to every one of them cost each several copies of every item. A group with no tree-capable member at all forwards with the same two-and-one rule at every hop, as the epidemic requires.

**5.4 Topology roles.** Any peer may additionally serve as a Rendezvous, which maintains a larger advertisement cache (four times the default bound in the reference implementation) and answers discovery queries for peers with constrained resources or short uptimes (edge sensors, mobile robots), or as a Relay, which forwards traffic for NAT-restricted peers. A relay forwards a `RELAY_FRAME` only to a member of the group the frame names, as the relay's own view holds it, and MAY refuse any forward by local policy (v0.1.20); the relay drops and counts a frame it refuses and answers nothing, so a member of one group cannot reach, through the relay, a peer the relay knows from another. Peers advertise these roles (RendezvousAdvertisement and so on) under leases. *Relay paths (v0.1.23, normative; SRAD large-networks-bulk-data §3.7).* A `RELAY_FRAME` without a `path` travels one hop: the relay delivers it to the target over a link it holds or opens and never forwards it to another relay. A `RELAY_FRAME` may carry `path`, the RELAY-role members it traverses in order (at most `max-path-hops`, default 4, no member repeated, the target absent), and `hop`, the index of the relay the outer envelope is addressed to. A relay receiving one refuses it unless `path[hop]` is itself and, for `hop > 0`, the envelope's `from` is `path[hop − 1]`; it delivers when the target is on a link it holds or can open; otherwise it forwards the frame once to `path[hop + 1]` with `hop + 1`, when that member is a RELAY-role member of the group it holds a link to, applying its own policy; otherwise it drops the frame. The origin chooses the path from the view it holds: a dial-only member advertises the relays it holds links to under the resource hint `relay.via`, and a relay advertises the relays it holds links to under `relay.links`, both comma-joined PeerIDs; the origin takes the shortest path from the relays it holds links to (or, when none reaches, the relays it could dial) to the relays the target declares (or any relay when the target listens), breaks ties on the lowest SHA-256 of the concatenated PeerIDs, and SHOULD reuse the path for later frames to the same target until a hop leaves the view, a signal names it, or `path-ttl` (default ten minutes) passes without traffic. A relay SHOULD dial every other RELAY-role member of its groups. A relay that drops a path frame MAY tell the origin: it sends, through its own ordinary send path, a `PIPE_DATA` of capability `aspace:cap/relay-signal` (§8) addressed to the origin, at most once per ten seconds per origin and target; the origin consumes the signal before any capability sees it and forgets the paths through the failed hop. The inner frame stays the origin's signed frame verbatim at every hop. A group with no rendezvous still functions on pure gossip and degrades only in discovery latency. JXTA gave rendezvous peers loosely consistent authoritative state; AgentSpaces rendezvous peers serve purely as caches, so losing one never affects correctness.

**5.5 Transport SPI.** The transport interface is a standards-based interface, `Transport { dial(PeerAddr), listen(), capabilities() }`. The reference implementation ships three bindings: an in-JVM loopback (powering tests and single-process fleets), TCP (length-prefixed frames over a socket), and QUIC (RFC 9000, added in v0.1.4 as `agentspaces-transport-quic`): the same length-prefixed frames over one bidirectional QUIC stream per peer connection, giving transport-level encryption always on, loss recovery, and connection migration, with everything above the transport unchanged. The QUIC handshake uses, by default, an ephemeral, per-process, unverified certificate: peer authentication lives in the signed-envelope layer against self-certifying PeerIDs (§11), identically over every transport, and QUIC's TLS supplies confidentiality alone. Constructed with the peer identity, both the QUIC binding and the TLS binding of TCP (`tls` scheme, TLS 1.3 per RFC 8446, shipped in v0.1.6) instead present the channel certificate of §5.6 and attest the remote end, which enables the per-frame signature elision that section specifies. Both bindings honour the same attestation rules (v0.1.13): both offer the enterprise-CA mode of §5.6 with a refreshable trust source, both attest a remote end from the full chain its handshake presented, and neither uses session resumption or 0-RTT early data, so every attestation rests on a full handshake that proved possession of the presented key. Bridges to Zenoh/DDS (robot fleets) or shared filesystems (air-gapped sync) plug in through the same SPI; they are planned and have not shipped.

*The HTTP and WebRTC bindings (v0.1.21, normative; SRAD agentspaces-js).* Five further bindings ship. `ws` (RFC 6455) carries one frame per binary message under the subprotocol `aspace.1`, with the 8 MiB frame cap enforced by close code 1009 and a text message refused with 1003; `wss` is the same inside TLS 1.3 with the `tls` binding's identity modes, and it admits a dialer that presents no certificate (a browser) as unattested. `http` and `https` carry batched frames through an HTTP exchange for intermediaries that break WebSocket: `POST /open` answers `201` with a session location, `POST /s/<sid>/up` carries a batch of length-prefixed frames under `Aspace-Seq`, `GET /s/<sid>/down` long-polls the server's batch under `Aspace-Ack`, and `DELETE /s/<sid>` ends the session; a session is one connection, unattested. A listener on any of the four refuses a request carrying an `Origin` header not on its allowlist (the default allowlist is empty). `ws` and `http` are refused by a node that requires attestation; on an unattested link of any of the four, two nodes in `SESSION` mode run the session attestation of §5.6 in band. `webrtc` carries frames over one ordered, reliable WebRTC data channel labelled `aspace/1` (negotiated id 0), chunked as annex B states; signalling travels as `PIPE_DATA` frames of the capability `aspace:cap/rtc-signal` (§8), directly when the target is connected and inside a `RELAY_FRAME` through a RELAY-role member otherwise; each side generates an ECDSA P-256 certificate per connection, places its SHA-256 fingerprint in the signed signal's `fp` list, and refuses a remote description whose fingerprints differ, so the channel attests the remote PeerID by the fingerprint its signed signal carried (`ENCRYPTED, ATTESTING, RELIABLE, ORDERED`). A peer with a `webrtc` acceptor advertises the endpoint `(webrtc, "sig", p)`: the address names no host, because the member is reached by signalling. No ICE server is configured by default. The label `aspace-dgram/1` is reserved for the `DATAGRAM` capability's unordered channel. A peer that cannot dial a scheme skips its endpoints. Peer reachability information travels inside PeerAdvertisements as a list of `(transport, addr, priority)` endpoints, so a peer advertises `quic` and `tcp` endpoints side by side. Dialers try endpoints in ascending `priority`, keep the advertised order among equal priorities, and skip endpoints whose scheme no configured transport serves; a listener declares the priority of each endpoint it advertises when it binds.

*Transport capabilities (v0.1.19, normative).* `capabilities()` returns a set drawn from `ENCRYPTED`, `ATTESTING`, `RELIABLE`, `ORDERED`, and `DATAGRAM`. Every binding is at least `RELIABLE` and `ORDERED`; `tls` and `dtls` add `ENCRYPTED` and `ATTESTING`; `quic` adds `ENCRYPTED`, and `ATTESTING` when it presents a channel certificate. `ATTESTING` is a capability, not a promise: a remote end presenting no certificate connects unattested. `DATAGRAM` is reserved for a datagram mode in which loss-tolerant kinds travel without recovery; no binding reports it yet. A node that requires attestation (§5.6) MUST refuse to register a binding without `ATTESTING`.

*The DTLS binding (v0.1.19, normative).* The `dtls` binding carries frames over DTLS 1.2 (RFC 6347) on UDP, addressed `host:port`. Both ends restrict themselves to the AEAD ECDHE suites (`TLS_ECDHE_ECDSA_WITH_AES_128_GCM_SHA256`, `..._AES_256_GCM_SHA384`, `..._CHACHA20_POLY1305_SHA256`, and their `ECDHE_RSA` counterparts), require the extended master secret (RFC 7627), refuse renegotiation and the Heartbeat extension (RFC 6520), and resume no session, so every attestation rests on a full handshake as for `tls` and `quic`. A listener MUST complete the stateless cookie exchange of RFC 6347 §4.2.1 before it holds any state for a source, and MUST send nothing larger than the datagram it answers to a source that has not completed it. A listener SHOULD issue an 8-byte Connection ID (RFC 9146) to every dialer that offers the extension; a dialer offers a zero-length id. A listener moves an association to a new peer address only on a record that carries its Connection ID, authenticates, and is the newest it has accepted, and until data sent to the new address is acknowledged it sends there at most three times the bytes received from it. DTLS 1.3 (RFC 9147) is not yet negotiated; an implementation that offers it MUST also offer DTLS 1.2 and MUST disable early data.

*The recovery sublayer (v0.1.19, normative).* DTLS delivers datagrams; the SPI promises a reliable, ordered frame channel, so the `dtls` binding runs a recovery sublayer on the plaintext of its application-data records, one PDU per record. All integers are big-endian and unsigned. Every PDU begins with the sublayer version `0x01` and a type byte; a receiver drops a PDU of unknown version and closes the association with `CLOSE(1)` on an unknown type. The types are `DATA` (`0x01`): flags (bit `0x01` an ACK block follows the 16-byte header), a lane byte that MUST be zero, a 32-bit packet number `pn`, a 32-bit frame sequence `fs`, a 16-bit fragment `index`, a 16-bit fragment `count`, the optional ACK block, and the fragment's bytes; `ACK` (`0x02`): two reserved bytes and an ACK block; `PING` (`0x03`) and `PONG` (`0x04`): two reserved bytes; `CLOSE` (`0x05`): a reason code (0 normal, 1 protocol error, 2 receive bound, 3 idle) and a reserved byte. Type `0x06` is reserved for datagram mode, `0x07` and `0x08` are the bare-UDP hello pair below, and `0x09` to `0x0F` are reserved. An ACK block is `largest_acked` (32 bits), `ack_delay_us` (32 bits), `n` additional ranges (16 bits, at most 32), `first_range_len` (16 bits), then `n` pairs of `gap` and `range_len` (16 bits each) with the meaning of RFC 9000 §19.3.1. A sender numbers every transmission with a fresh `pn` and every frame with the next `fs`; it splits a frame into fragments of which all but the last have one length, retransmits a lost fragment in a new `DATA` with a new `pn` and the same `(fs, index)`, and never sends the frame's length. A packet is lost when a packet three or more numbers newer is acknowledged, or when it was sent more than 9/8 of the larger of the smoothed and latest round-trip times before the newest acknowledgement (RFC 9002 §6.1); the retransmission timer follows RFC 6298, clamped to 200 ms to 10 s, and doubles on each expiry without acknowledgement. A receiver acknowledges on the second unacknowledged `DATA` or any out-of-order arrival and otherwise within 25 ms, delivers frames strictly in `fs` order, and holds at most 64 frames ahead of the next to deliver and 16 MiB of fragment bytes, closing with `CLOSE(2)` beyond either; a sender never starts a frame that would make an honest receiver exceed them. Either end sends `PING` after a quarter of the idle timeout with nothing sent and answers `PING` with `PONG`; an association silent for the idle timeout (60 s by default) fails. The sublayer carries no security function: DTLS authenticates and replay-protects every record. The golden vectors `dtls_pdu_*` fix the encoding. A bare `udp` binding MAY run the same sublayer over plaintext UDP for closed networks: the dialer sends `HELLO` (`0x07`, a 64-bit nonce, padded to 28 bytes), the listener answers `HELLO_ACK` (`0x08`, the nonce and a 64-bit token keyed to the dialer's address, 20 bytes) and holds no state until a PDU carries a valid token, and every PDU carries the token after its version and type bytes. It reports neither `ENCRYPTED` nor `ATTESTING`, so every frame on it stays signed.

**5.6 Channel authentication (v0.1.6, normative).** The envelope signature of §9 authenticates one hop. When the transport itself authenticates the channel, that per-frame signature repeats work the handshake already did, at roughly a thousand times the cost of the frame's serialization, so v0.1.6 lets an authenticated channel stand in for it. The mechanism has four parts.

*Channel certificates.* A peer generates a fresh EC P-256 key for TLS and wraps it in a self-signed X.509 certificate whose X.509 signature is the peer's Ed25519 identity signature over the TBSCertificate (signature algorithm id-Ed25519, 1.3.101.112, RFC 8410), with the raw 32-byte identity key published in the subject CN (multibase base58btc). The TLS handshake (RFC 8446) proves possession of the P-256 key; the endorsement proves the identity stands behind exactly that key; together they bind the channel to `PeerID = multibase(sha-256(identity key))`. No CA chain participates: the verifier's only trust decision is this PeerID pin, the raw-public-key trust model (RFC 7250) with a certificate as carrier. The identity's private key signs once, at certificate generation, and never enters the TLS machinery.

*Attestation.* A transport that verified the remote end's channel certificate reports the bound PeerID from its connection (`attestedPeer()` in the SPI). Verification is: signature algorithm is id-Ed25519, the CN decodes to a 32-byte key, and that key's Ed25519 signature over the TBSCertificate verifies. A remote end presenting no certificate, or one failing any check, still connects; its channel is merely unattested and its frames stay fully signed. The `tls`, `quic`, and `dtls` (v0.1.19) bindings attest mutually (each side presents its certificate; the server requests but does not require the client's).

*Frame relaxation.* The §9 envelope wrapper's key and signature fields become optional. A frame carrying both is a signed frame, acceptable from any connection, exactly as before. A frame carrying neither is a channel-attested frame: a receiver accepts it only when the connection's attested PeerID equals the envelope's `from`; every other receiver drops it. A frame carrying a key without a signature is always dropped; a frame carrying a 32-byte signature field without a key is a session frame (v0.1.17, below), dropped by every receiver that holds no session for it. Only self-originated envelopes may travel bare: the inner frame of a RELAY_FRAME remains the origin's fully signed frame verbatim, because the relay's channel attests the relay alone.

*Negotiation.* A node willing to receive channel-attested frames announces it with a signed `CHANNEL_HELLO` envelope on the attested connection, whose body carries `acceptsBare` and echoes the PeerID the sender attested for the remote end, binding the announcement to that very channel; the receiver honors it only when the echo names itself and the connection's attestation names the announcer. The announcement governs only what a sender elides. A receiver accepts a bare frame whenever the connection attests the frame's sender, whatever mode the receiver itself announced, because the acceptance rule of the previous paragraph is already sufficient: bare frames are accepted from the attested peer alone. A sender elides signatures only toward peers that announced, so fleets mix v0.1.5 and v0.1.6 nodes, and SIGNED-mode and ATTESTED-mode nodes, freely. Forcing signed mode is the only downgrade an attacker can cause, and signed mode gives up nothing but speed.

*Session attestation (v0.1.17).* A connection the transport cannot attest (plain TCP) MAY be attested by a signed handshake instead, so that one signature per connection, rather than one per frame, stands behind every frame on it. A node in the `SESSION` mode advertises the hint `wire.session=1` and, toward a member whose advertisement carries the same hint, opens a session once a non-introduction frame from that member has arrived (which proves the member admitted it), on the connection it sends over: a fully signed `SESSION_HELLO` carrying a fresh X25519 public key and a 32-byte nonce, naming the remote PeerID, and with no echo. The remote end, if it is in the `SESSION` mode and the connection is unattested, answers with its own signed `SESSION_HELLO` echoing the opener's nonce. Both ends derive `key = HKDF-SHA-256(X25519(ephemeral, peer ephemeral), salt = empty, info = "agentspaces session v1" || lower PeerID || 0 || higher PeerID || 0 || lower's nonce || higher's nonce, 32)`. A session frame is the envelope with no key and, in the signature field, `HMAC-SHA-256(key, canonical envelope)`; a receiver accepts it only on the connection whose session names the envelope's `from` and whose key verifies the tag, and only an envelope the node itself originated travels this way, so a relay's inner frame stays signed. The opener sends session frames as soon as the answer arrives; the answerer keeps signing until the first session frame from the opener verifies (key confirmation). A hello is accepted only fully signed, never bare or under a session, and only while younger than thirty seconds by the recipient's clock (older, it is ignored and counted as stale, never a strike: a live hello is seconds old, its stamp merged toward what the opener last heard, while a captured one replayed on a fresh link after the replay cache has turned over is the one frame that could otherwise create state at a recipient); one session stands per connection, with one peer, and ends with the connection; when both ends open at once the lower PeerID stays the opener; an unanswered opening may be repeated after five seconds. The stamp-based replay rule of §9 applies to session frames unchanged, and a session frame cannot be moved to another connection because its key is that connection's. The `dev-local` profile selects this mode; the TLS profiles attest through the handshake already, and zero trust signs every hop by choice.

*Required attestation (v0.1.10, normative for the enterprise-CA mode).* A node MAY require attestation. In that mode every inbound frame, admission frames included, MUST arrive on a connection whose attested PeerID equals the envelope's `from`; the node logs and drops a frame on an unattested connection or on a connection attested for someone else, and it drops the inner frames of `RELAY_FRAME`, because a relay's channel attests the relay alone, with one exception (v0.1.21): a fully signed `PIPE_DATA` of capability `aspace:cap/rtc-signal` addressed to the receiving node itself is admitted to that capability alone, because a browser member can reach a node it cannot dial only through a relay, and the data channel the signal opens attests the browser by the fingerprint its signed signal carried (§5.5, the `webrtc` binding). The check runs after replay deduplication and never strikes the sender. A bootstrap introduction needs an attested channel, and a peer advertisement forwarded by a third party refreshes existing members and admits nobody. When a signed frame from a current member arrives on a connection that attests nothing, the connection has just completed a fresh handshake that attests nothing, which is what a CA revocation, expiry, or credential replacement looks like at this layer: the node evicts the member from its membership view, closes the cached connection, and refuses the member at dispatch until one of its frames arrives on an attested connection again. A frame attested for someone else is dropped and never evicts, so an attested party cannot evict a member by replaying its signed frames. This is how the CA's revocation becomes the authoritative eject that v0.1.9 promised; the `tls`, `quic`, and `dtls` bindings accept a refreshable trust source so a deployment can load new CRLs without restarting the peer. A node in this mode registers attesting transports alone: a plaintext channel attests nobody, so dialing a peer's plaintext endpoint would arrive unattested at the far end and have this node evicted there. The starter enforces that rule from one property, `agentspaces.transport.tls.require-attestation`, which selects the mode, drops the plaintext binding, and refuses to start when TLS is off.

What this changes and what it leaves alone: the elided signature is hop authenticity only. The evidence layer, record signatures (§7.1), authenticated state transitions (§11a.4), signed claims, signed advertisements, and join credentials, is untouched, travels inside frame bodies, and verifies end to end regardless of how the enclosing frame was authenticated.

## 6. Layer 2: Advertisements and Discovery

**6.1 The advertisement model.** An Advertisement is a signed, typed, TTL-bearing document, shown here in its JSON (RFC 8259) rendering; on the wire it travels as canonical CBOR inside the signed wrapper of §9:

```json
{
  "type": "aspace:CapabilityAdvertisement",
  "id": "aspace://grp-7f3a…/cap/task-auction/peer-09c1…",
  "issuer": "peer:09c1…",
  "group": "grp-7f3a…",
  "issued": "2026-08-26T14:02:11Z",
  "ttl": "PT15M",
  "body": { …type-specific… },
  "sig": "ed25519:…"
}
```

The core defines these normative advertisement types: PeerAdvertisement (endpoints, roles, resource hints); GroupAdvertisement (§5.1); SpaceAdvertisement (space name, entry schema hints that describe the shape of data in the space, the conflict strategy, the admission rule, `GROUP` or `ALLOWLIST`, and the replication mode, `FULL` or `TAG_SHARDED`; §7.5); CapabilityAdvertisement (§8); AgentCard, a capability card describing an agent through a human- and LLM-readable description, the goals it can pursue, the input and output entry types it consumes and produces, cost hints, and space bindings, a map from each consumed entry type to the space the agent takes it from, which lets a caller route an input entry without configuring routes by hand (§10.6); and AssetCard (§6.1a, added in v0.1.1), the AgentCard's sibling for peers that provide data and pursue no goals. ModelCard (§6.1a.1, added in v0.1.24) is the AssetCard's sibling for a model a group may route to: its tasks, limits, licence, provenance, evaluations, cost hints, residency, engine, and the peers that serve it. An AgentCard carries no network endpoints: agents are reached by writing entries, and the A2A gateway (§12) supplies HTTP endpoints where foreign clients need them. The AgentCard stays deliberately close in spirit to A2A Agent Cards so a gateway can translate mechanically; within AgentSpaces, the Embabel extension generates AgentCards from Embabel metadata (§10.4), and the remote-actions bridge consumes them as planner actions (§10.6). The admission and replication fields of the SpaceAdvertisement and the space bindings of the AgentCard are additive record fields appended in v0.1.10; a decoder that lacks them ignores them, and a card without bindings routes as before. The AgentCard's `agentPublicKey` (v0.1.11, QA4 A4-7 phase 3) is appended likewise and omitted when absent: present, it is the raw Ed25519 key the issuing peer certified for the card's agent (§4.2), so a reader can tell an attested agent from an asserted name; absent, the card is byte-identical to the v0.1.10 card. Its `agentCertificate` (v0.1.13), appended after `actions` and omitted when absent, carries the peer's certificate for that key, so the attestation is provable: a cache MUST refuse a card whose certificate does not certify exactly its agent and `agentPublicKey`, verify under the card issuer's key, and cover the card's `issued`. The AgentCard's `actions` (v0.1.13) is appended after `agentPublicKey` and omitted when absent: each `CardAction(name, description, consumes, produces, space, kind)` declares one bound method — its name (the method name, unique within the card), what it does, the schemas it consumes and produces (empty for a void action), the space it takes or watches its input in, and how it is bound (`take`, `notify`, `ordered-take`, `ballot`, `on-decision`, or `embabel-action`). A card declares at most 64 actions, each description at most 512 characters, and the binder sorts them by name so a card's bytes do not depend on reflection order. Readers that understand actions use the declared pairs instead of forming the cross product of the card's flat `consumes` and `produces` lists; a card without actions is byte-identical to the v0.1.12 card and reads as before.

The RevocationAdvertisement (v0.1.9) is signed and typed like the others and is deliberately excluded from the ad-cache: a revocation is a permanent verdict, so its TTL bounds re-gossip and never the withdrawal itself, and caching it beside leased advertisements would let it expire. Publishing a revocation through discovery routes it to the group's revocation registry, which verifies the signature and the signer's authority before accepting it; an accepted revocation then travels on the dedicated `revocations` gossip stream with anti-entropy so late joiners converge, and a refused revocation is never gossiped. Enforcement does not stop at the revoked peer's own frames (v0.1.13, ASF-047): a member never admits the revoked PeerID to its membership view from anyone's copy of its self-advertisement, and never forwards that advertisement; discovery purges and thereafter refuses the revoked peer's advertisements; and a space refuses any state or claim whose *actor* is revoked — the record's issuer for a write, renewal, or cancellation, the claim holder for a claim or a completion — for entries the replica does not already hold. Replicas that already hold the revoked peer's entries keep them (§7.5: revocation does not remove work already admitted), an honest holder's completion of such an entry still lands, and a refusal is acknowledged in the space digest as an `a:<line>` entry so anti-entropy partners stop re-offering it. A late joiner therefore never adopts a revoked peer's history; that is the intended cost of revocation.

*One record per revoked peer (v0.1.13, normative).* A registry keeps exactly one record per revoked PeerID, chosen by a total order every member computes alike, so registries converge: the issuer's authority first (the founder, then a revocation rooted in the CA with evidence, then any other issuer a deployment's validator admits), then the newer `issued`, then the larger SHA-256 of the signed bytes. A lower-ranked statement never replaces a higher-ranked one whatever its stamp, so a founder's rotation cannot be erased by a later record from anyone else, and only the founder may name a `successor`. Acceptance side effects fire once per peer, on its first record.

*CA-rooted peer revocation (v0.1.13, normative for the enterprise-CA mode).* A RevocationAdvertisement may append `evidence`, the CBOR `RevocationEvidence(chain, crl?)`: the revoked peer's channel chain as DER, leaf first, at most four certificates, and optionally the CRL revoking its leaf as DER, at most 64 KiB, with at most 96 KiB in all; absent evidence is omitted, so earlier revocations keep their bytes. Under the `founder-or-ca` validator a member accepts a revocation from any issuer when its evidence proves, judged at the revocation's own `issued` instant so every member reaches the same verdict, that the chain validates to the member's CA anchors, that the leaf's CN binds the revoked PeerID, and that a CRL signed by the leaf's issuer (the evidence's, or one the member holds) lists the leaf for a reason that withdraws the identity: `keyCompromise`, `cACompromise`, `privilegeWithdrawn`, `cessationOfOperation`, or `unspecified`. A `superseded`, `affiliationChanged`, or `certificateHold` entry, an unverifiable or stale CRL, and a revocation naming a successor never authorize one; those remain connection-level, where attestation already refuses the leaf. No network is consulted on this path. A node in that mode judges every presented chain when a connection attaches and again whenever its trust changes (a CRL refresh); a leaf revoked for an authorizing reason makes it cut the link and, at most once per peer, root the revocation itself with the chain and CRL as evidence. The founder-rooted default is unchanged and ignores evidence.

*Revoking what peers certify (v0.1.13, normative).* A `CredentialRevocation` advertisement revokes something certified other than a peer identity: an `AGENT` (every key of an AgentID), an `AGENT_KEY` (one agent key, by the SHA-256 of its raw public key), an `X509_LEAF` (one channel certificate, by the SHA-256 of its DER), or a `JOIN_CREDENTIAL` (by the SHA-256 of its bytes). Its fields are `id = aspace://<group>/revocation/<kind>/<canonical target>`, `issuer`, `group`, `issued`, `ttl`, `target`, `reason` (one of `key-compromise`, `retired`, `superseded`, `privilege-withdrawn`, `unspecified`), an optional `effectiveFrom` no later than `issued`, and optional `evidence`. The founder may revoke any target, and an agent's own peer may revoke that agent and its keys; nobody else, unless a deployment's validator ranks them. It travels like a peer revocation, routed by discovery to the group's credential-revocation registry and gossiped on the `credential-revocations` stream with anti-entropy; old nodes ignore the stream. The registry keeps one record per target under the same total order (founder, trust root, the target's own peer, other), and holds it for the life of the process. Enforcement follows a freeze rule. What a replica already holds stays. A newly arriving signature by a revoked agent or key is refused when the reason is `key-compromise`, whatever its claimed signing time, because a stolen key can back-date; under any other reason it is refused only when signed at or after `effectiveFrom` (else `issued`), so a late joiner still receives the target's history. Records are judged at their issue stamp and agent-signed states at their `signedAt`; claims are refused under any revocation, because a renewal keeps the claim's original stamp, so a revoked holder's take lapses and the entry returns to the space, and its completion is refused. Discovery refuses and purges a revoked agent's cards; key-wrap holders serve it no key (§11a.2a); a vote tally excludes the ballots of a revoked agent (or of an agent whose peer is revoked) at every recount, so a decision may reopen (§8), while an `AGENT_KEY` revocation does not reach ballots already merged, since a tally does not see which key signed a ballot, and stops the key's new ballots where the space refuses them at merge; each group's authorizer refuses it (§11); a node refuses to write or take as it locally and unbinds it. A revoked join credential admits nobody, and a member it admitted is evicted. A revoked X.509 leaf cuts every link presenting it, now and on every later handshake.

*Revoking a delegation (v0.1.18 addendum, normative).* A `JOIN_CREDENTIAL` revocation whose hash names a delegation's bytes rather than a credential's refuses every delegated credential issued under that delegation and evicts the members admitted under it; a member records both hashes at admission so the revocation finds them. Only the founder's revocation verifies, as for any join credential.

**6.1a The AssetCard (v0.1.1 addendum).** A peer needs no agents to participate in a group (P4, P6), and the AssetCard is the advertisement type that makes such peers useful: a connector process joins a group on behalf of a warehouse, database, search cluster, message broker, or web service and publishes one signed, TTL-bearing AssetCard per asset it holds. An AssetCard carries the asset name and source URI, a human- and LLM-readable description, the declared shape of data (an entry schema name, table structure, index mapping, or message format), the freshness or update cadence, size and cost hints, and an access binding naming the mode and the capability that serves queries. Agents discover assets exactly as they discover agents: structurally through the ad-cache (`find(AssetCard.class, predicate)`) or by meaning through the semantic-discovery capability, and the connector normalizes incongruent data from its source into typed entries whose shape the card declares.

Connectors serve data in two styles, and most implement both. A catalog provider answers on demand through a data-query capability (`aspace:cap/data-query`, defined outside the core per P6): agents write typed query entries into an agreed space and the connector writes result entries back, inline when they fit the 64 KiB inline limit and content-addressed through the group's block exchange when they are bulk, so retries, observability, and service choreography across connectors come free with the space. The connector runtime builds its data space with a block exchange attached, and the query client reads bulk results through the same exchange, so a large catalog result travels as a block and the space replicates only its record. A materializing provider pushes changes into the space as its source changes: the provider implements `materialize(sink)` and hands the runtime one `Change(asset, key, row)` per changed row, and the runtime writes each as an `AssetChange(asset, key, row, servedBy)` entry leased for the asset's declared freshness, so the lease is the retention policy and a row the source stops refreshing ages out. Subscribing agents register `notify(Template.of(AssetChange.class), …)` and receive complex event processing over enterprise events. In both styles the lease is the liveness contract (P2): a connector that goes down stops refreshing, and its assets age out of every cache with no failure-detection machinery.

*Asset operations (v0.1.21, normative; SRAD agentspaces-js annex F).* An AssetCard's `access` map is open: beside `mode`, `query`, and `invoke` it MAY carry `read-ops` and `invoke-ops`, comma-separated operation names, and further keys a connector documents. The capability `aspace:cap/asset-invoke` (§8) adds the operation verb: an agent writes an `AssetInvocation(v, asset, operation, params, invocationId)` entry into the connector's space, exactly one connector takes it under the space's claim lattice with a lease, runs the operation, writes an `AssetInvocationResult(v, invocationId, asset, operation, ok, result, error)`, and completes the take. `invocationId` is the client's and stays constant across retries and takers; a connector that remembers results answers a repeat from memory and runs nothing twice. An operation absent from the card's `invoke-ops` is answered with an error naming the rule and never run. Under an authorizer, serving an asset needs the scope `aspace:connector-serve:<asset>` and running an operation the scope `aspace:connector-invoke:<asset>:<operation>`, judged on the requesting entry's signer. Both entries carry `v` and readers ignore unknown fields. A connector cell on a Workers runtime (celld, workerd, Cloudflare) publishes its Durable Objects, KV, D1, R2, queues, workflows, and routes as assets under the URI convention `workers://<application>/<kind>/<name>`, with ids stable across restarts; it is a non-agent member and publishes no AgentCard.

**6.1a.1 The ModelCard (v0.1.24 addendum; ISSUE-ModelCard).** A model is advertised as a `ModelCard`, a signed and leased advertisement beside the AssetCard: `id`, `issuer`, `group`, `issued`, `ttl`, `name` (the name a `ModelRequest` addresses), `version`, `tasks` (`chat`, `embed`, `rerank`, `classify`, `transcribe`, `vision`), `modality`, `limits` (`context`, `output`), `licence`, `provenance` (`upstream`, `weightRoot` on the content swarm, `weightHash`, `admittedBy`), `evals` (at most 64 entries), `costHints`, `residency` (`sites`, `classifications`, `releasable`), `engine` (`provider`, `ollama`, `vllm`, `onnx`; `tgi`, `nim`, `triton` reserved), and `servers`, the peers that hold it. The maps are open. The card asserts what its issuer signs; where a model may run and who may admit it are enforced by the party that reads the card (a platform's registry and gateway). The cache treats the card as any card, under the byte budget of §6.3 (v0.1.32): stored, refreshed by a newer `issued`, expired by `ttl`; `discovery.find(ModelCard.class, predicate)` filters it by name, task, engine, and residency site. The golden vector `model_card_cbor` pins the canonical bytes. *Compatibility.* A reader of an earlier revision meets the kind name `ModelCard` in a stored advertisement, finds no type for it, and refuses the advertisement without reading it (the same path that refuses a mistyped card); the TypeScript, Python, and Ruby clients keep every advertisement as data keyed by its kind name and index a ModelCard's name, tasks, and engine for semantic discovery.

**6.1a.2 The AppCard (v0.1.25 addendum; ISSUE-AppCard; v0.1.32, ISSUE-AppCardOriginFormats).** An `AppCard`, the tenth advertisement kind, beside the AgentCard, the AssetCard, and the ModelCard, advertises an application that joins a fleet: `id`, `issuer`, `group`, `issued`, `ttl`, `name`, `kind`, `shape` (`web`, `desktop`, `static`, `a2ui`), `owner`, `reads` (the `group/space` pairs it reads), `actions` (the action names it may request), `idpClient` (its identity-provider client), `url`, `package`, `version`, `views` (the view names it asks of its feed), and then, each appended in this order and omitted when absent, `ui`, `host` (§6.1a.4), `origin`, `formats`, and `spill` (§6.1a.5). `ui` holds `protocol` (`a2ui`), `version` (`v0.9.1`), `catalogIds`, `agent` (the surface agent's instance), `transport` (`views` through the feed, `a2a` through the gateway), `surfaces` (the surface ids), and the `components` of §6.1a.3. An app with `shape: a2ui` draws itself as A2UI surfaces its surface agent writes; an app of another shape may carry `ui` too and embed surfaces in its own pages.

*The kind (v0.1.32, AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09).* `kind` says how the app connects to the fleet, and its set is closed: `peer`, the app is itself a member of the group; `fed`, an agent of the group feeds the app its entries and views; `gateway`, the app sits behind the A2A gateway (§12); `assembled`, a static app its publisher assembled from extracted data and serves as is, which reads nothing live from the group or from anywhere else, as a published static HTML site does. The default is `fed`. v0.1.32 removes `realm` from the set and gives `assembled` the meaning above. v0.1.26 to v0.1.31 used `assembled` for an app a console drafted from its group's ui declarations (§6.1a.3), and v0.1.32 writes such an app as `fed` with an origin that names the console (`REGISTRY.md`, system `console`). A writer SHOULD keep the card consistent with its kind, and no reader enforces this advice: a `gateway` app that carries `ui` writes `ui.transport` `a2a`; a `fed` app that carries `ui` writes `ui.transport` `views`; an `assembled` app writes `reads`, `views`, and `actions` empty, carries no `ui`, and takes shape `static`.
