*Origin and formats (v0.1.32, AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09).* `origin` names the external system the app came from, `{system, ref, version, attrs}`. `system` holds 1 to 64 UTF-16 code units of `[a-z0-9]`, `.`, and `-`, and starts and ends with a letter or digit; a registered system holds no `.`, and an unregistered one is a reverse-DNS name its owner controls, with at least one `.`. `ref` is the app's identifier in that system: 1 to 256 code units with no control character (U+0000 to U+001F, U+007F). `version` holds 0 to 64 code units with no control character, and is `""` when the system names no version. `attrs`, a `map<String,String>` omitted when null, holds 1 to 8 entries; each key is the value of `system`, then `.`, then one or more of `[a-z0-9]`, `.`, and `-`, at most 128 code units in all, and each value holds 0 to 256 code units with no control character. `formats`, a `list<String>`, holds 1 to 16 distinct entries of 1 to 256 code units each, in the issuer's order. An entry without `:` is a media type: lower-case `type/subtype` under the restricted names of RFC 6838 §4.2, with no parameter and no `;`. An entry with `:` is the IRI (RFC 3987) of a vocabulary (a JSON-LD context, a profile, or an ontology namespace); at decode it holds a scheme `[a-z][a-z0-9+.-]*` before its first `:` and no whitespace or control character, and full IRI validation stays advice to writers. A media type with a profile becomes two entries, the media type and the profile's IRI. Lengths count UTF-16 code units, as S6 does. `origin.system`, the `attrs` keys, and the `formats` entries are open values, and `agentspaces-spec/REGISTRY.md` lists the registered ones. A reader MUST NOT refuse a card for a system, key, or format the registry lacks when the value meets its grammar. No origin system fixes a kind, so a reader neither refuses nor reinterprets a card for its pair of kind and origin, and an importer chooses the kind of what it imports.

*Refusals (v0.1.32).* A reader MUST treat as undecodable (§6.1b) a card whose `kind`, `shape`, `ui.transport`, `ui.components[].role`, `host.signOn`, or `host.status` lies outside its closed set; whose `origin` breaks a bound or a grammar above, or carries `attrs` as an empty map; or whose `formats` is an empty list, repeats an entry, holds an upper-case media type or a media type with a parameter, or breaks a bound.

*The AppCard level (v0.1.32, AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09).* A member advertises the hint `wire.appcard=2` (§9) once every decoder it runs reads the v0.1.32 schemas of the `AppCard` and the `AgentCard` and their value rules. The hint's text is canonical decimal, read as level N or above. A member without the hint, with any other text, or whose peer advertisement the issuer has not yet received, counts as level 1. An issuer writes `origin`, `formats`, a `host` whose `signOn` is `idp`, and `spill` (§6.1a.5) only while every member of its current view of the card's group is at level 2; otherwise it issues the card without them. A dial-only client that cannot see every member's hints writes none of them. When a member below level 2 joins, the issuer reissues each of its cards in that group at once without these fields, under a strictly newer `issued`. When no member below level 2 remains (one gossip period after the last one leaves, or as soon as its peer advertisement shows level 2), the issuer reissues the full card at once. Each implementation counts the cards it issued without a gated field because of the gate (`appCardsDowngraded`).

*Reading the card.* The card asserts what its issuer signs. `origin` carries no consent from the system it names, and `formats` grants nothing. The party that admits the card (a console and its edge) enforces what the app may read and request. The cache treats the card as any card, under the byte budget of §6.3. `discovery.find(AppCard.class, predicate)` filters it by name, kind, shape, and owner, and by three predicates: `fromOrigin(system)`; `fromOrigin(system, ref)`, where a `ref` ending in `/*` matches every `ref` that starts with the part before `*`; and `handles(format)`, which lower-cases a media type argument and matches an entry exactly, with `type/*` matching every media type of that type. `remoteFind` accepts the `fieldEquals` keys `origin.system`, `origin.ref`, and `formats` under the same rules, and an answerer that does not know a key matches no card for it. Neither `origin` nor `formats` ever spills, so the predicates read a stub (§6.1a.5) as they read a full card. The golden vectors `app_card_host_cbor` and `app_card_origin_formats_cbor` pin the canonical bytes, and eleven `adv_app_card_*` vectors pin the refusals (annex I). *Compatibility.* As for the ModelCard: a reader of a revision before v0.1.25 finds no type for the kind name and refuses the advertisement without reading it. A v0.1.31 reader refuses a card that carries `origin`, `formats`, or `spill` (P1, §9.1), and the AppCard level keeps such a card out of any view that holds one. A card without `host`, `origin`, `formats`, and `spill` keeps its v0.1.31 bytes. The four clients keep the card as data and index its name, kind, shape, origin system and ref, and formats for semantic discovery.

**6.1a.3 The UI declaration (v0.1.26 addendum; ISSUE-UiComponents).** An `AgentCard` MAY carry `ui`, appended after `agentCertificate` and omitted from the wire when absent, so a card without it is byte-identical to v0.1.25: `protocol` (`a2ui`), `version` (`v0.9.1`), `components` (each `id`, unique within the card; `catalogId`; `component`, the A2UI component name in that catalog; `entry`, the standard entry type IRI behind it or empty; `controls`, the action names a person may trigger, each an action of the card; `role`, one of `panel`, `control`, `row`), `entries` (`reads` and `writes`, the entry type IRIs the agent speaks through its components), and `catalogIds` (the distinct catalogs of the components, derived when empty). At most 32 components, 16 controls per component, 64 IRIs per list. An `AppCard.ui` MAY carry the same `components` list after `surfaces`, omitted when empty. The declaration says what an agent can show; it never carries a surface message, and nothing executable crosses in it. Binders derive it from an annotation on the agent (`@UiComponent` in Java; the clients' equivalents); a reader that renders A2UI but knows nothing of AgentSpaces reads `catalogId` and `component` as A2UI names. A catalog id is open data, and a reader never refuses a card for one; `agentspaces-a2ui` ships the open catalogs `agentspaces-viz` and `agentspaces-widgets` (v0.1.32). A console may draft an application for a group from its agents' declarations, and it publishes the result as a `fed` app whose origin names the console (§6.1a.2); the party that admits the application and the agent that answers the control enforce what a component may do. An `AgentCard` whose encoding passes the spill threshold moves its `actions` and `ui` into a content-swarm document (§6.1a.5), and it appends `spill` after `ui`.

**6.1a.4 The host (v0.1.28 addendum; ISSUE-AppHost; v0.1.32 sign-on values).** An `AppCard` MAY carry `host`, appended after `ui` and omitted from the wire when absent, so a card without it is byte-identical to v0.1.27: `{url, port, hostAgent, path, signOn, publishedBy, publishedAt, status}`. It says where a host serves an application for its end users: `url` is the address a person opens; `port` the port the host bound, or 0; `hostAgent` the peer id of the host serving it; `path` under the host, `/` by default; `signOn` is `idp` (the identity provider of whoever runs the host, enforced at the host's door; the default) or `none`; `publishedBy` the agent that published the interface; `status` is `serving` or `stopped`. v0.1.28 to v0.1.31 wrote `tenant` where v0.1.32 writes `idp`, and a v0.1.32 reader refuses `tenant` as undecodable (§6.1b). The publisher's request, `Publish.signOn` in `agentspaces-a2ui`, takes the same two values with the same default; `host.signOn` states what the app asks, so a host whose identity provider is not online still writes `idp`, and its door answers 503 with `Retry-After` until the provider is online (platform follow-ups, 2026-11-09). A host's door serves an app without a session only when the value is `none`, so any other value fails closed. An issuer writes a `host` whose `signOn` is `idp` only under the AppCard level of §6.1a.2, so a v0.1.31 reader never meets `idp`. An agent publishes its own interface; a host serves it; a console that reads the card lists the app and links to it. Golden vector `app_card_host_cbor` (`signOn` `idp`, neutral values).

**6.1a.5 The card spill (v0.1.32 addendum; ISSUE-AppCardOriginFormats; AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09).** No card kind has a size cap. A card whose canonical encoding (the `adBytes` its issuer signs, §9.1) is more than 65,536 bytes, the inline limit of a payload (§7.1), travels as a *stub*: the card with its large parts moved into a content-swarm document, which a reader fetches when it needs them. The rule covers the two kinds that carry `ui`. The *spill set* of an `AppCard` is `reads`, `actions`, `views`, and `ui`; the spill set of an `AgentCard` is `actions` and `ui`. Every other field stays on the stub, so discovery by name, kind, shape, owner, origin, format, agent, goals, and schemas reads the stub alone.

- *The parts document.* The issuer encodes the kind's parts record under §9.1, `AppCard.Parts {reads, actions, views, ui}` or `AgentCard.Parts {actions, ui}` (annex I), holding the full card's values of the spill set. It publishes the bytes as a content-swarm artifact (§7.1, annex G) cut into 65,536-byte pieces, with the manifest's `name` the card's `id`, `mediaType` `application/cbor`, and `sealing` `none`, since the card the document completes travels unsealed. The document carries no `issued`, so a refresh that changes no part reuses the artifact and its root.
- *The reference.* The stub carries `spill`, a `CardSpill {root, size, parts}` appended last and omitted when absent. `root` is the artifact's content root: the 59-character text `b` followed by the lower-case base32 (RFC 4648, no padding, zero pad bits) of a CIDv1 with codec raw (0x55) and a sha2-256 multihash. `size` is the document's length in bytes, from 1 to 2^53 - 1. `parts` names the spill-set fields whose full value is non-empty (neither null nor an empty list): one or more names, distinct, in the card's field order.
- *The stub.* Every spill-set field of a stub holds its placeholder: `[]` for the lists the card always writes (`AppCard.reads`, `actions`, `views`), and absence for the optional ones (`AppCard.ui`, `AgentCard.actions`, `AgentCard.ui`). A reader MUST treat as undecodable (§6.1b) a card with `spill` whose `root` is no such text, whose `size` lies outside its range, whose `parts` is empty, repeats a name, names a field outside the kind's spill set, or breaks the field order, or whose spill-set field holds anything but its placeholder.
- *The publisher.* A publisher MUST NOT spill a card whose encoding is at most 65,536 bytes. It spills a larger card while the AppCard level of §6.1a.2 holds for the whole view and a content swarm serves the group; otherwise it issues the full card inline and counts it in `cardsOversizeInline`, and it counts each spilled issue in `cardsSpilled`. It measures the card it would sign, after the AppCard level has removed what it removes. It logs a warning once per card id and encoded size when the encoding passes 57,344 bytes (56 KiB), so an operator sees a card approach the threshold before it spills. It keeps the artifact's manifest and holding live while it issues a card that names the root, and releases the root one `ttl` after the last such card.
- *The reader.* `discovery.resolve(card)` returns the card itself when it carries no `spill`. Otherwise it fetches the document from the artifact's holders and returns the full card: the stub with the document's values in the spill-set fields and no `spill`. It checks the fetched length against `size`, decodes the document under §9.1 as the kind's parts record, and checks that its non-empty fields are exactly those `parts` names. A document that fails a check fails the resolution and counts in `spillsRefused`; the cached stub stays, and the reader strikes nobody. A reader does not fetch a document whose `size` passes its fetch bound (16,777,216 bytes by default, configurable), and counts the case in `spillsOverLimit`. A reader without a content swarm keeps the stub. An answerer of `remoteFind` or of a semantic query matches the stub and never fetches a spill to answer.

The issuer's signature covers `spill.root`, and the root names the document's bytes, so the parts carry the issuer's word as the stub does. The golden vectors `app_card_spill_cbor` with `app_card_parts_cbor`, and `agent_card_spill_cbor` with `agent_card_parts_cbor`, pin two stubs and their documents, `card_spill_threshold_bytes` and `card_spill_warn_bytes` pin the two constants, and seven `adv_*_spill_*` vectors pin the refusals (annex I). *Compatibility.* `spill` travels only under the AppCard level, so a v0.1.31 reader never meets a stub; it meets the full card inline, as it does today, under its own cache's budget.

**6.1a.6 The content store (v0.1.32 addendum; srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md §3.2; ISSUE-ContentStore).** A *content store* is a member that holds roots for other members of its group under a lease and proves that it holds them. It advertises itself by an `AssetCard` with `asset` = `content-store:<name>`, `uri` = `aspace://<group>/store/<name>`, `shape` = `store`, `freshness` = the holding interval as an ISO-8601 duration, `costHints` carrying `pricePerGibMonth` only when its owner sets a price, and `access` with these keys: `kind` (`content-store`); `capacity`, `free`, and `maxObject` in bytes; `codecs` (`dag-cbor,raw`); `hashes` (`sha2-256`); `blind` (`true` or `false`); `sealing`, the modes of annex H it holds; `leaseMax` and `leaseDefault` in seconds; `copiesMax`; `pin` (`request,invoke`); `challenge` (`true`); `tier` (`hot` or `cold`); `delegates`; `policy` (`open` or `grant`); and `invoke-ops` (`pin,unpin,status,challenge`, annex F). Every value is text, because `access` and `costHints` are `map<String,String>` in annex I: an integer is canonical decimal, a boolean is `true` or `false`, and a list is comma separated without spaces. An integer, a boolean, or a list in either map would make the card undecodable for every receiver, its own issuer's runtime included. A reader ignores a key it does not know (P2-F-03). A store whose policy is `grant` and which has no authorizer adds `waiting` = `authorizer`, takes no pins, and refuses the `pin` invoke until an authorizer appears, and a requester passes such a card by. The store refreshes `free` on every holding interval, and on close it publishes a last card with `free` = `0` and a time to live of two holding intervals. A store advertises the content-swarm feature level 2 (§8) and refuses to start at level 1, since only a level-2 member answers a possession challenge. A requester finds stores by `find(AssetCard)` with `access.kind` = `content-store`. The pin entries a store takes are reserved types (§7.1), and annex H states how a store takes, holds, renews, releases, and proves a root. The golden vector `asset_card_content_store_cbor` carries a card.

**6.1b Unknown values (v0.1.11, normative).** Enumerated advertisement fields (membership policy, conflict strategy, admission, replication) are encoded as their names. A receiver that meets a name it does not know MUST treat the advertisement as undecodable and drop it without caching or forwarding it, exactly as it treats a malformed one; it MUST NOT substitute a default. This is what lets a revision add a value (v0.1.11 added two admission rules) without a wire-format change: an older peer refuses the advertisement it cannot interpret, and the space or group it describes stays invisible to that peer rather than being joined under the wrong rule. *Closed card fields (v0.1.32; AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09).* The rule reaches the closed fields of the `AppCard`, each written as text: `kind`, `shape`, `ui.transport`, `ui.components[].role`, `host.signOn`, and `host.status` (§6.1a.2 to §6.1a.4), and the names in `spill.parts` (§6.1a.5). Every implementation checks them when it decodes the card. The open values of `origin` and `formats` stay outside the rule (§6.1a.2).

**6.1c The action contract (v0.1.25 addendum; ISSUE-CardContract).** A `CardAction` MAY carry a `contract`, appended after `kind` and omitted from the wire when absent, so an action without one is byte-identical to the v0.1.13 action: `pre`, the situation names or conditions that must hold before the action runs beside the schema it consumes; `post`, the schemas it produces and the situations it establishes beside the schema it produces; `cost`, a free hint a planner orders by (a number, a duration, `low` or `high`); and `methods`, the HTN method names the action belongs to (reserved). Each list holds at most 32 terms and the cost hint at most 64 characters. The binder carries a contract from `@SpaceTake(pre, post, cost)` and `@OrderedTake(pre, post, cost)`; the Embabel extension from `@Action(pre, post, cost)`; a package manifest's card actions carry the same fields. A planner (the `agentspaces-planner` module: the `Planner` SPI, the enablement planner, the bounded search planner) reads a key's state from its entries and its marking (§6.4) and the fleet's actions with contracts, and answers with steps a peer writes as `PlanStep` entries (§6.4) that workers take through their ordinary verbs. The golden vector `card_action_contract_cbor` pins the canonical bytes. *Compatibility.* A reader of an earlier revision decodes the action and ignores the field; the TypeScript, Python, Ruby, and Clojure clients keep a card's actions as data and pass the contract through unread.

**6.2 TTL semantics.** `ttl` bounds cache lifetime from `issued`; caches MUST evict expired advertisements and MUST NOT forward them. Issuers refresh by re-publishing with a new `issued`. Recommended defaults: PeerAdvertisement 10 minutes; AgentCard 15 minutes; SpaceAdvertisement 15 minutes; GroupAdvertisement 24 hours; capability advertisements 15 minutes. Short TTLs serve as the liveness mechanism (P2): the freshness of an advertisement answers the question "does this peer still offer this capability?" with no failure-detection round trips.

**6.3 The ad-cache and discovery.** Every peer maintains a local advertisement cache per group, which gossip populates. Discovery is therefore local-first: `discovery.find(type, predicate)` queries the local cache, and `remoteFind` escalates in parallel to the group's rendezvous peers (a direct query to each) and to a scoped gossip query (a rumor carrying the predicate, with answers routed back to the asker) under the hop budget, then waits for answers and re-runs the local scan. `remoteFind` validates the predicate against the advertisement type before it publishes anything, so a predicate naming an unknown field fails immediately with an argument error and never reaches the network. Core predicates are structural (type plus field matchers). Layer 4 defines a semantic-discovery capability service (embedding-based matching over AgentCard descriptions) so that the base protocol stays model-free.

*The cache budget (v0.1.32; AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09).* Every peer bounds its advertisement cache by entry count and by bytes, the sum of the lengths of its cached advertisements' canonical encodings (`adBytes`). Both bounds are configurable. The defaults are 8,192 advertisements and 33,554,432 bytes (32 MiB), each multiplied by four on a peer with the RENDEZVOUS role. After it evicts expired advertisements, a cache refuses a new id that would pass either bound, and it refuses a newer version of a cached id that would pass the byte bound and keeps the version it holds. It counts each refusal in `adsRefusedCacheFull`. A refusal drops the advertisement from this cache alone and strikes nobody; anti-entropy offers it again once room returns. The budget replaces the sentence "a card is under 4 KiB" that v0.1.24 to v0.1.31 gave the ModelCard and the AppCard, which no implementation checked, and no card kind has a size cap of its own (§6.1a.5).

## 7. Layer 3: The AgentSpace (Distributed Tuple Space)

### 7.1 Data model

An Entry is any serializable domain object (Java record or POJO) registered with the space's schema registry. The space wraps each written entry in an EntryRecord:

```
EntryRecord {
  entryId     : UUID (issuer-generated)
  spaceId     : URI
  type        : schema name + version
  payload     : canonical serialized entry  (inline if ≤ 64 KiB)
  payloadRef  : content reference           (for large payloads: a block CID, multibase z, fetched through the block exchange; or a content root, multibase b, fetched through the content swarm, §7.1)
  issuer      : AgentID
  issued      : timestamp (hybrid logical clock)
  lease       : { holder, expires, kind: WRITE | TAKE }
  tags        : map<string,string>          (indexable)
  sig         : issuer signature
  keyEpoch    : content-key epoch           (v0.1.13, appended; omitted for epoch 0, §11a.1)
}
```

*Reserved types (v0.1.14).* A few entry types are the library's own and every replica MUST name them identically whatever registry an application uses for its domain types: `SpaceCredential` (§7.5), the vote's `Proposal`, `Ballot`, and `Decision` (§8), and `JoinTicket` (§10.3), the ticket a fan-in writes and takes in its fleet-wide modes. *Rule entries (v0.1.24, ISSUE-Constraints).* The rule entries `Vocabulary`, `Constraint`, `Goal`, `ConstraintViolated`, `GoalStuck`, `PlanStep`, and `JuryDecision` are reserved under `ai.badmonkey.agentspaces.constraints.<Name>#v1`, tagged `charter`, `charterVersion`, and `unit`; a peer that knows no rules stores and replicates them as any foreign type. A registry that names an application's types by an ontology namespace delegates everything else to the default `<fully.qualified.ClassName>#v1` rule. A client that knows no joins stores and replicates a `JoinTicket` as any foreign type and never acts on it.

*Store entries (v0.1.32; srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md §3.2.2 and §6.10.3, rule 18).* The content store of §6.1a.6 reads three reserved types: `ai.badmonkey.agentspaces.agent.content.StorePin#v1`, the terms a requester signs; `ai.badmonkey.agentspaces.agent.content.SignedPin#v1`, the entry a store takes, which carries the terms byte for byte under the requester's signature; and `ai.badmonkey.agentspaces.agent.content.StoreReceipt#v1`, a store's record that it holds a root. A `SignedPin` carries its terms' tags `root` and `requester`, and a `StoreReceipt` the tags `root` and `holder`. All three are protocol records with `@WireRecord` and the schemas of annex I.1a, read strictly under §9.1 by the stores and requesters that know them. They travel without a gate: a replica that does not know them stores, replicates, and serves them as foreign types and never decodes them. A store reads no bare `StorePin` entry. None of the three gains a field; a change of shape takes a new type name (P2-F-08). Annex H gives the rules of the pin.

A replica gossips each record inside its replication state, which carries the mutable CRDT fields and their signature (§7.3, §11a.4):

```
EntryStateDto {
  record           : EntryRecord
  issuerPublicKey  : the issuer peer's raw Ed25519 key
  adds, removes    : observed-remove dots
  leaseStamp       : HLC stamp of the lease register
  leaseValue       : the current lease
  completed        : monotone completion flag
  stateSig         : signature over the mutable state (§11a.4)
  agentCertificate : the issuer agent's certificate     (v0.1.11, appended; omitted when absent, §4.2)
  stateCertificate : the state signer's certificate     (v0.1.13, appended; omitted when absent)
  signer           : AgentID of the state signer        (v0.1.13, appended; omitted when absent)
  signedAt         : HLC stamp of the state signature   (v0.1.13, appended; omitted when absent)
}
```

Large payloads (model weights, images, sensor batches) are content-addressed, and a Bitswap-style block exchange transfers them out of band; the space replicates only the record. This keeps gossip traffic small and provides deduplication and integrity checking at no extra cost. *Blocks and roots (v0.1.23, normative; SRAD large-networks-bulk-data).* A `payloadRef` beginning with the multibase prefix `z` is the CID of one block (`z` + base58btc(sha-256(bytes))), carried whole in one `BLOCK` frame and therefore bounded by the frame cap; a writer refuses a block a frame cannot carry. A `payloadRef` beginning with `b` is a *content root*: the CIDv1 (multibase base32, multicodec raw 0x55, multihash sha2-256) of the concatenated sha2-256 hashes of the artifact's fixed-size pieces (a power of two from 64 KiB to 4 MiB; the last piece may be shorter). A root names an artifact of any size. Its *manifest* (annex G) is an ordinary signed, leased entry of the reserved type `ai.badmonkey.agentspaces.agent.content.Manifest#v1` in the group's content space, tagged `root`, carrying the size, the piece size, the piece hashes inline up to 64 pieces and as a block CID beyond, the sealing state, and metadata; a *holding* (`...Holding#v1`, tagged `root` and `holder`, short-leased and refreshed) states which pieces one member holds, so the replicated space is the provider index. Pieces move as `PIPE_DATA` payloads of the capability `aspace:cap/content-swarm` (§8) between members over whatever transport sessions exist, from every holder at once; a receiver verifies each piece against the table before storing or serving it, and a member that completes an artifact publishes a complete holding and serves it. A space escalates a payload above its configured threshold (default 4 MiB) by publishing it as an artifact and recording the root; between the inline limit and the threshold the block path applies. A reader that sees a `b` reference it cannot resolve (no content swarm) treats the entry as it treats an absent block. Under a content key the space seals the payload before escalation, so the artifact's pieces are the sealed bytes; an artifact published directly to the swarm under a content key is sealed per range at send time instead (§8).

*Tree roots (v0.1.29, normative; SRAD agentspaces-pdt).* A `payloadRef` beginning with `b` whose CIDv1 multicodec is `dag-cbor` (0x71) names a *persistent tree* (annex H): the tree of content-addressed nodes whose root node the CID names, moved by the tree messages of the content swarm (§8) from every holder of the root. A reader tells the two apart by the codec alone, 0x55 a piece artifact and 0x71 a tree, and a bulk implementation MUST refuse a reference of the other codec. A space that opts into tree escalation (`agentspaces.pdt.spaces.<name>.escalate-above`, default off) hands a payload above its threshold to the tree encoder, which decodes the payload's CBOR, holds every map, array, and byte string above 4 KiB as a sub-tree of annex H and everything else in place, and records the root as the `payloadRef`; the reassembled bytes are identical to the canonical payload. Because the next payload shares every unchanged sub-tree with this one, a reader that holds the earlier version fetches only the nodes that changed. The replica pins the root while the entry lives and releases it on removal, exactly as it holds an artifact; a reader that sees a tree root it cannot resolve treats the entry as it treats an absent block. *The pre-seal rule.* A space that escalates to trees MUST NOT seal the payload before escalation, since a payload sealed under a fresh nonce shares nothing between versions; it records the key ring's write epoch in `keyEpoch` without encrypting the payload, and the nodes travel sealed per message under that epoch (annex H, mode `group-key`) or are sealed deterministically per node (mode `sealed-nodes`). The record signature covers `payloadRef`, so the root binds the tree to the entry. Before escalating, a writer checks that every current member of the group advertises the `trees` parameter (§8); when one lacks it, the writer escalates through the piece artifact when a swarm is configured and through the block or inline path otherwise, and `agentspaces.pdt.spaces.<name>.require=true` turns that fallback into a refusal of the write naming the members (the view a space checks does not exist when the space is built, so the refusal is per write, as `ReplicatedSpace.escalationSeam` raises it).

### 7.2 Operations

The space exposes the Linda quartet plus events, all lease-aware.

`write(entry, lease)` publishes an entry and returns an `EntryHandle` for renewal or cancellation. The write lease is the entry's TTL, so unrenewed entries vanish (P2).

`read(template, timeout)` and `readAll(template, limit)` perform non-destructive matches.

*Matching order (v0.1.7).* Within one entry type, a single-node space evaluates candidates in insertion order, so `read` and `take` prefer the oldest matching entry of the template's type: first-in first-out among equals. The implementation partitions storage by entry class (matching never scans entries a template's type cannot accept), and the ordering guarantee is the observable face of that index. Across different entry types, and on replicated spaces, where arrival order differs per replica and take arbitration (§7.4) decides winners, no inter-entry order is promised.

`take(template, takeLease, timeout)` performs a destructive, exclusive match under the space's conflict strategy (§7.4). The taker holds a TAKE lease; if the taker dies or lets the lease lapse without calling `complete()`, the entry reappears. This reproduces the JavaSpaces crash-recovery idiom. JavaSpaces built it on transactions; AgentSpaces builds it on leases.

`complete(handle, resultEntry?)` consumes the taken entry permanently and optionally writes a result atomically: the result record is built and validated before the completion commits, so a result that cannot be written never leaves the task completed-but-resultless.

`notify(template, listener, lease)` registers a leased subscription. Events arrive at least once, with entry-record deduplication, and give applications a foundation for complex event processing (CEP) over the entry stream. The event kinds are WRITTEN, TAKEN, COMPLETED, EXPIRED, and REAPPEARED; a replicated space emits EXPIRED and REAPPEARED from its lease sweep exactly once per transition, so a subscriber can build a watchdog on a replicated space as on a local one.

*Cancel events (v0.1.32, normative; core cancel events, 2026-11-09).* A cancel raises EXPIRED, the kind a lapse raises, so a subscriber hears an entry leave the same way whether its writer withdrew it or let it lapse. The cancel travels as the issuer's signed state whose `removes` cover its `adds` (§7.3, §11a.4), and the wire carries nothing new. The cancelling replica raises EXPIRED at once, and every other replica that held the entry present raises it when it merges the withdrawal. A replica raises one EXPIRED per entry, whichever comes first of the cancel and the lease sweep, and a redelivered cancel raises nothing. A replica that first learns of an entry from its withdrawn state raises nothing, since the entry was never present there. Completion dominates a concurrent cancel: a cancel that reaches a replica after the entry's completion raises nothing there, and a completion that merges after a withdrawal still raises COMPLETED, as it does after a lapse. A writer's cancel of an entry its replica holds completed or withdrawn does nothing and sends nothing, as on a local space. A holder whose replica merged the withdrawal before the holder completed cannot complete, as after a lapsed take, and a take whose settle window ends after the withdrawal merged comes back empty.

Templates are typed: `Template.of(TaskEntry.class).where("kind", eq("summarize")).where("priority", gte(3))`. Matching runs against local replicas, so reads never block on the network (local-first, P7). `read(template, ConsistencyHint.FRESH, timeout)` and `readAll(template, ConsistencyHint.FRESH, limit)` perform exactly one anti-entropy pull toward one random group member and then match locally; `ConsistencyHint.LOCAL` is the default and matches the replica as it stands. Content-addressed entries (§7.1) follow the same rule: a read never waits on a block. A read that meets a record whose block is absent starts one background fetch; a blocking `read` awaits that fetch within its own timeout, and `readAll` skips entries whose block has not arrived.

*Tag conditions (v0.1.14).* A template MAY carry conditions on the record's tags beside its field predicates: `Template.of(Finding.class).whereTag("rdf:type", eq(iri))`, or `hasTag("region")`. A replica judges tag conditions against `EntryRecord.tags` after the type comparison and before it decodes the payload, so the matching order within a type is type, tags, claim, decode, fields, and a keyless relay or a tag-sharded replica can select by tag without holding the content key, which is why `type` and `tags` stay plaintext under group content keys (§11). Tag conditions apply to every verb, including the subscription a `notify` registers. A read MAY return the record's metadata beside the decoded value (`readAllEntries`: entry id, authenticated issuer, attestation, tags, lease, issue stamp), and a subscription event carries the same view, so a listener reads an entry's tags without a second read. A completion MAY write its result with tags, atomically with the completion, as a write can.

*The portable filter grammar (v0.1.21, normative).* A field condition stated as text, as a binder annotation or a client template carries it, is one clause `field op literal` with `op` one of `=`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, or `in` (a comma-separated list, `field in a,b,c`). The earliest operator in the clause is the operator, the longest at a tie, and an operator at the first position is malformed. A clause is judged against the field's value's string form: numerically when both the value and the literal parse as decimal numbers (so `3.0` equals `3`), else by the string's lexicographic order; a null value matches `!=` alone; `in` matches when any trimmed element equals the value. Every implementation judges identically; the golden vectors `filter_grammar_cases` and `filter_grammar_malformed` pin it.

### 7.3 Replication

A space replicates across the peers of its group (full replication by default; tag-sharded partial replication is also implemented, so a replica can hold only the slice of a space its tag predicate selects). The replica state is a delta-CRDT: the entry set is an OR-Set keyed by `entryId` with add and remove semantics, the lease fields are last-writer-wins registers ordered by hybrid logical clock, and tombstones persist for twice the maximum lease before garbage collection. The collection rule is exact: a replica collects an entry that is completed, withdrawn, or past its write lease once `leaseExpiry + 2 × maxLease` has passed, where `maxLease` is the largest lease the replica has observed, capped at 24 hours, or a value the builder fixes. A replica MUST refuse a state re-offered by a lagging partner once that state is past the collection horizon, so a collected tombstone never returns. Deltas ride the group's rumor channel, and digests reconcile via anti-entropy. Consequently, replicas that have seen the same updates are identical, partitions merge deterministically, and a disconnected robot's local space continues to serve reads, writes, and (strategy permitting) takes. The mutable CRDT fields a delta carries are themselves authenticated (§11a.4), so a relay or any other member cannot forge a completion, a removal, or a lease that would make a signed entry vanish.

*The recordless state delta (v0.1.29, normative; SRAD agentspaces-pdt group L).* The `record` of an `EntryStateDto` (§7.1) MAY be null on a delta that carries a renew, a cancel, or a complete. A writer omits the record only when (a) every member of its current view advertises the hint `wire.recordless=1` (§9; on by default in every implementation, off in Java through `PeerNode.Builder.recordless(false)`) and (b) its replica holds evidence that the record replicated since the write, a digest match or an acknowledgement for the entry. A receiver that holds the record merges the state; a receiver that does not keeps the state pending, emits no WRITTEN event, and receives the whole DTO in the next anti-entropy round, which already ships whole DTOs for differing lines. The state signature never covered the record (§11a.4), so no signature changes. The golden vector `state_delta_recordless_cbor` pins a renew delta with `record` null. The Python, TypeScript, and Ruby clients accept a null record, keep the state pending, and advertise the hint.

*Stamp bounds on folded states (v0.1.30, normative; HLC logical counter, 2026-11-08 (Rust team report); HLC round four, 2026-11-08).* A replica judges three stamps of a state: the record's `issued` stamp and the state's `leaseStamp`, which it folds into its hybrid logical clock, and the `signedAt` of an agent-signed state (§11a.4), which dates the signer's certificate. Each of the three is a stamp like any other and travels as the CBOR item §9 fixes. A replica MUST refuse a state when any of the three stamps carries a logical counter above `LOGICAL_LIMIT` (65536). The replica judges the stamps after the state's signatures and certificates verify and after the revocation check (§6.1), and before the state merges, so a state whose actor the group revoked meets the revocation refusal alone and counts in no stamp counter (HLC round five, 2026-11-08), and the refusal names the party that signed the refused stamp: the record's issuer for `issued`, and the party authorized for the transition (the claim holder for a completion, the issuer for every other transition) for `leaseStamp` and `signedAt`. The replica drops the whole state, feeds none of its stamps to its clock, counts the refusal (`stampsLogicalRefused`), and, where it keeps strikes (§9), strikes that party through the same misbehavior report its admission check (§7.5) uses. A state whose `leaseStamp` is null decodes in every implementation, so its delta counts nothing as undecodable; it holds no lease register, so the replica merges nothing from it, counts nothing for it, and strikes nobody (HLC round five, 2026-11-08; HLC round six, 2026-11-08, R8). A state that omits the field breaks the record rule P1 of §9.1, so its delta is undecodable (HLC round six, 2026-11-08).

*Counting a refusal (HLC round four, 2026-11-08).* A replica keys each refusal on signed content alone: for a state, the entry id, the record's `issued` stamp, and the state's signed view (§11a.4); for a claim (§7.4), the entry id and the claim's signed map. It counts and strikes after the signatures verify, once per distinct key, however often and by whatever path partners offer the same signed content, so neither a relay that re-wraps the content nor a forger who cannot sign moves a count. The promise of one count per distinct key rests on a bounded cache of refused offers, 4096 keys in every implementation, which evicts the least recently offered key first, so a key that partners keep offering stays in the cache (HLC round five, 2026-11-08): a state or claim that falls out of the cache and arrives again counts and strikes again. The cache only stops a second count. A replica judges every offer afresh against its current wall time and never refuses an offer because the cache holds its key (HLC round five, 2026-11-08). A replica that sends space digests MUST acknowledge a refused state in its digest as an `a:<line>` acknowledgement (TECH-SPEC §7.5), as it acknowledges a state whose actor the group revoked (§6.1), so its partners stop re-offering the state, and it acknowledges only a state whose signatures verified, so a forger cannot make it acknowledge a line it never judged. A replica that pulls with empty digests has no digest line to acknowledge with and relies on the cache alone. A stamp's logical counter never changes, so the replica refuses a state with an unacceptable stamp on every later offer, by rumor and by anti-entropy alike, and a hostile entry reaches the clock of no conforming replica however far it travels. A stamp outside the canonical grammar makes its delta undecodable in every implementation, exactly as any other item in a stamp's place does: the replica drops the delta, counts it (`deltasUndecodable`), and strikes nobody (§9). A state whose stamps are acceptable and within the far-future bound below merges exactly as before.

*Far-future records (HLC round four, 2026-11-08; HLC round five, 2026-11-08).* A replica measures the `issued` stamp and the `leaseStamp` of a verified state, the `signedAt` of a verified agent-signed state (HLC round six, 2026-11-08, owner ruling F4), and the stamp of a verified gossiped claim (§7.4), against its own wall time, with `MAX_DRIFT` (ten minutes) and `SKEW_MARGIN` (5 seconds) as in §9. The rule binds a peer-signed carrier and an agent-signed one alike: the certificate of an agent-signed record or claim bounds its stamp by the certificate's validity window alone (§4.2), and the replica judges the stamp here once the signature and the certificate verify (HLC round five, 2026-11-08). A stamp whose physical part is at most `wall + MAX_DRIFT + SKEW_MARGIN` merges, under the clamp of §9. A stamp past that bound and at most one hour further, `wall + MAX_DRIFT + SKEW_MARGIN + 3600000` ms, defers its carrier: the replica does not merge the state or the claim, feeds no stamp to its clock, does not acknowledge the carrier in its digest, does not remember it as refused, and strikes nobody. The replica counts the deferral once per distinct key in `stampsFarFutureDeferred`, a count of no misbehavior that lets an operator see a skewed or dragged peer (§9, the counters). Anti-entropy and later rumors offer the deferred carrier again, and it merges once the replica's wall time has advanced far enough. A stamp further past the bound than that hour is hostile: the replica refuses the carrier, counts the refusal once per distinct key in `stampsFarFutureRefused` and never in `stampsLogicalRefused`, strikes the signer of the stamp with the reason `far-future stamp` where it keeps strikes, and acknowledges a refused state in its digest. The replica judges every offer afresh against its wall time at that offer, so a carrier it refused as hostile merges when a partner offers it again after the replica's wall time has caught up, and the cache of refused offers keeps that later offer from counting twice (HLC round five, 2026-11-08). A replica judges the far-future bound after the logical limit, so a stamp that fails both counts as a logical refusal. The certificate check of §4.2 bounds an agent's `signedAt` by the certificate's validity window alone, so the replica merges, defers, or refuses an agent-signed state by its `signedAt` exactly as by its `issued` and `leaseStamp`, and the party authorized for the transition signs both `leaseStamp` and `signedAt` (HLC round six, 2026-11-08, owner ruling F4). Before v0.1.31 the certificate check bounded `signedAt` at the receiver's wall time plus `MAX_DRIFT`, so a state signed further ahead failed verification and dropped uncounted. A claim the ordered log commits meets the logical limit alone, because the log's arbitration must stay identical on every member. The deferral protects an honest taker. A hostile member can drag the taker's clock to the taker's own ceiling with one acceptable stamp, so the taker's next writes, renewals, and claims carry stamps up to `MAX_DRIFT` ahead of the taker's wall time, and a replica whose wall clock trails the taker's would otherwise refuse each of them and strike the taker until it quarantined an honest peer.

### 7.4 Conflict-resolution strategies (`take` exclusivity)

Exclusive take is the one operation CRDTs cannot express, so it is where strategies differ. A space declares its strategy in its SpaceAdvertisement. The API stays identical across strategies, which makes the strategy choice orthogonal to application code; only the guarantees and the latency change. The core defines three strategies.

`LEASE_RACE` (default). Takers write a claim (a TAKE-lease LWW register on the entry) and wait one gossip settle-window (configurable, default twice the gossip period). Arbitration is deterministic: the claim with the lowest `(hlc, peerId)` pair takes the entry, and the other takers receive an empty result and retry elsewhere. The strategy guarantees at most one completed take per entry, because the CRDT merge rule rejects a second `complete`; transient duplicate work remains possible during partitions. The cost is one settle-window of latency and zero extra machinery. This default suits idempotent tasks, and agent workloads should strive to be idempotent.

`AUCTION` (probabilistic, cost-aware). The take is a bid: `take(template, bid(cost))`. Rounds of a CBAA/CBBA-style auction-plus-consensus among the current bidders allocate the entries matching the template: each peer locally selects the tasks it expects to secure at its bid, gossips its winner list, and conflicts resolve in favor of the better bid with deterministic tie-breaks, converging in O(diameter) gossip rounds to a conflict-free assignment. This turns the space itself into a decentralized least-cost task allocator: agents write tasks, agents bid, and the space converges. The application supplies the bid functions, which may be learned policies.

`ORDERED` (strict). Takes serialize through an ordered-log capability service (a small Raft quorum elected within the group; see §8). This yields exactly-once take semantics at the price of quorum liveness requirements, and it suits only non-idempotent, high-stakes entries (P1). ORDERED is not selected as a space strategy; instead the ordered-log capability's take coordinator drives the takes over the log, and its accept decision is a pure function of committed state (the claim's generation), so every member arbitrates identically regardless of when it applies a log entry. Arbitration under ORDERED is by epoch alone: the log admits exactly one claim per epoch (a committed claim's epoch MUST equal the entry's current epoch plus one), so no two committed claims ever share an epoch and the lattice's stamp comparison is never reached for them. A log's decision is authoritative for its epoch: a replica that installed a committed claim MUST NOT let a gossip-borne claim at the same or a lower epoch displace it, while an identical claim may still merge (so a replica holding an unattested copy is upgraded by the attested one). The committed claim is installed at every replica exactly as its holder signed it, with the holder's key and signature carried through the log unchanged; the coordinator MUST NOT re-sign or re-stamp it, because a completion is authenticated against the key the stored claim carries (§11a.4), and a claim signed by any other key can authenticate no completion at any other replica. (v0.1.11, QA4 A4-5: the reference implementation had re-stamped committed claims with the log index and re-signed them locally, which bought arbitration priority at the cost of the holder's attestation; a replica that missed the completion rumor could then never converge by anti-entropy, whose digest keys claims by content.)

*Claim stamps (v0.1.10).* Because LEASE_RACE prefers the lowest stamp, a replica bounds the stamps it will merge, so an admitted member cannot win every race by back-dating its claims. Two rules apply. A claim's stamp MUST NOT precede the entry's `issued` stamp by more than the clock drift ceiling (ten minutes, §11), which is a pure function of the two stamps and so decides identically on every replica. A claim that contests another holder's live claim MUST NOT be older than the replica's current time minus the drift ceiling minus one settle window. A replica drops a claim that breaks either rule and strikes its signer once per distinct claim, and it feeds the stamps of the claims and leases it accepts into its own hybrid logical clock under the bounded-drift regime. *A third rule (v0.1.30, normative; HLC logical counter, 2026-11-08 (Rust team report)).* A claim's stamp MUST be acceptable (§9): canonical text with a logical counter from 0 to `LOGICAL_LIMIT` (65536). Like the first rule, this rule is a pure function of the stamp and so decides identically on every replica. A replica drops a claim that breaks it before the claim enters the lattice, never feeds its stamp to the clock, counts the refusal (`stampsLogicalRefused`) after the claim's signature and certificate verify and its holder passes the revocation check (§6.1), so a revoked holder's claim counts in no stamp counter (HLC round five, 2026-11-08), and, where it keeps strikes (§9), strikes the claim's signer, once per distinct claim keyed on its entry id and signed map, under the same bounded cache of refused offers as a refused state (§7.3). A gossiped claim whose stamp lies past the receiver's far-future bound meets the deferral rule of §7.3 (HLC round four, 2026-11-08): further than one hour past `wall + MAX_DRIFT + SKEW_MARGIN` the replica refuses the claim, counts it, and strikes its signer, judged right after this third rule; within that hour the replica defers the claim, merges nothing, and strikes nobody. The rule binds a claim the ordered log commits as well: a replica applies it before it installs a committed claim (`ORDERED` above), counts the refusal, and strikes the holder when the holder's own signature attests the claim, so every member refuses the same committed claim and the log's arbitration stays identical across members. Without this rule, one claim stamped just inside the drift ceiling with a saturated counter would freeze each replica's clock that merged it, and that replica's own later claims, stamped from the frozen clock up to ten minutes ahead, would lose every contested race.

*The signed bytes of a claim (HLC round five, 2026-11-08).* A take claim is Java's `TakeClaim` record, whose seven fields keep the record's declared order on the wire (§9): `entryId`, `spaceId`, `epoch`, `stamp`, `holder`, `bid`, `expiresAtMillis`. Every implementation signs that encoding and verifies a claim's signature over it. A claim whose map arrives in any other order breaks the record rule P1 of §9.1, so its delta is undecodable at every replica alike (HLC round six, 2026-11-08, owner ruling F3); round five had verified such a claim over the re-encoded order. A bid is a finite double, and every implementation orders bids as Java's `Double.compare` does, so a bid of -0.0 ranks ahead of a bid of 0.0 (§9.1, P2).

*Orphaned claims (HLC round six, 2026-11-08, R10).* A claim merges before its entry's state, since a completion authenticates against it, so a claim can arrive for an entry that never follows. Every replica drops a claim on an entry it collected or never held once the claim's expiry passes, so the claim map stays bounded under churn. A live claim stays, and a partner that holds the entry offers the claim again with the state.

*What the two rules leave open (recorded 2026-09-23, gate2-review G2-2).* The second rule judges a claim against the claim a replica has already merged, so its outcome depends on what that replica saw first. Three consequences follow, and a deployment should know all three. A claim on an entry no replica has claimed yet is judged by the first rule alone, so an admitted member may stamp its claim at the entry's `issued` moment and beat every later honest claimant at any replica its claim reaches first: broad TAKE scope therefore carries the power to win contested races, and an operator who wants takers to compete on equal terms grants that scope narrowly. Two replicas that merged different first claims keep different winners, and anti-entropy does not reconcile them, because each replica applies the second rule again to the other's far-past claim and refuses it. A legitimately old claim from a healed partition meets the same rule: replicas that merged a newer claim first refuse it and strike its signer, while the returning replica keeps its own claim as the winner, so an honest peer can accrue strikes for a claim it made before the partition. A fleet that needs strict fairness or strict convergence here should narrow TAKE scope rather than rely on the arbitration alone.

A space fixes one strategy for all of its entries, which keeps the semantics legible. An application that needs several semantics uses several spaces (`tasks-bulk` on LEASE_RACE, `tasks-auction` on AUCTION, `commits` on ORDERED), the way it would use multiple queues.

### 7.5 Space lifecycle

A member creates a space by publishing a SpaceAdvertisement into a group, and any group member may then attach. The advertisement's `id` is `aspace://<groupID>/<spaceName>`, and it carries the space name, the conflict strategy, schema hints naming the entry types the space expects, the admission rule, and the replication mode (§6.1). A replicated space built with a discovery publisher publishes its advertisement on creation, and the founder keeps it alive by refreshing it within its TTL (15 minutes by default); any member finds the space by name with `find(SpaceAdvertisement.class, …)`, and an advertisement nobody refreshes lapses from every cache like any other. Every space the Spring starter configures publishes its advertisement this way and shares its group's block exchange (§10.1).

Space admission decides who may write, take, and complete, and the advertisement carries the rule so every member applies the same one. Under `GROUP`, the default, every group member may. Under `ALLOWLIST`, only the AgentIDs the founder lists may: a local `write`, `take`, or `complete` by an unlisted agent fails with a `SpaceAdmissionException`, an inbound record or claim from an unlisted agent is dropped and reported as misbehaviour, and reads stay open to the whole group. The starter configures the rule per space with `admission` and `allowed-agents` (§10.1) and fails fast on an empty allowlist, since a space nobody may write to is a configuration error.

Under `CREDENTIAL` (v0.1.11), the credential issuer, one PeerID named when the space is built, admits agents by writing credentials into the space itself. A space credential is an ordinary entry of the reserved type `SpaceCredential#v1` naming an AgentID and its scopes (`WRITE`, `TAKE`, or both), written under a lease equal to the credential's validity. The record signature of §7.1 already binds the entry to its issuer, every replica already stores and replicates it, and its lease is its expiry, so the mechanism adds no signature scheme and nothing on the wire. A replica MUST drop a `SpaceCredential` record whose issuer is any peer other than the configured credential issuer and MUST report it as misbehaviour. The credential issuer itself is always admitted to write and take. Any other agent is admitted for a scope when a live credential naming that AgentID and scope is present in the local replica, and replicas judge each transition once, as it arrives: a new entry needs its issuer's `WRITE`, a completion needs the claim holder's `TAKE`, a lease renewal needs the issuer's `WRITE`, and a withdrawal needs nothing. A credential that later lapses or is revoked therefore does not remove work it already admitted. A group-level revocation of an agent, an agent key, or a peer, as distinct from cancelling a space credential, follows the freeze rule stated in the `CredentialRevocation` paragraph of §6.1: what a replica already holds stays, and newly arriving signatures are refused by reason and signing time. A record or claim from an agent with no live local credential is dropped without a strike, since the credential may simply not have arrived yet; anti-entropy re-offers the delta on the next round and it merges once the credential is present. Under `ALLOWLIST` a refusal still strikes, because the list is local configuration and cannot be late. Admission is judged for the *acting agent*, never for the peer: when several agents of one peer share a replica through views (§4.2, QA4 A4-7 phase 3), each view's writes, claims, and completions are admitted or refused on that agent's own standing under `ALLOWLIST` and `CREDENTIAL`, so one agent of a peer may be admitted while its sibling is refused. `grant(agent, scopes, validity)` writes the credential, and only the issuer's node may call it; `revoke(agent)` cancels the agent's live credentials, and the cancellation replicates like any removal, so a revocation reaches the fleet within one gossip round. A credential the issuer stops renewing lapses on its own, which is P2 applied to admission. Credential entries replicate to every shard of a tag-sharded space regardless of the shard predicate, and the credential type stays out of the advertisement's schema hints.

Under `AUTHORIZER` (v0.1.11), an agent may write when the space's `Authorizer` (§11) permits `SPACE_WRITE` for its peer in the scope of the space name, and may take or complete when it permits `SPACE_TAKE`. A space thereby defers admission to the security profile's answerer: the group's membership grants under `dev-local` and `mtls`, the identity provider's scopes (`aspace:space-write:<space>`, `aspace:space-take:<space>`, or the unscoped forms for every space) under `mtls-oidc` and `zero-trust`. An `AUTHORIZER` refusal drops without a strike, like a `CREDENTIAL` refusal, because a token can arrive late exactly as a credential can. The starter configures either rule with `admission: credential` (with `credential-issuer`, defaulting to this node) or `admission: authorizer` (§10.1).

The founders lease the space itself. A space built with a founder lease turns read-only when that lease lapses, and a founder may also mark it read-only explicitly or renew the founder lease while it still runs. A read-only space refuses `write` and `take` with a `SpaceReadOnlyException`, still serves reads and subscriptions, and still accepts a bare `complete` so a take already in flight can finish; it stops refreshing its advertisement, so the advertisement's lapse is how the fleet learns the space has been released. Garbage collection of a read-only space after its longest entry lease is planned; today its tombstones are collected under the §7.3 rule and the space object stays attached until its host closes it.

### 7.6 Reserved space names (v0.1.27 addendum)

Beside the names a platform may choose, the specification reserves `commerce` (ISSUE-CommerceCapability): the space a trading group keeps its mandates and orders in, created with `LEASE_RACE`, `AUTHORIZER` admission, and a one-year lease on its entries, by a group that trades and by no other. A reader without the open commerce module sees its entries as typed maps.

## 8. Layer 4: Capability Services

A capability service is any protocol beyond the core that a peer offers to its group. The pattern is uniform. First, the provider publishes a CapabilityAdvertisement carrying the capability type URI, version, endpoint binding (a space it watches, or a direct pipe), parameters, cost hints, and TTL. Second, consumers discover it through the ad-cache. Third, binding is either space-mediated (requests and responses flow as entries in an agreed space, giving retries, observability, and decoupling at no extra cost; the right default for request/response capabilities, and what `vote` uses) or direct (a bidirectional pipe, the right choice for continuous protocols whose exchange rate would otherwise fill a space with entries nobody reads, and what `aggregate`, `gossip-learn`, `ordered-log`, `semantic-discovery`, and `key-wrap` use). The trade is explicit: a pipe-bound capability gives up the space's retries, its audit trail, and its visibility to the fleet console, and takes on the obligation to be driven by the protocol clock described below. A pipe-bound capability that wants any of those properties back publishes them into a space deliberately, as `gossip-learn` does with its per-epoch `Evaluation` entries. Fourth, the capability's own mini-spec defines the interaction itself. Space-mediated binding also supports service choreography: an application can sequence several capability invocations through shared spaces, with each service consuming the entries the previous one produced. The capability runtime that hosts providers validates a provider's advertisement before it stores or starts the provider, and it refreshes every hosted advertisement on each refresh tick; a host drives that tick itself or asks the runtime to schedule it at a period shorter than the advertisement TTL, and the Spring starter schedules it with the card refresh (§10.4). A capability runtime drives two distinct clocks and MUST NOT conflate them. The *advertisement clock* is the refresh tick just described, measured in minutes. The *protocol clock* is `CapabilityProvider.tick()`, which advances a continuous capability's own algorithm by one round: a push-sum exchange, a gossip-learning offer, a Raft election-timer step. The runtime registers its protocol tick with the peer's tick when it is constructed, so a provider is driven because it is registered, exactly as a replicated space converges because it is constructed and joins the same clock (§7.3); an application supplies no scheduler of its own. A provider declares whether it needs the protocol clock through `requiresTick()`, and a provider whose semantics are counted in ticks rather than wall time is told the true cadence through `driverCadence(Duration)` before its first advertisement, so a tick-derived wall-clock value it advertises — the ordered log's leader lease of §8 below — describes the clock it is really driven at rather than one assumed at construction. A host that needs determinism, a test above all, detaches from the peer tick and drives the protocol tick itself, declaring its own cadence. A continuous capability that has never completed a round holds nothing but its own local contribution, and it MUST refuse to answer a fleet-wide query in that state rather than return that contribution as if it were a fleet figure.

The release standardizes the following capabilities in informative annexes; they sit outside the core normative surface.

**`aspace:cap/aggregate`** provides gossip aggregation with six operators, `sum|avg|count|min|max|quantile`, over locally reported values or over a numeric field of entries matching a template. Any member starts an epoch under an agreed identifier, and all participants converge in O(log N) rounds. One pipe frame carries the whole family as a union: a push-sum `Share` (the sender halves its mass and ships half), an `Extremum` for max-gossip, a bucketed `Histogram` vector pushed like a share, and a `Roster`, the union of 8-byte participant tokens (the first 8 bytes of `sha-256(PeerID)`). `AVG` is the push-sum ratio of value to weight; `SUM` is that mean times the roster size; `COUNT` is the roster size; `MIN` and `MAX` are the extremum; `QUANTILE` reads a cumulative histogram over a declared range (100 equal buckets by default) and is exact to within one bucket. Epochs are paced to the gossip period and go quiet (v0.1.16, v0.1.17): a participant shares an epoch once per period, stops sharing it once its estimate has not moved for forty ticks until a share moves it again, and forgets it after a retention; a watch on an estimate settles on shares received, or, once it has heard the epoch at all, on that silence. In template mode each participant reads up to 10,000 local matches: `SUM` and `COUNT` contribute the local total and local match count, `AVG` runs a parallel epoch for the count so the result is entry-weighted, `MIN` and `MAX` contribute the local extremum (and nothing when nothing matches), and `QUANTILE` contributes one unit per match. Over a fully replicated space, template-mode `SUM` and `COUNT` count each entry once per replica, while the other operators are replication-invariant. The advertisement carries `modes=sum,avg,count,min,max,quantile`. This is the primitive for quorum sensing: fleet-wide battery, backlog depth, coverage. *Settling (v0.1.14, ISSUE-OnEstimate).* The aggregate MAY let a listener wait for an epoch to settle: `onEstimate(epochs, Settle(ticks, tolerance), listener)` is evaluated on every protocol tick after the exchange step, for every epoch the capability knows (started locally or first seen in a received share), and fires once per epoch per registration when the estimate has been present and within the tolerance of the previous tick's value (relative, absolute below 1e-9 so a zero epoch can settle) for `ticks` consecutive ticks; `Settle.FIRST` fires on the first present estimate. The listener runs off the tick thread. A settle rule does not detect a poisoned epoch; value poisoning stays an accepted risk (§11). `awaitSettled(epochId, settle, timeout)` is the blocking form; `awaitEstimate` remains the first-presence form.

**`aspace:cap/vote`** supports collective decisions in two implemented modes, `MAJORITY_GOSSIP` (preference vectors aggregated via push-sum; cheap and approximate) and `QUORUM` (explicit ballots written as entries and tallied once a configurable quorum of the electorate has voted); a third bio-inspired `CROSS_INHIBITION` mode (option competition for best-of-N choices under noise) is planned. A deployment MAY narrow the electorate further with the `Authorizer` of §11 (v0.1.12): a tally counts a ballot only when its authenticated issuer is in the electorate and `permits(issuer, VOTE, voteSpace)` holds, which lets an identity provider name the voters of a QUORUM decision with an `aspace:vote` scope as it names Raft voters and directive issuers. Both tests are evaluated locally at every tally, so a decision stays recomputable by any member that shares the replica's view of the electorate and its grants. A third test joins them in v0.1.13: a ballot whose issuer the group has revoked, or whose peer is revoked (§6.1), stops counting at the next tally, so a decision can reopen; an `@OnDecision` that already fired for the proposal does not fire again. *Counting granularity (v0.1.11, QA4 A4-7 phase 2, normative).* A tally counts identities at the granularity its authorizer speaks (§11): under `PEER` the counted identity is the ballot issuer's PeerID, so one member is one counted ballot however many agent names it writes; under `AGENT` each permitted agent counts once, and a ballot counts only when its record is `AGENT_ATTESTED` (§4.2), because a peer-asserted name is exactly what agent-level counting exists to distrust. Counting and authorization therefore never disagree, which is the invariant one-member-one-vote rests on; an implementation MUST NOT count per AgentID while authorizing per PeerID. A decision records the granularity that produced it. The electorate predicate is a liveness filter over who is currently present and is not an eligibility check: a fresh AgentCard alone admits no voter the authorizer does not permit. Proposing is an ordinary space write and is governed by the space's own admission rule rather than by `VOTE`. The electorate is a predicate over the authenticated ballot issuer, applied at every tally: the shipped rule counts a ballot while the issuer's AgentCard is unexpired in the group's ad-cache, so quorum is relative to the live electorate and a decision can reopen when cards lapse; the default electorate counts every authenticated issuer, for groups that publish no cards. A ballot for an option the proposal does not offer never counts. Ballots are signed entries, so any member can audit the tally from the space.

**The commerce capability (v0.1.27 addendum; ISSUE-CommerceCapability).** `aspace:cap/commerce` is a reserved capability type with no implementation in the core: a peer advertises it to say what it does in a trade. Its `parameters` carry `role`, one of `merchant`, `shopper`, `payment`, and MAY carry `profile` (a URL, the merchant's business profile as the Universal Commerce Protocol publishes it at `/.well-known/ucp`), `ucp` (a comma-separated list of the UCP capability ids served), and `jwk` (the public key the peer signs mandates with, as an RFC 7517 JWK, JSON). The reserved space `commerce` (§7.6) is where the group keeps its mandates and orders; the records that travel there, `Mandate` and `Order`, and the verification of an Agent Payments Protocol credential chain, are the open `agentspaces-commerce` module's, with golden vectors `mandate_cbor`, `order_cbor`, `capability_commerce_cbor`. The constants are `ai.badmonkey.agentspaces.api.ad.Reserved`.

**Cost-aware task allocation** ships today as the `AUCTION` space strategy (§7.4): the CBAA/CBBA engine runs inside the space, so agents write tasks, bid through `take(template, bid)`, and the space converges on a least-cost assignment. A standalone `aspace:cap/task-auction` capability that exposes the same engine directly, for bundle allocation with marginal-score bidding and heterogeneous constraints outside a single space, is planned; until then the strategy is the supported surface.

**`aspace:cap/ordered-log`** elects a Raft group among volunteering members and exposes an append log whose committed commands flow to listeners in commit order, backing the ORDERED strategy through a take coordinator, which carries each submitter's signed claim through the log unchanged and installs it on every member's replica with that attestation intact (§7.4). A log instance has a fixed member set; reconfiguring means starting a new log, and single-server membership changes are future work. The leader lease is an advertisement: each member's CapabilityAdvertisement carries `members`, its `role` (`leader`, `follower`, or `candidate`), and its `term`, and the leader's alone carries `leaseUntil`, the issue instant plus its election timeout in wall time (the election timeout in ticks times the tick period, one second by default). Consumers find the leader by filtering the providers of the type for `role=leader`; a stale advertisement is superseded by a later issue or lapses with its TTL.

**`aspace:cap/gossip-learn`** provides decentralized model improvement over a mergeable-model SPI: `MergeableModel<M>` supplies `merge` (which MUST be commutative), `encode`, `decode`, and a `name`, with element-wise weight averaging over big-endian doubles as the default. Models are content-addressed: the content identifier is `base58btc(sha-256(encode(model)))`, an encoding up to 64 KiB travels inline, and a larger one is stored in the group's block exchange and travels as its CID. The exchange is a token-passing offer and accept over pipes: a node sends `OFFER` to one sampled member and locks the offered model until the reply arrives or three ticks pass; an unlocked receiver merges the offer and answers `ACCEPT` with its own pre-merge snapshot, a locked receiver or non-participant answers `BUSY`, and the offerer merges the acceptance only when the token matches its outstanding offer. Both sides therefore merge exactly the same pair, so any interleaving of concurrent exchanges conserves the fleet sum of a commutative merge, and only a lost `ACCEPT` moves mass one-sidedly, after which the offerer times out. A merge that throws abandons the exchange. The epoch protocol writes one `Evaluation(modelId, epoch, loss, contentId)` entry per local model into a metrics space under a lease when a participant ends an epoch, so per-epoch losses are auditable from the space. The advertisement carries `merge=<name>`, `exchange=offer-accept`, and `content=inline` or `content=inline,cid`. The capability serves both classic gossip learning and DFL-style neighborhood averaging, and it explicitly suits training the AUCTION bid policies in situ.

**`aspace:cap/semantic-discovery`** maintains an embedding index over the group's advertisements (AgentCards, AssetCards, and CapabilityAdvertisements) for fuzzy lookup by meaning ("who can summarize legal PDFs cheaply?", "who holds customer order history?"). The embedder sits behind an SPI so deployments choose their model; the reference implementation ships a deterministic feature-hashing embedder that works with no model at all, and richer embedders plug in without protocol changes. Peers that rank with different embeddings return different matches, so the capability advertisement names its embedder (v0.1.12): the parameters `embedder` (a stable identity such as `hashing` or `spring-ai:text-embedding-3-small`), `dimensions`, and `normalized`. A remote query SHOULD go only to peers whose advertised embedder and dimensions equal the querier's; a peer that advertises no embedder is taken to match, and a deployment may lift the requirement. Indexing entry tags directly, so agents can search live entries by meaning and not only the peers that produce them, is planned.

**`aspace:cap/rtc-signal`** (v0.1.21) carries WebRTC signalling for the `webrtc` binding of §5.5: `OFFER`, `ANSWER`, `CAND`, and `END` bodies of annex B as `PIPE_DATA` payloads, origin-signed, each with a version `v` and unknown keys ignored, sent directly to a connected target and inside a `RELAY_FRAME` through a RELAY-role member otherwise. A node rate-limits signalling per remote peer (offers per minute, candidates per session, bytes per signal, a pending session expiring at the dial timeout), every value a property, and ignores an `OFFER` from a non-member; nothing caps the number of sessions or open connections. A node without the binding treats the capability's frames as unknown pipe data.

**`aspace:cap/content-swarm`** (v0.1.23; SRAD large-networks-bulk-data) moves the pieces of a content root (§7.1) between members. Its `PIPE_DATA` payloads are the tagged CBOR maps of annex G: `PIECE_WANT` (a range of a piece, with a request id), `PIECE` (the range, accepted only against an outstanding request with the same root, index, offset, and id), `HAVE` (advisory), `CANCEL`, `REJECT` (1 not held, 2 over the holder's upload budget, 3 refused by policy or grant, 4 unknown root), `MANIFEST_WANT`, and `MANIFEST` (the manifest value, validated against its root before use). A holder serves a member the authorizer permits `CONTENT_FETCH` for the root (every member by default); a fetcher asks the rarest piece first among the holders it can reach, holds a window of ranges per holder that grows on success and halves on a timeout, prefers holders by relay hop count, requests the last pieces from every holder and cancels the duplicates, and strikes a holder whose piece fails its hash. When the manifest says `sealing: group-key`, each `PIECE` range is AES-256-GCM under the epoch's content key with the key's nonce in front and the associated data `root|index|offset`; a keyless holder refuses to serve and a keyless fetcher cannot fetch. The advertisement binds `pipe` with the parameters `pieceSizes`, `sealing`, and `seedMode`. A *collection* is an `AssetCard` (§6.1a) with `access.kind = content-collection` whose `aspace:cap/data-query` rows are manifest summaries, so a holder lists artifacts that are not replicated as manifests.

*Trees (v0.1.29; SRAD agentspaces-pdt).* The content-swarm advertisement MAY carry the parameter `trees` = `"1"`, which says the member holds and serves the persistent trees of annex H. Four further `PIPE_DATA` payloads, each sent only to a member whose newest unexpired advertisement carries the parameter and dropped unread by any other: `NODE_WANT` (tag 8: `req`, `cids`, 1 to 256 CID strings), `NODES` (tag 9: `req`, then either `nodes`, a list of node byte strings, or `sealed`, one sealed byte string with its `epoch`, and `missing`, at most 256 CIDs the holder lacks; at most 4 MiB of node or sealed bytes), `TREE_WANT` (tag 10: `req`, `root`, `haves`, at most 64 roots or null, `budget` in (0, 64 MiB], and `sketch`, reserved and ignored at level 1, with the level-2 grammar below), and `TREE_END` (tag 11: `req`, `frontier`, at most 1,024 CIDs, `reason` 1 done, 2 budget reached, 3 a `haves` root not held, 4 the walk cap). A holder answers a `TREE_WANT` breadth first from the root, prunes every subtree it finds under a `haves` root it holds, stops at the budget, at 100,000 walked nodes, or at its upload budget, and closes with `TREE_END` and the frontier; when it holds none of the `haves` it sends the top two levels and closes with reason 3. A `NODES` is accepted only against an outstanding `req` to that peer; every node is verified against the CID asked for or a link of a node already verified, and a node that fails strikes its sender. `REJECT` (tag 5) answers a refused tree request with `index` -1 and the root (or the first CID of a `NODE_WANT`); the `CONTENT_FETCH` operation, the grant, and the upload and download budgets govern tree traffic as they govern pieces. In `group-key` mode a holder seals the node list under the write epoch with the associated data `aspace:pdt|<recipient PeerID>|<req>`, and a keyless holder refuses (reason 3) a requester whose advertisement says `sealing = group-key`. A holding for a tree root has `have` null, `complete` from the node store, and `count` the nodes held under the root. A receiver drops and counts a message outside the bounds.

*PDT 2 (v0.1.32; srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md §6.10, P2-F-01 to P2-F-08).* The content-swarm advertisement MAY carry the parameter `pdt`, the member's PDT feature level as canonical decimal text, and this revision defines level `"2"`. A member without the parameter, or with any other text, stands at level 1, as every 0.5.0 member that advertises `trees` = `"1"` does; `trees` keeps the value `"1"` and its meaning, since a 0.5.0 member compares it with `"1"` exactly. A member sends a level-2 form only to a member whose newest unexpired content-swarm advertisement carries a level of 2 or above, and a member advertises level 2 only once every decoder it runs reads every level-2 schema of annex I.1a under §9.1 and acts on each form or answers with the fallback below. The level gates point-to-point forms alone: entries, advertisements, and rumours reach members that join later, so they travel under the rules of annex H instead. A field that a later level adds to a point-to-point message is appended, omitted when null, and sent only above the gate, so a message without it keeps its level-1 bytes; a field that a later level adds to a head, a manifest, or a card goes into that record's open text map (`attrs`, `access`). The level-2 forms are these.

- *The possession challenge, `CHALLENGE` (tag 12):* `req`, `root`, and `picks`, 1 to 16 of `{cid, offset, length}`. A pick without `offset` and `length` names a node of the root, or for an artifact root a piece by the raw CID of its table digest; a pick with both names a chunk group of a BLAKE3 blob by the blob's identity (annex H). A holder answers with a `NODES` under the same `req` that carries the picks it holds and lists the rest in `missing`, or with `REJECT` reason 1 when it holds none, reason 3 when the challenger may not fetch the root or a pick, and reason 2 over its upload budget. The challenger verifies each node by its CID and refuses an answer that arrives after its deadline.
- *Set reconciliation, the `sketch` grammar:* a `TREE_WANT` whose `root` is `""`, whose `haves` are empty, and whose `sketch` is set opens or continues a round that reconciles two sets of CIDs.
