The holder answers with a `TREE_END` of reason 5, an empty `frontier`, and its reply in `sketch`, a byte string appended after `reason` and omitted when null, and the asker repeats under one `req` until a reply carries no ranges. The leading byte of `sketch` selects the encoding: 0x61 the negentropy v1 protocol and 0x62 RIBLT (annex H). A holder that does not reconcile, or that reads the asker at level 1, answers `REJECT` reason 3, and past 64 rounds of one session it answers reason 2. A holder accepts `root` `""` only beside a `sketch`, a `TREE_END` carries `sketch` exactly when its reason is 5, and a sketch holds at most 64 KiB.
- *Intra-chunk deltas, `NODES.deltas`:* a list of at most 256 `{cid, base, dcz}`, appended after `missing` and omitted when null, each of which rebuilds the blob chunk `cid` from the chunk `base` by the `dcz` rule of annex H. A holder adds a delta only to a plaintext answer to a `TREE_WANT` whose `haves` show that the receiver holds the base's root, and only when the frame is under half the chunk; it adds none to a sealed answer or to an answer to `NODE_WANT`. Node bytes and delta bytes together stay within the 4 MiB bound of a `NODES`, and a receiver refuses a sealed `NODES` that carries deltas. A receiver that cannot apply a frame asks for its `cid` by `NODE_WANT`. A frame that does not open costs its sender no strike, and the receiver then opens no later frame from that sender in that fetch; a frame that opens to bytes that fail `cid` strikes its sender, as any node that fails its CID does.

A level-1 member that receives a level-2 form anyway drops it and counts it: a sketch `TREE_WANT` fails the CID check on `root`, tag 12 is an unknown tag, and a strict level-1 reader finds a `NODES` or a `TREE_END` with an appended field undecodable under its level-1 schema. No member strikes the sender of a level-2 form. Since v0.1.32 every content-swarm message, tags 1 to 12, and the manifest record a `MANIFEST` carries read under their annex I.1a schemas through the strict reader of §9.1, so a field the schema does not name, a field out of order, or a wrong-typed field makes the message undecodable. A receiver drops such a message, counts it (the reference counts tags 1 to 7 and a manifest record in `pieceMessagesDropped`, and every other tree message and an unknown tag in `treeMessagesDropped`), and strikes nobody. *Eager push (P2-P-01 to P2-P-04).* A ref writer MAY push a delta above the inline bound as rumours on the gossip stream `pdt:<space>:<name>`, in `PushBundle` payloads that a member that does not follow the ref relays by item id without decoding them; annex H gives the announcement and the follower's rules.

**`aspace:cap/relay-signal`** (v0.1.23) is the one-message capability of §5.4: a RELAY-role member tells an origin that a path frame to `target` was dropped at `failedHop`. The payload is the CBOR map `(target, failedHop, reason)`; a node consumes it before dispatching pipe data and never forwards it; under required attestation it is admitted on the same terms as `aspace:cap/rtc-signal`.

**`aspace:cap/asset-invoke`** (v0.1.21) is the operation verb of §6.1a: `AssetInvocation` and `AssetInvocationResult` entries in the connector's space, taken under the claim lattice, exactly once per `invocationId`.

**`aspace:cap/llm-gateway`** (v0.1.24; ISSUE-ModelCard) names a peer that routes model requests for a group: the advertisement's parameters are `tasks` (the kinds routed), `policyVersion`, and `coordination` (`lease-race`, `auction`, or `partition`). The convention of `agentspaces-model-wire`: a `ModelRequest` tagged `task=<kind>` or addressed to the gateway's advertised model name is the gateway's; one addressed to a model name a `ModelServer` advertises is unchanged.

**`aspace:cap/guardrail`** (v0.1.24; ISSUE-ModelCard) names a peer that checks content: the parameters are `checks` (the kinds offered) and `provider`. The module `agentspaces-guardrail` defines the `GuardrailProvider` SPI (`check(kind, content, context)` returning pass, refuse, redact with the replacement, or quarantine), the `GuardrailPolicy` and `GuardrailFinding` records, the built-in provider (prompt-injection patterns, personal-data classes, term and domain lists, a schema check against a fragment's field list), and `Guardrails.apply`, the one evaluation path: the policy's checks in order, a throwing or slow provider yielding the policy's `onError`.

Third parties define capabilities by minting a type URI and publishing the mini-spec; the core enumerates none of them (P6). This is the extension point for future conversational agreement protocols, for example an argumentation and negotiation capability in which proposal and counter-proposal entries flow through a space until a vote capability closes them.

## 9. Wire Protocol Summary (normative sketch)

All messages are CBOR (RFC 8949) frames, length-prefixed on the stream bindings (TCP, TLS, QUIC) and delimited by the recovery sublayer on the datagram binding (§5.5, v0.1.19); the length prefix belongs to the stream bindings, and the frame itself carries none. A frame is a signed wrapper `{ envelope, senderPublicKey, signature }` around an envelope `{ ver, group, kind, from, to, stamp, body }`, each field present and a null written as CBOR null (§9.1): `ver` is the wire version, `group` the GroupID the frame belongs to, `kind` one of the kinds below, `from` the sender's PeerID, `to` the intended recipient's PeerID or null on a genuinely unaddressed frame (the bootstrap self-introduction to a seed whose PeerID the sender does not yet know), `stamp` the sender's hybrid logical clock stamp, and `body` the kind-specific CBOR. The stamp fills the role earlier drafts gave an `epoch` field, and `to` binds an addressed frame to its recipient, so a receiver drops a frame whose `to` names another peer. The wire version is 3 (v0.1.15): every map inside a signed or hashed structure is encoded with its entries in RFC 8949 §4.2.1 order, the shorter UTF-8 key first and bytewise between equal lengths, so the canonical bytes of a structure depend on its content and not on the order its author built a map in; record components keep their declared order. A receiver applies the per-stream deltas of one `PULL_RESP` in the order it registered the streams, never in the order the wire carries them, so a revocation registry registered before a space judges the records the same pull brings. A receiver MUST drop a frame whose `ver` is anything else before verifying its signature; the drop is counted and never strikes the sender, so a future version can coexist with this one at the cost of mutual silence.

The kinds are `PING`, `PING_REQ`, `ACK` (membership probes; since v0.1.17 `PING` and `ACK` carry three appended fields, null from older nodes and ignored by them: `root`, `hashes`, and `have`, §5.2); `RUMOR` (one item on a named stream: an advertisement, an entry delta, a capability frame, or a discovery query); `DIGEST` and `PULL_RESP` (anti-entropy: per-stream digests and the deltas the partner lacked; since v0.1.17 a `DIGEST` sets exactly one of `digests`, `hashes`, or `want`, the latter two only between members advertising `wire.hashes=1`, §5.3); `QUERY` and `QUERY_HIT` (scoped, hop-budgeted discovery, with hits routed directly to the asker); `BLOCK_WANT` and `BLOCK` (content-addressed payload exchange); `PIPE_DATA` (direct capability frames, in datagram form, multiplexed by capability type URI); `RELAY_FRAME` (a complete signed frame a RELAY-role peer forwards verbatim to a peer nobody can dial, verified end to end against the origin; since v0.1.23 the body `(target, frame)` may append `path` and `hop`, omitted when absent, §5.4); `CHANNEL_HELLO` (the per-connection channel-authentication announcement of §5.6, added in v0.1.6); added in v0.1.17 and each sent only to a peer whose advertisement carries the hint that names it, so an older reader never receives one, `BATCH` (several of one sender's frames to one receiver in one signed envelope, the body a list of encoded inner envelopes dispatched in order; hint `wire.batch=1`), `HAVE`, `GRAFT`, and `PRUNE` (lazy push, §5.3; hint `wire.tree=1`), and `SESSION_HELLO` (session attestation, §5.6; hint `wire.session=1`); and, added in v0.1.10, `GROUP_AD_WANT` and `GROUP_AD`. A `GROUP_AD_WANT` names the wanted group in the envelope's `group` and carries an empty body; a receiver serves it to non-members exactly as it serves a `PING`, under the bootstrap rate budget. A `GROUP_AD` answers with the signed founding advertisement (§5.1) the answering member itself joined with; a member serves only advertisements that are self-certifying founding documents, and the requester re-derives the GroupID and checks the founder's signature before it acts on one, ignoring every answer that fails. Earlier drafts also listed `DIGEST_DIFF`, `PULL`, `PIPE_OPEN`, and `PIPE_CLOSE`; the `DIGEST`/`PULL_RESP` pair and bare `PIPE_DATA` realize them and the four names are retired. The hint `wire.recordless=1` (v0.1.29) gates no kind: it says the advertiser accepts an entry state delta whose `record` is null (§7.3), and a writer omits the record only when every member of its view carries the hint. The hint `wire.appcard=2` (v0.1.32; AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09) gates no kind either: it is a level, read as canonical decimal text, and level 2 says the advertiser decodes the v0.1.32 schemas of the `AppCard` and the `AgentCard` with their value rules. An issuer writes `origin`, `formats`, a `host` whose `signOn` is `idp`, and `spill` only while every member of its view advertises level 2 or above (§6.1a.2, §6.1a.5).

The wrapper's sender key and signature are optional as of v0.1.6: both present is a signed frame, both null is a channel-attested frame acceptable only on a connection attested for the sender (§5.6), and any other combination is dropped. Hybrid logical clocks timestamp all items. Receivers reject envelopes whose signature fails verification or whose group the receiver does not belong to, and they deduplicate gossip items by stream and payload hash (§5.3). The QUIC (RFC 9000) binding ships in v0.1.4, carrying these envelopes over one bidirectional stream per peer connection; a refinement mapping capability pipes to QUIC streams one-to-one remains future work. The protocol thus rests on standards-based interfaces and formats throughout: CBOR encoding, Ed25519 signatures, and URI naming, with nothing Java-specific on the wire.

*The hybrid logical clock (v0.1.30, normative; HLC logical counter, 2026-11-08 (Rust team report)).* A stamp is `(physical, logical, node)`: epoch milliseconds, a logical counter that orders the events of one millisecond, and the issuing node's id, ordered by physical, then logical, then node, and written on the wire as the CBOR text string `physical:logical:node`. Every implementation compares node parts, and the holders a take claim's tie-break compares (§7.4), as sequences of UTF-16 code units, the order Java's `String.compareTo` gives; a comparison by Unicode code point differs from it once a character above U+FFFF meets one from U+E000 to U+FFFF (HLC canonical stamps, 2026-11-08). A node validates its own node part, the peer or agent id it stamps with, where it builds or first uses a clock, and it refuses a node that breaks the node rule of the canonical stamp below, so it never emits a stamp that a receiver refuses for its node (HLC round four, 2026-11-08). The place differs per implementation (HLC round five, 2026-11-08): Java validates the node in the `HybridLogicalClock` constructor; the Python, TypeScript, and Ruby clients keep one clock state per node and validate the node in `hlc_now` (Python, Ruby) and `hlcNow` (TypeScript), before every stamp they issue for it, the first one included; and Ruby's `Identity#agent` also refuses such a local name when it builds the agent id. A node issues a local stamp at `physical = max(wall, last physical)`, with the logical counter one above the last when the physical part did not advance and 0 when it did. A node merges a remote stamp by clamping the remote physical part to its own wall time plus the drift ceiling (`MAX_DRIFT`, ten minutes, §11) minus one millisecond, taking the largest of its wall time, its last physical part, and the clamped remote physical part, and advancing the logical counter of whichever of the last and the remote stamps hold that maximum (the larger counter when both do), or starting at 0 when wall time alone holds it. These rules bound the clock, with `LOGICAL_LIMIT` = 65536 (2^16):

- *The canonical stamp (HLC canonical stamps, 2026-11-08).* A stamp is the text `<physical>:<logical>:<node>`, and a remote stamp is acceptable only when its text is canonical. The physical and logical parts are each `0` or a non-zero ASCII digit followed by ASCII digits, so neither carries a leading zero, a sign, a fraction, an exponent, whitespace, or a non-ASCII digit. The physical part fits a signed 64-bit integer, at most 9223372036854775807. The logical part runs from 0 to `LOGICAL_LIMIT` inclusive, and a stamp at exactly `LOGICAL_LIMIT` is acceptable. The node part is non-empty and holds no `:`, no control character (U+0000 to U+001F, U+007F), and no lone surrogate (U+D800 to U+DFFF outside a surrogate pair). The whole text holds at most 3996 UTF-16 code units (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08). No UTF-8 text encodes a lone surrogate, so a received stamp never holds one, and the last rule binds the local node an implementation that holds text as UTF-16 code units could otherwise build (HLC round five, 2026-11-08). Every implementation MUST refuse any other text wherever it decodes, merges, orders, or deduplicates a stamp: the replay filter, the claim comparison, lease stamps, folded states, the `signedAt` of an agent-signed state (§7.3, §11a.4), the persisted snapshots it restores, and the claims the ordered log commits. A canonical stamp has exactly one text, so the bytes a signer signed always equal the re-encoding of the stamp a receiver decodes from them, and every implementation verifies a signature over the same bytes. Every implementation decodes a counter above `LOGICAL_LIMIT` that is otherwise canonical and fits a signed 32-bit integer, verifies the signature that covers it, and refuses it then, so the refusal names the signer and counts as a logical refusal in every implementation alike (HLC round four, 2026-11-08); a counter above 2147483647 fails the grammar's range and makes its carrier undecodable.
- *The stamp item (HLC round four, 2026-11-08; HLC round five, 2026-11-08).* On the wire a stamp is one CBOR item: a text string (major type 3) of definite length under its shortest length header, with no tag, whose bytes are valid UTF-8 in shortest form, with no overlong sequence and no surrogate code point encoded in UTF-8, and whose text is a canonical stamp. Any other item in a stamp's place is not a stamp: the same text under a longer length header than its length needs (`78 05 35 3A 30 3A 6E` for `5:0:n`, or a two-, four-, or eight-byte length), an indefinite-length text string, a tagged text string, a text string whose bytes are overlong or otherwise invalid UTF-8, a byte string, an integer, a float, a map, an array, or a simple value. A receiver MUST treat a carrier that holds such an item where a stamp belongs as undecodable, drop it before any signature check, count it as undecodable, and strike nobody, because no verified signature names a signer. A decoder that repairs its input silently, by unwrapping a tag, joining the chunks of an indefinite-length string, decoding an overlong sequence, reading a longer length header as the shortest, or replacing invalid bytes with U+FFFD, MUST judge the raw item instead of the text it returns. Java's Jackson CBOR parser unwraps tags, joins chunks, decodes overlong UTF-8, and reads any length header without a trace, so Java re-encodes each decoded stamp text as the shortest-form item and requires the bytes the item occupies in the input to equal it. Since v0.1.31 the Python, TypeScript, and Ruby decoders refuse every head longer than its argument needs and every indefinite-length text of 3996 UTF-16 code units or fewer (§9.1, S3 and S6), so no such item reaches their stamp parsers (HLC round six, 2026-11-08). A longer text travels only in Jackson's chunks, which no stamp item may use, so every implementation refuses it where a stamp belongs, and a `pdt.issued` that holds one falls back as any other value that is no stamp does (R1; HLC round seven, 2026-11-08). Each clock refuses a node past 3965 UTF-16 code units, which is 3996 less the longest physical part (19 digits), the longest logical part (10 digits), and the two colons, so every stamp it issues fits in one definite text: Java in the `HybridLogicalClock` constructor (`HlcTimestamp.MAX_NODE_UNITS`), Python and Ruby in `hlc_now`, and TypeScript in `hlcNow`.
- *CBOR outside a stamp (HLC round five, 2026-11-08; HLC round six, 2026-11-08).* Every implementation decodes the rest of a frame or a space delta under the wire encoding of §9.1, so a hostile item reaches all of them or none. An indefinite-length text string stands only for a text past 3996 UTF-16 code units, in Jackson's exact chunks, and every implementation writes a text that long the same way, so a signature over a long string verifies wherever it verifies in Java. Every text string, map keys and chunks included, MUST be well-formed shortest-form UTF-8, with no overlong sequence and no encoded surrogate. Tags 2, 3, and 4 stand only in application values, in the forms rule S9 fixes: a bignum only for an integer outside the `long` range, read as Jackson reads it, and a decimal normalized with its exponent within -(2^31-1) to 2^31-1. No tag stands in a protocol field or in a map key's place. The protocol defines one other tag, 42, the link of a persistent-tree node (annex H); it appears only inside a node's own bytes, which travel as a byte string and decode through the node codec, so the frame and delta decoders never meet it. A carrier holding a refused item is undecodable, with the drop and the count of a stamp that is not a stamp item, and it strikes nobody. Java's `CborCodec.fromWire` walks the raw bytes (`WellFormedCbor.checkCanonical`) before Jackson reads them, because Jackson drops tags and decodes overlong UTF-8 without a trace, and then compares the decoded record's encoding with the bytes received.
- *The advisory head stamp (HLC round four, 2026-11-08; HLC round five, 2026-11-08; HLC round six, 2026-11-08).* The `pdt.issued` and `pdt.issuer` attributes of a ref (annex H) are advisory payload that only orders heads, so a reader never refuses, counts, or strikes anything for their values and never drops a head for them. A reader orders a head by `pdt.issued` only when that attribute is a text string holding a canonical stamp with a counter of at most `LOGICAL_LIMIT` and a physical part at most the reader's `wall + MAX_DRIFT + SKEW_MARGIN` (R2), and `pdt.issuer` is either absent or a text string that parses as an agent id under R5 (`<peerId>/<localName>`, with a non-empty peer id before the first `/` and a non-empty local name holding no `/` and no `:`); it then pairs `pdt.issued` with `pdt.issuer`, or with the entry's own issuer when `pdt.issuer` is absent. In every other case the reader orders the head by the entry's own `issued` stamp and the entry's own issuer together: an absent or null `pdt.issued`; a `pdt.issued` that is not text, falls outside the grammar, exceeds the limit, or lies beyond that bound; and a `pdt.issuer` that is present but null, not text, or no agent id. A malformed `pdt.issuer` therefore never pairs with an accepted `pdt.issued`. Every other attribute holds text, and a null or non-text value under any other key makes the `PRef` undecodable, so the reader ignores the head and counts it in `headsIgnored` (R1, R3).
- *The refusal.* A node MUST refuse a remote stamp that is not acceptable at every point where the stamp would reach its clock, its order, or the judgement of a certificate: the envelope stamp of a frame (a member's frame and a newcomer's self-introduction alike), the `issued`, `leaseStamp`, and `signedAt` stamps of a folded state (§7.3), the stamp of a gossiped take claim and of a claim the ordered log commits (§7.4), and the stamps of a persisted snapshot it restores. A refused stamp never reaches the clock. The node does not merge the frame, the state, or the claim that carries it, counts the refusal, and strikes the signer where it keeps strikes. A node counts and strikes a refused state or claim after the signatures over it verify, once per distinct signed content (§7.3), however often and by whatever path partners offer it. Java peers keep strikes; the Python, TypeScript, and Ruby clients keep none and record the refusal in a count alone. A stamp outside the canonical grammar, like any item that is not a stamp, makes its carrier undecodable in every implementation: the node drops the carrier before a verified signature can name a signer, counts it as undecodable, and strikes nobody (TECH-SPEC §1.4). A frame whose stamp is not a stamp item therefore drops at decode, before any signature check (HLC round five, 2026-11-08). Java holds one exception to striking nobody: when the inner frame of a verified `BATCH` does not decode, Java strikes the outer signer with `malformed frame in BATCH`, because the outer signature covers the inner bytes. No client handles `BATCH`, which travels only to a peer that advertises `wire.batch=1`, and a client that adds it strikes the outer signer the same way where it keeps strikes (HLC round five, 2026-11-08).
- *The counters (HLC round four, 2026-11-08; HLC round five, 2026-11-08).* Every implementation reports the same seven stamp outcomes under Java's names, written in its own language's case convention: `framesLogicalRefused`, frames refused for a counter above `LOGICAL_LIMIT`; `framesFarFuture`, frames past the far-future ceiling by more than `SKEW_MARGIN`; `framesFarFutureSkewed`, frames past the ceiling by at most `SKEW_MARGIN`; `stampsLogicalRefused`, distinct states and claims refused after verification for a counter above the limit; `stampsFarFutureRefused`, distinct states and claims refused after verification as hostile far-future (§7.3); `stampsFarFutureDeferred`, distinct states and claims deferred (§7.3); and `deltasUndecodable`, space deltas that do not decode, a stamp outside the grammar or an item that is not a stamp included. A hostile far-future refusal counts in `stampsFarFutureRefused` alone and never in `stampsLogicalRefused`, and a deferral counts once per distinct key. An operator therefore reads one meaning per counter across a mixed fleet.
- *The drift cap.* A merge MUST clamp the remote physical part to `wall + MAX_DRIFT - 1`, one millisecond inside the ceiling. A carry the merge triggers at the clamp therefore lands at most at `wall + MAX_DRIFT`. The ceiling holds for up to 65,536 clock events (local stamps and merges alike) per millisecond of wall time: a clock a hostile stamp dragged to the ceiling uses the counter from 0 to `LOGICAL_LIMIT` there, and each further event in the same millisecond carries one millisecond past it.
- *The carry.* A node MUST NOT saturate the counter. When an increment, local or in a merge, would take the logical counter past `LOGICAL_LIMIT`, the node advances the physical part by one millisecond and resets the counter to 0. A conforming clock therefore never returns the same stamp twice and never issues a counter above `LOGICAL_LIMIT`, whatever it merges.

*The frame filters (v0.1.30, normative; HLC logical counter, 2026-11-08 (Rust team report)).* A receiver judges a frame's stamp in a fixed order, the same in every implementation (Java's `PeerNode` and each client's frame filter), and it measures both time checks against its own wall time. It parses the stamp before any of them, so a stamp that does not parse never reaches them: the receiver drops the frame as the refusal rule above says. First, it drops a stale frame, one whose stamp's physical part trails the receiver's wall time by more than the frame-age window (five minutes in the reference implementation), and an exact replay, a repeat of a `(from, stamp)` pair it holds among its recent frames; it counts both drops (`framesStale`, `framesReplayed`) and strikes for neither, because anyone can replay a frame its author signed. Every later check judges a fresh, signed frame that only its sender could have produced, so a refusal there is attributable. A receiver that keeps strikes holds a sender that reaches its strike threshold in local quarantine (§5.2). For a member's frame it applies the stamp bounds after admission and before the clock merge. A stamp whose physical part is strictly greater than the receiver's wall time plus `MAX_DRIFT` (ASF-020) drops the frame. *The skew margin (HLC canonical stamps, 2026-11-08).* With `SKEW_MARGIN` = 5 seconds, a receiver counts a frame whose physical part lies past `wall + MAX_DRIFT` by at most `SKEW_MARGIN` in `framesFarFutureSkewed` and strikes nobody; it counts a frame further past the ceiling in `framesFarFuture` and, where it keeps strikes, strikes the sender with the reason `far-future stamp`. A stamp that is not acceptable drops the frame, counts the refusal in `framesLogicalRefused`, and strikes the sender with the reason `logical counter above 65536`. A newcomer's self-introduction meets the acceptable-stamp check before the receiver admits the newcomer, with the same drop, count, and strike, so a hostile introduction admits nobody, even in an `OPEN` group. A conforming peer's frames never fail the acceptable-stamp check, since its clock carries. The drift clamp and the skew margin together keep an honest peer out of quarantine: a hostile member can drag the honest peer's clock to its wall time plus `MAX_DRIFT`, and a receiver whose wall clock trails the sender's by up to `SKEW_MARGIN` drops and counts the honest peer's frames that land past its own ceiling and strikes nobody. Strikes never decay, so without the margin a receiver running a few milliseconds behind would strike such a peer on every frame until it quarantined the peer.

### 9.1 The wire encoding (v0.1.31, normative; HLC round six, 2026-11-08; HLC round seven, 2026-11-08)

Every carrier a receiver decodes from the network is one CBOR document in the wire encoding this section defines: a frame, and each document a frame nests as a byte string that the receiver decodes (an envelope body, a space delta, an `adBytes`, an inner `BATCH` envelope, a capability payload, a `PRef`, an entry payload). The encoding is the image of Java's `CborCodec` encoder (Jackson 2.21.7 as the codec configures it) after four encoder changes that make the bytes a function of the value. Java writes a `BigInteger` inside the `long` range as a plain integer (JC-1), a `BigDecimal` stripped of its trailing zeros with its mantissa under its shortest head (JC-2), only the NaN of `Double.NaN` and `Float.NaN` (JC-3), and every `Set` with its elements in the order of their encodings (JC-4). Every value therefore has exactly one encoding. A receiver decodes a carrier, encodes what it decoded, and refuses the carrier unless the two byte sequences are equal (owner ruling F3), so a signature always covers exactly the bytes that arrived, and no relay can relabel a signed carrier into a form one implementation accepts and another refuses.

*Terms.* An *item* is one CBOR data item (RFC 8949 §1.2), and a *document* is a byte sequence that holds exactly one item. A *protocol record* is a record the protocol defines; annex I lists every one a carrier reaches, with its fields in wire order, as Java's own serializers generated them. An *application value* is a value an application chooses: an entry payload, an embedded item of a persistent-tree node (annex H), and the value of a `pdt.issued` or `pdt.issuer` attribute (§9.2, R1). A *carrier* is a document a receiver decodes from the network.

*Items (rules S1 to S12).* Every document obeys these rules, and a document that breaks one is undecodable.

- *S1, one item.* A document holds exactly one item. A byte after the item, a second item, or a truncated item makes it undecodable.
- *S2, nesting.* A document nests at most 64 arrays and maps, and a receiver refuses the 65th level (ASF-018). The two-element array inside tag 4 counts as a level, because Jackson counts it against its nesting limit, and an empty innermost container counts like any other.
- *S3, shortest heads.* Every head (an integer's value, a string's length, an array's length, a tag number) uses the shortest form RFC 8949 §4.2.1 allows: the argument in the initial byte below 24, else 1, 2, 4 or 8 bytes, the fewest that hold it.
- *S4, integers.* An integer from -2^63 to 2^63-1 (a Java `long`) is major type 0 or 1 under its shortest head. An integer outside that range is a bignum (S9), so a receiver refuses major type 0 or 1 with an argument above 2^63-1.
- *S5, byte strings.* Definite length only.
- *S6, text.* Every text string, map keys and chunks included, is well-formed shortest-form UTF-8 (RFC 3629) with no encoded surrogate. A text of at most 3996 UTF-16 code units is one definite text string. A longer text is Jackson's chunked form exactly: `7F`, then chunks of 3996 units each (3995 when the chunk's last unit would be a high surrogate, so a surrogate pair never splits), then the remaining 1 to 3996 units, then `FF`, each chunk a definite text string under its shortest head. A receiver refuses any other chunking, an indefinite text of 3996 units or fewer, and a definite text past 3996 units. No encoder writes a lone surrogate.
- *S7, arrays.* Definite length only.
- *S8, maps.* Indefinite length only (`BF`, the key and value pairs, `FF`; the empty map is `BF FF`). Every key is an untagged text string, and no key occurs twice in one map. A receiver refuses a definite-length map, a duplicate key, and an integer, byte-string, or other non-text key.
- *S9, tags.* Only tags 2, 3 and 4, in these forms (owner ruling F2). Tag 2 (positive bignum) and tag 3 (negative bignum) stand only for an integer outside the `long` range, over a definite byte string that holds `BigInteger.toByteArray()` of the magnitude: big-endian minimal two's complement, so the first byte lies below `80` and a leading `00` appears only before a byte of `80` or above. Tag 2 reads as the magnitude and tag 3 as the magnitude negated (Jackson's reading, which departs from RFC 8949 §3.4.3), so 2^63 is `C2 49 00 80 00 00 00 00 00 00 00` and -2^63-1 is `C3 49 00 80 00 00 00 00 00 00 01`. A bignum's byte string holds at most 1000 bytes, a leading `00` included, alone or as a decimal's mantissa, because Jackson's `StreamReadConstraints` read no longer number; a receiver refuses a longer one, and every encoder refuses to write one (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08). Tag 4 (decimal fraction) is `C4 82`, an exponent, and a mantissa, for mantissa × 10^exponent: the exponent is a plain integer from -(2^31-1) to 2^31-1, the mantissa is a plain integer inside the `long` range or else a bignum as above, and the decimal is normalized, with zero as `C4 82 00 00` and a nonzero mantissa never divisible by 10. So 1.5 and 1.50 are both `C4 82 20 0F`, and -12.5 is `C4 82 20 38 7C`. A receiver refuses every other tag, tags 0, 1 and 55799 included. Tag 42 appears only inside a persistent-tree node (annex H), no tag stands in a map key's place, and no tag stands in a protocol field (P3).
- *S10, floats.* A float64 (`FB`) or a float32 (`FA`); a receiver refuses a half float (`F9`). Java writes a `double` as float64 and a `float` as float32 and never shortens either. Infinities and -0.0 keep their IEEE bits. The only NaNs are `FB 7F F8 00 00 00 00 00 00` and `FA 7F C0 00 00`, the bits of Java's NaN constants, and a receiver refuses every other NaN bit pattern.
- *S11, simple values.* `F4` (false), `F5` (true) and `F6` (null) only.
- *S12, break.* `FF` appears only where it ends an indefinite map or an indefinite text string.

*Protocol records (rules P1 to P4).*

- *P1, fields.* A record is a map (S8) whose keys are exactly the fields its schema names, in the schema's declared order. A field is either *always* present, with a null written `F6` (as `Envelope.to` on an unaddressed frame, and the wrapper's `senderPublicKey` and `signature` on a channel-attested frame), or *omitted when null* (`?null` in annex I, as `Ping.root`, `EntryStateDto.signer`, `PRef.attrs`), or, for `AppCard.Ui.components` alone, *omitted when null or empty*. A missing always-present field, an `F6` for a field omitted when null, a field the schema does not name, and fields out of order each make the carrier undecodable. A receiver therefore refuses a record that carries a field it does not know, so a new field needs a new wire version or a hint the receiver advertises, as `wire.recordless=1` gates a null `record` (§7.3).
- *P2, kinds.* Each field holds the kind its schema names and no other:

  | Schema kind | Item |
  |---|---|
  | `int`, `Integer` | a plain integer from -2^31 to 2^31-1 |
  | `long`, `Long` | a plain integer (S4) |
  | `double` | a float64 (`FB`), an integral value included (`3.0` is `FB 40 08 ...`), a NaN only in S10's one form. `TakeClaim.bid` is finite, because Java's `TakeClaim` refuses any other bid, and every implementation orders bids as Java's `Double.compare` does, so -0.0 lies below 0.0; the pipe records of annex I take any float64, as `PushSumAggregate.Extremum.value` is NaN until its node holds a value (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08) |
  | `boolean` | `F4` or `F5` |
  | `String` | text (S6) |
  | `bytes` | a byte string (S5) |
  | enum | text holding a constant's exact name |
  | `PeerId`, `GroupId`, `SpaceId`, `EntryId` | non-empty text |
  | `AgentId` | text `<peerId>/<localName>`, both parts non-empty, the local name holding no `/` and no `:` (R5) |
  | `HlcTimestamp` | the stamp item of §9 holding a canonical stamp |
  | `Instant` | text exactly as Java's `DateTimeFormatter.ISO_INSTANT` prints it: `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SS`, then `.` and 3, 6 or 9 digits when the nanoseconds are not zero (the fewest that hold them), then `Z`, in the years 0000 to 9999 |
  | `Duration` | text exactly as Java's `Duration.toString()` prints a non-negative duration: `PT0S` for zero, else `PT` with `<h>H` when the hours are not zero, `<m>M` when the minutes are not zero, and `<s>S` or `<s>.<f>S` when the seconds or nanoseconds are not zero, `<f>` holding 1 to 9 digits with no trailing zero |
  | `list<X>` | a definite array of X in the writer's order |
  | `set<X>` | a definite array of distinct X, ordered by the encoded bytes of the elements, the shorter encoding first, then bytewise (`roles` is `[RELAY, RENDEZVOUS]`) |
  | `map<String, X>` | an indefinite map of text keys in the RFC 8949 §4.2.1 order of wire version 3, the shorter UTF-8 key first, then bytewise; its values of kind X, never `F6` |
  | a record | a nested map under P1 |

  A receiver checks that the text of a typed text field is exactly the text the encoder writes for the value it parsed, so an `Instant` or `Duration` that Java would print differently, or a stamp that is not canonical, makes the carrier undecodable.
- *P3, no tags and no coercion (owner ruling F1).* No protocol field holds a tag, and no field accepts an item of another kind: an epoch, nonce, counter, or `ver` as a float, a decimal, a bignum, or text; a bid as an integer, a float32, text, or null; a byte string where text belongs; an integer where text or a boolean belongs; base64 text where bytes belong; epoch seconds where an `Instant` belongs. Each makes the carrier undecodable in every implementation.
- *P4, primitives.* A field of a primitive Java kind (`int`, `long`, `double`, `boolean`) is never `F6` and never absent.

Java reads every protocol record it receives from the network, except the content swarm's messages (§9.3), through `CborCodec.fromWire`, which walks the item rules over the raw bytes (`WellFormedCbor.checkCanonical`), maps the record with every Jackson coercion turned off (`ACCEPT_FLOAT_AS_INT` and `ALLOW_COERCION_OF_SCALARS` off, a `CoercionConfig` that fails every scalar shape into another logical type and every empty value, `FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES`, `FAIL_ON_NUMBERS_FOR_ENUMS`, `FAIL_ON_NULL_FOR_PRIMITIVES` and `FAIL_ON_TRAILING_TOKENS` on, text, byte-string, `Instant` and `Duration` deserializers that take their own item alone, and a null refused as a map value and, since HLC round seven (2026-11-08), as a list or set element), and compares the record's encoding with the bytes received. A protocol record that travels as an entry payload carries `@WireRecord` and reads the same way (the vote proposal and ballot, and `PRef`). A capability's pipe payload (`PipeFrame.payload`) holds a protocol record too, under the schemas annex I.1 lists last, and every implementation that reads one applies P1 to P4 and the canonical-bytes check to it (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08). Application values and what a node persisted keep their own reads, described below.

*Application values (owner ruling F2).* An application value obeys S1 to S12 alone. The order of a map's keys is part of the value: a map keeps the order its writer wrote it in, and a reader that hands a map to an application and later writes it unchanged keeps the arrival order. Java writes a `Map` sorted (wire version 3), a record in declared order, and a set in element order. The types each implementation decodes into hold the value exactly, so every decoded value re-encodes to the bytes it came from:

| Item | Java | Python | TypeScript | Ruby |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| plain integer | `Integer`, `Long` (a `BigInteger` in range writes as one) | `int` | `number` when it is a safe integer, else `bigint` | `Integer` |
| bignum (tag 2 or 3) | `BigInteger` | `int` | `bigint` | `Integer` |
| decimal (tag 4) | `BigDecimal`, written stripped | `decimal.Decimal`, whose trailing zeros the encoder strips exactly | `CborDecimal` (an exact `exponent` and `mantissa`, normalized when built) | `BigDecimal` |
| float64 | `double` | `float` | `CborDouble` (a non-integral `number` also encodes as float64) | `Float` |
| float32 | `float` | `cbor.Float32` | `CborFloat32` | `AgentSpaces::CBOR::Float32` |
| map | `LinkedHashMap` read, `Map` written sorted | `dict` | a plain object that remembers the order its keys arrived in; a `Map` with text keys also writes, in insertion order | `Hash` |

Each encoder refuses what the encoding cannot hold: a non-text map key, a NaN or infinite decimal, a decimal exponent outside -(2^31-1) to 2^31-1, and a lone surrogate; Ruby also refuses a `Hash` whose Symbol and String keys name the same text. A JavaScript object puts integer-like keys first whatever order a program set them in, so a TypeScript application that writes such keys in an order of its own passes a `Map`, which `cbor.dumps` writes in insertion order (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08), and `cbor.canonicalMap` marks a map the encoder writes in the RFC 8949 order. Java reads an entry payload through `CborCodec.fromDocument`, which checks the item rules and maps leniently, unknown fields ignored. A payload outside the encoding leaves its entry replicated, since the record signature covers the payload's bytes and every replica holds the same entry, and every read skips its value; `Space.undecodableEntries` names such entries to a reader that counts them (R3). What a node persisted before v0.1.31 reads through the lenient `CborCodec.fromBytes`, so a store written under 0.5.0 still opens.

*Persistent-tree nodes.* A node keeps its own layer (annex H): a definite map in DAG-CBOR order, shortest integers, and links as tag 42. Each embedded item, an application key or value, obeys S1 to S12 over its own bytes, tags 2, 3 and 4 included, and a decoder refuses a node whose embedded item breaks them. A bare link item in a value slot stays the one exception (a spilled value), and a decoder refuses a link item in a key slot.

*The receiver's procedure.* A receiver judges each carrier in these steps and stops at the first failure.

1. *Items.* It checks the raw bytes against S1 to S12.
2. *Schema.* It decodes the protocol record under P1 to P4. Each opaque byte-string field that holds a nested document decodes when the receiver needs it, through the same steps.
3. *Canonical bytes.* It encodes the decoded record and compares the result with the bytes it received.
4. *Version.* It drops a frame whose `ver` is not 3, as §9 says.
5. *Signatures*, then revocation, then the stamp checks (§7.3, §7.4), unchanged.

A failure in steps 1 to 4 makes the carrier undecodable: the receiver drops it, counts it where it counts an undecodable carrier of its kind (`deltasUndecodable` for a space delta; Java's `framesDropped` and the clients' malformed-frame count for a frame or a body; `headsIgnored` for a `PRef`; an advertisement or revocation goes unheld), and strikes nobody, since no verified signature names a signer. Each of the four steps drops with the same count and no strike, so their order changes no verdict; Java checks `ver` once the frame has decoded. The one exception to striking nobody stays the `BATCH` rule of §9 (E15).

*The signed bytes.* Once a carrier passes step 3, the bytes of every sub-item equal the encoding of its decoded value, so a signature check may use either. A receiver verifies over raw received bytes where the signer signed an opaque byte string: `StoredAd.adBytes`, `SignedPeerAd.adBytes`, `SignedRevocation.adBytes`, the envelope `body` and the `BATCH` inner envelopes inside the envelope signature, the inner frame of `RELAY_FRAME`, and a record's `payload` inside its signed view. It verifies over a sub-item of the carrier for the envelope of a frame (its signature and its session MAC) and the claim of a `SignedClaim` or a `ClaimCommand`. It re-encodes a projection from decoded fields for the views that never travel as such: `SignView`, `StateSignView`, `FoundingFields` and `FoundingDocument`, the unsigned `AgentCertificate`, `CredentialView`, `DelegationView`, and `EpochCommitment`; their typed text fields (`issued`, `ttl`) are the decoded text, which step 2 proved canonical. Every implementation sorts the two sorted fields of these views as Java does: `StateSignView.adds` and `.removes` by replica in UTF-16 code unit order (`String.compareTo`), then by counter, and `SignView.tags` by the RFC 8949 order of P2.

*The beacon document (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08).* The beacon document of §10.1 is JSON (RFC 8259) and lies outside the CBOR encoding, and owner ruling F3 reaches it in the same shape. Its canonical JSON is what Java's `BeaconDocument.toJson` writes: the members `v`, `issuer`, `issuerKey`, `issued`, `ttl`, `groups`, `signature` in that order, a group's members `name`, `id`, `founding`, `seeds`, `wire`, `multicast`, `parameters`, `invite` in that order, and a seed's `transport`, `address`, `priority`; a null member of the document or of a group omitted, and a null seed member or map value written `null`; the keys of `wire` and `parameters` sorted in UTF-16 code unit order (`String.compareTo`); no whitespace; integers in decimal; and strings escaped as Jackson escapes them, `\"` and `\\`, then `\b`, `\t`, `\n`, `\f` and `\r`, then every other character below U+0020 as `\u00XX` in upper-case hex, and every other character, DEL included, as itself. The signature covers the document without its `signature` member, written the same way except that each character past U+FFFF appears as two upper-case `\uXXXX` escapes of its UTF-16 surrogates, as Jackson's byte writer writes it (`canonicalBytes`); the served document holds such a character as UTF-8. A reader refuses a document that carries a `signature` unless the bytes it received equal the canonical JSON of the document it parsed, and unless `signature` and `issuerKey` are each the padded standard base64 (RFC 4648 §4) of the bytes they decode to. Whitespace, a byte order mark, a trailing newline, another member order, an unknown or duplicate member, another escape, a number sent as text or as a float, and another base64 text of the same signature therefore each refuse a signed document instead of relabelling it. Each field holds Java's type: `v` and a seed's `priority` within a 32-bit `int`, each `wire` value null or within one, `ttl` within a 64-bit `long` (TypeScript reads it exactly, as a `bigint` past 2^53), `groups` and `seeds` as arrays, `wire` and `parameters` as objects, and every other member as text or null, so a reader refuses a document that holds anything else, signed or not since v0.1.32 (§9.3), and a number written with a fraction or an exponent fills no integer member. A document without a signature still reads leniently in form, with whitespace, any member order, and unknown members ignored, while each member it names holds its type as above, and its groups still meet the checks of Java's `Group` record: each group has a name, no seed is null, and no parameter carries a forbidden name. Java's `BeaconDocument.parse`, Python's `beacon.parse_document`, TypeScript's `parseBeaconDocument`, and Ruby's `Beacon.parse_document` apply these rules. The encoding keeps Java's form under the reference rule, and it does not adopt RFC 8785 (JCS), which orders members by name, would change every signed byte, and would break the Rust peer's beacon reader, which pins Java's form.

*What the encoding ends.* Before v0.1.31 Java verified a frame over its decoded envelope re-encoded in record order, so a relay could relabel a signed frame in ways Java accepted and the clients refused, and Jackson coerced a float, a decimal, or text into an integer field. The clients verified the envelope in arrival order with extra fields kept. The encoding closes those forms and the rest of the list owner ruling F3 and R12 name: a longer head, an integer sent as a decimal or as text, duplicate map keys, unassigned simple values and undefined, half floats, indefinite-length byte strings, bytes after the first item, integer map keys, and Jackson's coercions. It also ends forms honest builds wrote before v0.1.31, each of which every implementation now writes canonically: the clients' unsorted `Digest.digests` map, TypeScript's unsorted `PullResp.deltas`, `SyncDelta.claims`, `EntryRecord.tags` and `PeerAdvertisement.resourceHints`, Java's per-JVM order of `PeerAdvertisement.roles`, Java's small bignums, unnormalized decimals and raw NaN bits, and the clients' plain integers from 2^63 to 2^64-1. Bad Monkey has published nothing at 0.5.0, so no transition window applies.

### 9.2 The owner's rulings of HLC round six (v0.1.31, normative; HLC round six, 2026-11-08)

The fifth review round left a claim number that arrives in another CBOR form than its holder signed, and an indefinite-length byte string, to the owner. The owner's rulings of 2026-11-08 settle both and every split the round-six probe found, and Java's behaviour decides any point no ruling covers, unless it breaks F1 or F3.

- *F1, wrong-typed protocol fields.* A protocol record whose field arrives with the wrong CBOR type (an epoch as a float, a decimal, or text; a bid as text or null; a byte string where text belongs; an integer where text belongs) is undecodable in every implementation and counts where its carrier counts an undecodable carrier (P3). Java turns Jackson's coercions off for protocol records alone and keeps the lenient mapping of application values.
- *F2, the big-number tags.* Tags 2, 3 and 4 stand in application values everywhere, in the forms S9 fixes, and each implementation decodes them into the exact types of the table above and hands them to applications. No protocol field holds them, no map key holds any tag, and tag 42 stands only inside a persistent-tree node, whose embedded items admit tags 2, 3 and 4 alone (Java's `NodeCodec` checks each one).
- *F3, canonical bytes.* Every implementation verifies a signature over the bytes it received, and refuses a carrier (undecodable, striking nobody) unless encoding what it decoded yields exactly those bytes (§9.1).
- *F4, an agent's `signedAt`.* The far-future rule of §7.3 judges the `signedAt` of an agent-signed state as it judges `issued` and `leaseStamp`: the state merges up to `wall + MAX_DRIFT + SKEW_MARGIN`, waits as deferred for up to one hour past that, and is hostile beyond, refused, struck where the implementation strikes, acknowledged where it acknowledges, and counted in `stampsFarFutureRefused` or `stampsFarFutureDeferred`. The certificate check bounds `signedAt` by the certificate's validity window alone (§4.2).
- *R1, `PRef.attrs`.* `attrs` is a map of text to text. A value under `pdt.issued` or `pdt.issuer` that is null or not text makes the head fall back to the entry's own stamp and issuer together (E10). A null or non-text value under any other key makes the `PRef` undecodable: every reader ignores the head and counts it in `headsIgnored`. The 4 KiB bound counts the UTF-8 bytes of the keys and of the text values, so a non-text value under `pdt.issued` or `pdt.issuer` counts nothing toward it. No reader reads a byte string as base64 text.
- *R2, a head stamped ahead.* A `pdt.issued` whose physical part lies beyond the reader's `wall + MAX_DRIFT + SKEW_MARGIN` is not acceptable, and the head falls back to the entry's own pair, so no writer can win every conflict by stamping ahead.
- *R3, `headsIgnored`.* Every reader counts in `headsIgnored` exactly what Java's `Refs` counts: a head that breaks a bound, names another kind, or names another ref than its tag, an entry whose `PRef` does not decode, and a head whose root does not load within the load timeout, each once per entry while the entry stays live.
- *R4, the base walk.* Every reader walks `base` links exactly as Java's `Refs.walk` does (annex H): every version a walk reaches at its 64th step has no base, a revert cycle within the 64 steps counts as reached, and a diamond at the limit resolves as in Java.
- *R5, agent ids.* An agent id, wherever it appears (a record's `issuer`, a claim's `holder`, a lease's `holder`, `pdt.issuer`, a certificate's `agent`), follows Java's `AgentId` grammar: a non-empty peer id, `/`, and a non-empty local name with no `/` and no `:`. Every implementation refuses such a local name where it builds an agent id (`Identity.agent` and its counterparts), and a record, claim, lease, or certificate that names one is undecodable on the wire. A `pdt.issuer` that breaks the grammar falls back under R1.
- *R6, claim checks.* Every implementation applies both claim-stamp rules of §7.4: a claim stamped more than the drift ceiling before its entry's `issued` stamp, and a far-past claim that contests another holder's held claim, as Java's `mergeClaim` judges them.
- *R7, a revoked holder's claim.* A replica that sends space digests acknowledges a revoked holder's claim with an `a:c:` line, and keeps at most 8192 acknowledgements, the least recently refused forgotten first, as Java's `REFUSED_ACK_CAP` does.
- *R8, a state with a null lease stamp.* A replica drops, uncounted, a state whose `leaseStamp` is null (E7). A state that omits the field breaks P1, so its delta is undecodable.
- *R9, TypeScript's tickets, takes and votes.* TypeScript keeps a ticket whose write lease lapsed as its key's lock until the replica collects it, as Java's `KeyTickets.earliestLive` does; fires `TAKEN` once per claim epoch while the claim is in force; counts only the live proposals and ballots of its own vote space; and releases a collected entry's bulk payload through the content seam (Java's LB-E-04).
- *R10, orphaned claims.* Every implementation drops a claim on an entry its replica collected or never held once the claim's expiry passes, so the claim map stays bounded under churn. Java (`dropOrphanedClaims` in its sweep) and TypeScript (`collectTombstones`) drop them in their timer work; Python and Ruby run no periodic work, so each prunes as a space carrier arrives, once its claim map holds at least 1024 claims and twice as many as its last scan left.
- *R11, TypeScript's ref space.* The TypeScript persistent-tree facade opens and pulls its ref space when it is built, and the space of a ref when the facade first names it, as Java holds its ref space from the start. The peer no longer scans the payload bytes of a space it has not joined for heads.
- *R12, the remaining CBOR forms.* Every receiver refuses trailing bytes, simple values other than false, true and null, half floats, indefinite-length byte strings, duplicate keys, and integer map keys under §9.1.

*The golden vectors.* F4 changes the verdict of one golden vector without changing its bytes: `adv_agent_state_drift_delta` (an agent-signed state whose `signedAt` lies eleven minutes past its `certificate_verify_at`) is deferred, so it still leaves no entry merged. `golden.json` labels it refused, and its owner corrects the label.

### 9.3 Implementation status (v0.1.31; HLC canonical stamps, HLC round four, HLC round five, HLC round six and HLC round seven, 2026-11-08; v0.1.32, PDT 2 phase 8, 2026-11-09)

Java, Python, TypeScript, and Ruby conform to every rule of §9, §9.1, and §9.2 (round seven left two gaps, and v0.1.32 closes both, as the next paragraph records) and to the stamp rules of §4.2, §7.3, §7.4, and annex H: the canonical stamp and the stamp item under its shortest length header, the wire encoding with its item and record rules and the canonical-bytes check on every carrier, the exact types of the big-number tags with the 1000-byte bignum cap, the stamp of at most 3996 UTF-16 code units, the validated local node with no lone surrogate and at most 3965 units, the records of the capability pipes, the canonical signed beacon document, the refusal at every merge point with its count after verification keyed on signed content, signatures before revocation before stamps, the far-future rule for `issued`, `leaseStamp`, an agent's `signedAt`, and claim stamps of peer-signed and agent-signed carriers alike, each offer judged afresh, the seven counters, the cache of the 4096 most recently offered keys, a claim's signed bytes in `TakeClaim` order, Java's claim checks, the bounded claim map, the advisory head stamp with its fallback and `headsIgnored`, the shared base of a ref merge, the drift cap one millisecond inside the ceiling, the carry, the frame filters in the order of §9, and the skew margin. The HLC round seven conformance harness ran all four on 2026-11-08, regenerating every shared input and running Java from its built classes, over 649 rows. 630 rows are those of round six: the round-five cases as round six decides them, the 155 vectors of annex I through each carrier's receive path, a relabel of every field of a state, a claim, an envelope and a body under F1 and F3 with each carrier honestly signed, the F2 round trip of every big number Java writes, each client's own writer folded by Java, the head of a folded `PRef` under R1 to R5 with every document vector of annex I under `pdt.issued`, the base graphs of E16 and R4, the claim churn of R10, and the local names of R5. 19 rows cover the vectors round seven adds: `doc-56` to `doc-62` through their carrier and under `pdt.issued`, `frame-30` and `frame-31` as vectors and through the receive path, and `claim-23`. 646 rows agree, and no row shows a disagreement. The other three rows report behaviour with no verdict to compare: the receive path of `frame-28` and `frame-29` (Python and Ruby ignore the `DIGEST` kind and never decode its body, TypeScript refuses `frame-28` and accepts `frame-29`, and Java's reading strikes the sender of a malformed body), and when each implementation drops a lapsed orphaned claim (R10). A live test of the shared base of a ref merge runs between Java and Python (example 33 keeps every key of both writers); no live Java test exercises the TypeScript or Ruby ref reader, and the harness checked their base choice on the same version graphs. The harness also ran 86 round-seven rows through each implementation's reader of the record: 20 aggregate frames, 13 semantic-discovery queries and responses, 13 Raft frames, 9 WebRTC signals, and the 31 beacon vectors of annex I. Of the 258 client verdicts, 240 match Java's, none differs, and 18 have no reader, because Python and Ruby read no WebRTC signal. An adversarial review of the same day added 49 beacon documents, each signed by Java over tricky content or relabelled from one, and 48 hostile pipe payloads. After its fixes no client verdict differs from Java's, and 28 of the pipe verdicts have no reader.

Round seven (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08) closed four of the six gaps round six named, and two defects besides. Every implementation now refuses a bignum past 1000 bytes (S9). The clients read the aggregate, semantic-discovery, and Raft payloads under their annex I schemas, and TypeScript reads the WebRTC signal (`signal.decode`) the same way, so an unknown key or a wrong type refuses the body there as in Java. A `Space` wrapper, such as the read-only and routing wrappers a console builds over a space, passes `undecodableEntries` through, so a ref reader behind one counts an undecodable entry in `headsIgnored`. A stamp past 3996 UTF-16 code units is no stamp in any implementation. TypeScript's `cbor.dumps` writes a JavaScript `Map` as a map in insertion order, where it wrote an empty map before. Java's strict mapper refuses a null list or set element, which had let `SemResponse.hits`, `AppendEntries.entries`, and `RtcSignal.fp` hold a null that the clients refused, and had let `RaftLog` append a null entry. Round seven left two gaps, and v0.1.32 closes both (PDT 2 phase 8, 2026-11-09). Java reads the content-swarm messages (annex G, §8) through the strict `CborCodec.fromWire` under their annex I.1a schemas since PDT 2 phase 1 (P2-X-09, 2026-11-09), so Java refuses and counts a message with an unknown, misordered, or wrong-typed field, as the clients do (`MessagesTest.everyMessageReadsUnderItsWireSchemaAndRefusesARelabel`, `ContentVectorsConformanceTest`); a dispatcher peeks only at the tag before the strict read. Every implementation now refuses a beacon document, signed or not, whose named member holds another type than Java's field, a group name sent as a JSON number among them, and still reads the form of an unsigned document leniently (§9.1). Java's `BeaconDocument.parse` reads with every coercion off, and Python, TypeScript, and Ruby check each named member's type before the canonical-bytes check; the vectors `beacon-32` to `beacon-40` of annex I.2 pin the rule in all four, and the locator that fetched the document reports the refusal in its trace, as for every refused beacon document. Two readers are absent, so they trail no rule: no client reads the gossip learner's exchanges (`GossipLearner.Exchange`), and Python and Ruby read no WebRTC signal. Four byte fields of the pipe records stay opaque to the record rules, as annex I.1 lists.

The AgentSpaces Rust team owns the Rust peer. As its source read late on 2026-11-08, that peer's network decoder (`cbor::decode` in `agentspaces-wire`) applies the item rules S1 to S12 of §9.1, so it refuses a head longer than its argument needs and reads Jackson's text chunks and tags 2, 3, and 4, and its encoder writes a text past 3996 UTF-16 units in Jackson's chunks (HLC round six, 2026-11-08). Beyond that codec, the claim-order refusal below, and a guard on its own clock, it has adopted none of the rules of v0.1.30 and v0.1.31. It keeps a stamp with a counter above `LOGICAL_LIMIT` out of its clock, yet it still dispatches the frame that carries such a stamp and folds the state or the claim that carries one. It also saturates the counter, adopts a stamp at the ceiling unclamped, accepts a C0 control character or DEL in a node, judges no `signedAt`, defers no far-future record, counts every far-future frame alike, and bounds an agent-signed record, claim, or state in its certificate check. Whether it applies the record rules P1 to P4 and the canonical-bytes check to every record stays for the Rust team to confirm (FR-22, FR-23). It refuses a claim whose map arrives out of `TakeClaim` order, as §9.1 now requires of every implementation. A Rust string cannot hold a lone surrogate, so that node rule holds there already, and the Rust peer implements no persistent data trees, so R1 to R4 do not reach it. A read-only reading of the same source for round seven (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08) shows where it stands on that round's rules. Its codec already refuses a bignum past 1000 bytes and builds none (`MAX_BIGNUM_BYTES`, `CborError::BignumTooLong`). It bounds neither a stamp's length nor a node's, since `Hlc::new` checks nothing. Its aggregate reader (`Aggregate::from_frame`) reads an integer where a double belongs, ignores an unknown key and an absent union field, and refuses a null `epochId` that Java accepts. Its beacon reader (`beacon.rs`) ignores unknown members of a signed document, verifies the signature over a re-encoding, and writes a character past U+FFFF raw in the signed bytes where Java escapes it, so it refuses an honest Java-signed document that holds one. `issues/ISSUE-HlcCanonicalStamps.md` (revision 5, with FR-31 to FR-34 for round seven) gives the Rust team the exact changes and the vectors. Until the Rust team ships them, a Rust replica can hold states and claims that every other implementation refuses and refuse records that every other implementation holds, and the guarantees this section draws from these rules do not cover it.

*PDT 2 (v0.1.32; srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md §10, built 2026-11-08 and 09).* Java implements every rule of PDT 2 that this revision states as normative, advertises `pdt` = `"2"` wherever its content swarm runs at level 2, and runs the content store (`agentspaces-store`). Python and TypeScript implement level 2 and advertise `pdt` = `"2"`: both read `db` refs, relaxed vectors, texts, `PRef.v` 2 heads, and the version graph, verify BLAKE3 windows through the `bo` walk, read `dcz` frames, run both sides of a sketch session, answer `CHALLENGE`, receive pushed bundles, and write `SignedPin` entries through a store chooser, and TypeScript also writes `vg` nodes and packs relaxed vectors. Neither keeps delta frames, so neither sends `NODES.deltas`. Ruby stays at feature level 1: it reads `db` refs, relaxed vectors, texts, `PRef.v` 2 heads, and BLAKE3 windows, writes `SignedPin` entries, serves `NODE_WANT` and `TREE_WANT`, and sends level-1 forms alone. The 36 PDT 2 entries of `golden.json` pass in all four, and Java and the clients reproduce every message of the negentropy conformance vectors. These rows of the design are not built: chunk-group picks, which a holder answers as missing; VCDIFF frames, whose format byte `v` every reader refuses by name; structured nodes named by BLAKE3 (P2-B-07); the Merkle Search Tree boundary and the ClojureScript adapter (P2-D-10, P2-D-12); a late joiner's reconcile of its version-graph nodes (P2-K-06); the browser worker's lazy `db` reader (P2-C-05); and the eight round trips of the challenge deadline, since the reference measures no round trip and waits a configured 2 s. Java's and Ruby's version-graph walks stop at their first failed batch and keep no backoff per chain node, where Python and TypeScript keep one (§9.4). The AgentSpaces Rust team owns the Rust port of PDT 2 (P2-C-03), and the Rust peer implements no persistent data trees yet.

*Rationale.* The drift cap bounded the physical part alone. Before v0.1.30 a clock adopted a remote counter unchecked and saturated it at the largest value its integer type held, so one signed stamp with a physical part just inside the drift ceiling and the largest counter froze the receiver's clock on one stamp until wall time passed that physical part, up to ten minutes, and a repeated stamp kept it frozen. The frozen node then stamped every frame alike, so its receivers' replay filters dropped its pings, acknowledgements, rumors, and digests, and their failure detectors evicted it; a frozen replica's lease renewals tied with the leases they renewed, and the last-writer-wins register keeps the held value on a tie, so leases lapsed and taken entries reappeared; and the claim lattice, which prefers the lower stamp, made a replica stamping ten minutes ahead lose every contested take. Because record, lease, and claim stamps travel inside replicated entries, one hostile entry froze every replica that gossip and anti-entropy brought it to. An honest counter resets every millisecond and stays tiny, so the limit costs honest peers nothing. An adversarial review of the first v0.1.30 text found two further faults. A client that parsed stamps leniently adopted a negative, fractional, empty, or unreadable counter: a counter of -1e300 froze a TypeScript clock, because adding one to it changed nothing, a negative counter poisoned the clock so that Java peers refused its later stamps as malformed and evicted the client, and an unreadable counter folded into a Ruby claim made every later honest claim on that entry fail. A clamp at exactly `wall + MAX_DRIFT` let a carry land one millisecond past the ceiling, and every receiver's far-future check then struck the honest peer until it reached quarantine. The acceptable stamp and the clamp one millisecond inside the ceiling close both faults. A third adversarial review found that the implementations still read stamp text differently (HLC canonical stamps, 2026-11-08). Java read `007:00:n` as `7:0:n` and verified signatures over the re-encoded text, while Python and TypeScript verified them over the raw text, so a claim signed over non-canonical text replaced an honest claim in a TypeScript replica that Java could never verify, and the claim lattice split between Java and the clients. TypeScript also capped the physical part at 2^53 and the counter at ten digits where Java took up to 2^63, and Ruby took a byte string for a stamp. The canonical stamp grammar gives every stamp one text, every implementation the same range, and every signature the same bytes. The same review found that a hostile member could drag an honest peer to the ceiling, and a receiver whose wall clock ran slightly behind then struck that peer on every frame until quarantine; the skew margin turns those strikes into counted drops. A fourth adversarial review (HLC round four, 2026-11-08) found that the implementations still disagreed in five places. The clients judged only the grammar of `signedAt` while Java also refused its counter above the limit; the clients and Java fell back differently from a malformed `pdt.issued` or `pdt.issuer`; Java accepted a stamp sent as an indefinite-length or tagged text string or as overlong UTF-8, because its CBOR parser repairs all three; a state or claim stamped past a receiver's ceiling, as an honest taker dragged ahead stamps its records, could bring that taker to quarantine; and the implementations counted refusals under different keys and names. The judgement of `signedAt`, the advisory head stamp, the stamp item, the deferral of far-future records, and the counting rule close those gaps, and every peer validates its own node so that it never emits a stamp the grammar refuses. A sixth round (HLC round six, 2026-11-08) found that Java verified signatures over repaired re-encodings and coerced wrong-typed fields while the clients verified in arrival order, so the four still accepted different relabels of one signed carrier; one wire encoding and the canonical-bytes check give every carrier one form and every signature one set of bytes. A seventh round (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08) found four more places where the implementations read one carrier differently: a bignum past the 1000 bytes Java reads, a stamp too long for one definite text, the records inside a capability pipe, and a signed beacon document, whose signature covered a re-encoding, so whitespace, member order, an unknown member, or another base64 text of the signature relabelled it. The bignum cap, the stamp bound, the pipe schemas, and the canonical beacon document give each of them one form.

### 9.4 Waits on other peers (v0.1.32, guidance; swarm retries, 2026-11-09)

Lateness and loss are the normal case in a peer-to-peer group: a copy arrives late, a holder leaves, and an answer goes missing. This section gives the shape that every wait on another peer SHOULD follow and the values the reference implementation uses for the waits this specification governs. It adds nothing to the wire, and an implementation that waits otherwise still interoperates. `ROBUSTNESS.md` §10 records the owner's principle and the 34 places where the reference implementation waits on a peer.

- *Timers in time.* A wait reads the clock and sizes itself from what the path observes, such as the round trip, the bytes in flight, or the delivery time per hop, between a floor and a ceiling. A wait never counts ticks, because a scheduler runs missed ticks back to back after a stall.
- *A spread first ask.* When many members wait for one item, each member adds a uniform random delay over a window to its first ask, so the asks do not reach one peer together.
- *The missing piece alone.* A member keeps what arrived, asks again only for what is missing, and asks holders or announcers other than the origin first.
- *Backoff with jitter.* Each further ask waits longer than the last, with a random spread, and cycles through the known sources again.
- *A liveness path keeps trying.* A member waits for a head's root while the head's entry lives, for a pinned root while its fetch makes progress, and for its seeds until its owner closes it. Budgets per peer and per tick bound what a hostile peer can cost. They limit how often a member asks and how much it tracks, and they never end the wait for an item an honest peer will serve.
- *The caller's deadline.* A read with a deadline returns at its deadline, the work it started goes on, and the next read joins that work.
- *Counters.* Each wait counts its first asks, its retries, its premature asks, and its fallbacks, by cause.
- *The gossip tree.* An implementation answers lateness with timers, retries, and other sources, and it keeps the structure and rules of the lazy-push tree of §5.3: the eager and lazy edges, the prunes, the origin rule, and the reset.

The waits this specification governs, with the values of the reference implementation:

| Wait | Rule | Values |
|---|---|---|
| The first `GRAFT` for an announced item (§5.3) | 500 ms after the first `HAVE` plus a spread, to an announcer other than the first heard | a spread over twice the 90th-percentile graft round trip, from 1 s to 4 s; later asks after 1, 2, 4, and 8 s; the item kept while an announcer announced it within 15 s |
| A tree request, `NODE_WANT` or `TREE_WANT` (§8) | a timeout from the smoothed round trip; only the undelivered part goes back | `max(10 s, 8 SRTT)`; a `missing` answer benches that source for that CID from 1 s, doubling to 16 s, with a spread of up to half; a source that times out three times sits out 60 s |
| A head whose root has not loaded (annex H) | the read leaves the head out of its merge and counts it as late (`headsLate`), apart from the faults it counts in `headsIgnored`; the fetch goes on | a failed fetch is due again after 1 s, doubling to four load timeouts, with a spread of up to half, started from the tick while the entry lives, other holders before the issuer |
| A chain node of the version graph (annex H) | a backoff per missing `vg` CID | as for a failed head fetch (Python and TypeScript; §9.3) |
| The push grace (annex H) | the follower waits for the announced bundles, then fetches what is missing | the repair window plus the bundles at the stream's budget, from 2 s to 15 s; bundles that come late stored for two graces |
| A possession challenge (annex H) | the challenger refuses an answer after its deadline | 2 s plus eight round trips by design; the reference waits a configured 2 s |
| A store's fetch of a pinned root (annex H) | the fetch timeout counts from the last progress | the operator's `fetchTimeout` |
| A client's seed dials | rounds of seed dials until the owner closes the client | 5, 10, 20, and 30 s between rounds, with a spread of up to a quarter |
| A block fetch (§7.1) | the issuer and two random members | waits of 0.8, 2, 5, and 15 s; attempts after 2, 5, 15, and 60 s; once a minute while the entry lives |

A new capability's protocol states, for each of its waits on another peer, the timer in time, what it re-queues, which holders it asks before the origin, the backoff with jitter, the budget that bounds a hostile peer, and how its liveness path keeps trying.

## 10. The Embabel Extension: `embabel-agentspaces`

The first implementation ships as an Embabel/Spring Boot starter. The design intent: an Embabel developer gets P2P coordination by adding a dependency and a few properties, with zero new mental models beyond "the blackboard can now be shared, and shared state is leased."

### 10.1 Bootstrapping

Adding the starter dependency (`agentspaces-spring-boot-starter`) is the whole enablement step. Spring Boot auto-configuration starts the peer, joins the configured groups, attaches the configured spaces, and enrolls annotated beans. Earlier drafts showed an `@EnableAgentSpaces` annotation; it does not exist, and the starter dependency plus the `agentspaces.*` properties replace it, because auto-configuration is the idiomatic Spring Boot form of the same intent. A group is identified in one of two mutually exclusive ways. `founding` names a string every member turns into the same GroupID locally, for fleets whose members share configuration. `join` names an existing self-certifying group by its `aspace://<groupID>` URI (or bare GroupID) and requires `seeds`: the peer asks the seeds for the founding advertisement with `GROUP_AD_WANT`, verifies the first `GROUP_AD` answer against the GroupID it holds (§5.1), and joins with it, failing after `join-timeout-millis` (default 10,000) when no seed served a verified document. When neither is set, the group name doubles as the founding string.

```yaml
agentspaces:
  keystore: ./peer-keys                  # omit for an ephemeral identity
  bind: 0.0.0.0:7500                     # omit for a dial-only peer
  roles: [ rendezvous ]                  # optional; a rendezvous sizes its ad-cache at 4x (32,768)
  tick-millis: 250
  card-refresh-millis: 300000            # refreshes cards and capability advertisements alike
  identity:                              # how bound agents sign (§4.2)
    agent-keys: subordinate              # peer (default) | subordinate: each bound agent gets its own key
    agent-keystore: ./agent-keys         # v0.1.13; blank (default): agent keys last one process
    agent-certificate-ttl: PT24H         # v0.1.13; default 24 h, renewed at half-life
  groups:
    - name: research-fleet
      founding: research-fleet-v1        # every member derives the same GroupID
      seeds: [ "hq.example:7500" ]
      content-key: "<base64 of 32 bytes>" # optional; encrypts every space below, served by key-wrap
      content-key-rotation:              # v0.1.13 (§11a.3)
        rotate-every: P7D                # unset (default): this peer never rotates on its own
        cutover-delay: PT1M              # unset (default): two gossip periods plus 30 s
        writer-grace: PT1H               # default 1 h; past it a writer lacking the new epoch refuses to seal
      spaces:
        - name: tasks                    # LEASE_RACE, settle-window-millis 200 by default
        - name: tasks-auction
          strategy: AUCTION
        - name: control
          admission: allowlist           # group (default) | allowlist | credential | authorizer
          allowed-agents: [ "<peerId>/steward" ]   # an empty allowlist fails fast
        - name: partner-tasks
          admission: credential          # the issuer grants and revokes per agent (§7.5)
          credential-issuer: "<peerId>"  # default: this node
        - name: audited-tasks
          admission: authorizer          # the profile's Authorizer answers SPACE_WRITE / SPACE_TAKE
    - name: partner-fleet
      join: "aspace://z7f3a…"            # fetch and verify the founding document from the seeds
      join-timeout-millis: 10000
      seeds: [ "partner.example:7500" ]
  capabilities:
    enabled: true                        # the whole shipped-capability surface
    aggregate: true
    vote: true
    votes-space: votes                   # a configured space of this name, else a private one
    semantic-discovery: true             # the deterministic hashing embedder
    key-wrap: true                       # serves the content key when one is configured
    ordered-log: false                   # true fails fast; supply a @ProvidesCapability bean
    gossip-learn: false                  # true wires default weight-averaging gossip learning
  security:
    profile: mtls                        # dev-local | mtls (default) | mtls-oidc | zero-trust
    grants:                              # membership profiles only; PeerId lists, empty = any admitted member
      directive-issuer: [ "<console peerId>" ]
      raft-voter: []
      connector-serve: []
      key-holder: []
      key-rotator: []                    # v0.1.13; empty = the founder alone may rotate (§11a.3)
      space-write: []
      space-take: []
      vote: []
      model-serve: []
    oidc:                                # required under mtls-oidc and zero-trust; fails fast when incomplete
      issuer: https://idp.example/realms/fleet
      audience: agentspaces
      jwks-url: https://idp.example/realms/fleet/protocol/openid-connect/certs
      token-file: /run/secrets/fleet-token   # or token: <jwt>; re-read every card-refresh tick
  transport:
    tls:
      trust-store: /etc/agentspaces/ca.p12   # selects the enterprise-CA mode (§5.6)
      revocation-validator: founder      # founder (default) | founder-or-ca: CA-rooted peer revocation (§6.1), needs trust-store
      crl-refresh: PT5M                  # default 5 min; how often the crls files are checked for replacement
  console:
    command:
      enabled: true
      token: ""                          # blank with security.oidc configured selects the JWT gate
      required-scope: aspace:console:operate
  a2a:                                   # v0.1.13, off by default (§12)
    enabled: true
    bind: 127.0.0.1                      # a routable bind needs a token or the OIDC gate
    port: 7594
    space: tasks                         # where A2A tasks are written; required when enabled
    token: ""                            # blank with security.oidc configured selects the JWT gate
    required-scope: aspace:a2a:client
  multicast:
    enabled: false                       # LAN bootstrap beacon, opt-in
    group: 239.255.42.99:7787
    interval-millis: 5000
  embabel:
    remote-actions-enabled: true         # with Embabel on the classpath
    auto-deploy: true                    # SmartLifecycle deploys the generated agent
    deploy-poll-millis: 2000
    remote-action-timeout-millis: 30000
```

Under the starter, every auto-configured space publishes its SpaceAdvertisement on creation and rides a per-group block exchange, so bulk payloads are content-addressed without further wiring (§7.5); a configured `content-key` seals every space in the group and the key-wrap capability serves it to admitted members (§11a); annotated beans bind into every group whose spaces satisfy them, with one card per group (§10.3, §10.4); and `@ProvidesCapability` beans are registered through the bean post-processor (§10.5).
