Eleven golden vectors change (the envelopes and frames, which carry `ver`, and `join_ticket_record_cbor`, whose tags now sort `key` before `join`); the rest are byte-identical. `@SpaceReduce` joins the annotation model (§10.3): a fold over an unbounded stream of elements per key whose accumulator is an entry, advanced by the element's own atomic completion and serialized by a per-key ticket; `CardAction.kind` gains `reduce`.

**v0.1.14 (October 2026).** The workflow-shape revision (issue #16). Templates gain tag conditions judged before decode, reads and events gain the record's metadata, and completions may write tagged results (§7.2); the binder may share its spaces' schema registry so a fleet can name its types by an ontology namespace while the reserved types keep their names (§7.1, §10.3); the annotation model gains the `tags` attribute, the `Tagged` return, `@SpaceJoin` with its `LOCAL`, `LEASED`, and `ORDERED` modes, the `Motion` return, and `@Propose` (§10.3). On the wire: nothing changes; one reserved entry type, `JoinTicket`, is added and pinned by `join_ticket_record_cbor`, and `CardAction.kind` gains the values `join`, `propose`, and `on-estimate`, which a v0.1.13 reader keeps and does not invoke. The same revision carries the workflow verbs (ISSUE-WorkflowVerbs, ISSUE-OnEstimate): the `Entries` fork with `produces` on the card, `where` field filters and sealed returns, the gather (`atLeast`, `countedBy`, `settle`), event-kind selection on `@SpaceNotify` (`on = EXPIRED` makes a lease a timer), and `@OnEstimate` over the aggregate's new settle rule (§8, §10.3); all additive, nothing on the wire.

**v0.1.13 (October 2026).** The certificates-and-revocation revision (TODO items 6 to 11). Every change is an appended field omitted when absent, so a fleet that opts into nothing keeps every byte, and the golden vectors gain the new structures without changing one existing vector. Agent certificates are judged at the signing time of what they certify, within the HLC drift bound, so an agent's history stays verifiable after its certificate lapses; renewing identities re-issue at half-life over one key (§4.2). An agent-attested entry's renewals, cancellations, and completions must be signed by the agent (`signer`, `signedAt`, `stateCertificate`), and a peer-signed transition on such an entry is refused (§11a.4); two v0.1.11 vectors that carried one change verdict on purpose and are republished as hostile. Revocation covers everything certified: `CredentialRevocation` revokes agents, agent keys, X.509 leaves, and join credentials under a freeze rule that keeps history for retirement and refuses everything new for a compromise; registries keep one record per target under an authority-first total order, which also ends successor erasure; and the enterprise-CA mode can root a peer revocation in the CA with the chain and CRL as evidence (§6.1). Group content keys become a ring of epochs with signed, authority-checked rotation, time-based scheduling against the fleet-wide nonce budget, ring persistence, and per-agent key wrap sealed to an agent's certified X25519 key (§11a.1 to §11a.3). Each group's authorizer refuses what the group revoked, and the shared OIDC authorizer also requires group membership (§11). AgentCards declare their actions and carry their agent's certificate (§6.1). The A2A gateway gains Spring auto-configuration, and QUIC reaches parity with TLS in the CA mode (§5.5, §12). On the wire: `EntryRecord.keyEpoch`, `EntryStateDto.{stateCertificate, signer, signedAt}`, `StateSignView.{signer, signedAt}`, `SignView.keyEpoch`, `AgentCertificate.encryptionPublicKey`, `AgentCard.{actions, agentCertificate}`, `RevocationAdvertisement.evidence`, key-wrap `KeyRequest.{epochs, agent}`, `KeyResponse.keys`, `WrapBinding.{epoch, rotator, agent}`, the new `CredentialRevocation` advertisement, and the `credential-revocations` gossip stream.

**v0.1.21 (October 2026).** The JavaScript revision (SRAD agentspaces-js). Five transport bindings ship: `ws`, `wss`, `http`, `https`, and `webrtc` (§5.5), with attestation by signed DTLS fingerprint and the relayed-signal exception under required attestation (§5.6); the capabilities `aspace:cap/rtc-signal` and `aspace:cap/asset-invoke` (§8); the asset operations of the AssetCard's open `access` map and the `workers://` convention (§6.1a); the portable filter grammar (§7.2); annexes B, C, and F. The membership view stream of v0.1.17 is pulled by a joining client and served by any member that accepts connections. Nothing changes in the envelope, the frame kinds, or the advertisement fields; the wire version stays 3 and every earlier golden vector is byte-identical.

**v0.1.22** is reserved for the Spring AI 2.1 revision (`CardAction.confirm`, review spring-ai-2 §12.3).

**v0.1.24 (November 2026).** The rules revision (ISSUE-Constraints). The reserved rule entries and their tags (§7.1); the constraint gate in the binder, its two call sites, the fail-open rule, and the `rules.gate` hint (§10.3); an entry view names the record's schema as written (`type`), and a replica that merges another peer's take claim fires a `TAKEN` event once per claim epoch with the holder as the actor (§7.2), so a watcher that never takes hears of the takes. Nothing changes in the envelope, the frame kinds, or the claim records; the wire version stays 3 and every earlier golden vector is byte-identical. The `FlowManifest` reserved entry, the reconciler's reading of it, and templates by wire type name (§10.3, ISSUE-FlowManifest). The ModelCard advertisement (§6.1a.1) and the capability names `aspace:cap/llm-gateway` and `aspace:cap/guardrail` with the guardrail module (§8; ISSUE-ModelCard).

**v0.1.29 (November 2026).** The persistent-trees revision (SRAD agentspaces-pdt; phases 0 to 6 built 2026-11-07 and 2026-11-08). A tree root (codec 0x71) beside the piece root in `payloadRef`, the tree escalation of a space, and its pre-seal rule (§7.1); the recordless state delta and the `recordless` hint (§7.3, §9); the `trees` parameter and the tags 8 to 11 of the content swarm (§8); annex H (nodes and CIDs, the map and vector layouts, FastCDC and the prolly rule, the spill rules, `PRef#v1`, the four messages, the sealing modes, the vectors). Nothing changes in the envelope or the frame kinds; `Manifest` appends one optional field (`layout`) omitted when absent; `EntryStateDto.record` becomes nullable on three delta kinds behind a hint; the wire version stays 3 and every earlier golden vector is byte-identical. Eighteen golden vectors are added (annex H), `pdt_sealed_node` and `state_delta_recordless_cbor` among them, and `pdt_map_collision` cannot be produced. The sealed-nodes nonce was re-pinned on 2026-11-08 after the security review: it is keyed (HMAC-SHA256 under the per-epoch node key) and no longer the bare digest of the plaintext, which let a keyless holder confirm a guessed node.

**v0.1.28 (November 2026).** The `host` group on `AppCard` (§6.1a.4), appended last and omitted when absent; nothing else on the wire changes.

**v0.1.27 (November 2026).** The commerce names, appended and none on the wire: `aspace:cap/commerce` as a reserved capability type with the roles `merchant`, `shopper`, `payment` and the parameters `profile`, `ucp`, `jwk` (§8 addendum), and `commerce` as a reserved space name (§7.6); the constants in `ai.badmonkey.agentspaces.api.ad.Reserved`. The records that travel in that space, `Mandate` and `Order`, and the verification of an AP2 credential chain are the open `agentspaces-commerce` module's, with the vectors `mandate_cbor`, `order_cbor`, `capability_commerce_cbor` beside it.

**v0.1.23 (October 2026).** The large-data revision (SRAD large-networks-bulk-data). Content roots beside block CIDs in `payloadRef`, the manifest and holding records, and the escalation rule (§7.1); the capabilities `aspace:cap/content-swarm` and `aspace:cap/relay-signal` (§8, annex G); relay paths on `RELAY_FRAME` with the `relay.via` and `relay.links` hints, the path rule, pinning, and the unreachable signal (§5.4, §9); the `CONTENT_FETCH` operation. Nothing changes in the envelope or the frame kinds; `RELAY_FRAME` appends two optional fields a 0.4.0 reader ignores; the wire version stays 3 and every earlier golden vector is byte-identical. The golden vectors for the new records and messages are listed in annex G and are pending.

**v0.1.30 (November 2026).** The clock-bounds revision (HLC logical counter, 2026-11-08 (Rust team report)). The AgentSpaces Rust team reported, and the Java source confirmed, that the hybrid logical clock capped only the physical part of a remote stamp, adopted its logical counter unchecked, and saturated the counter, so one signed stamp froze a receiver's clock for up to the ten-minute drift ceiling, and through replicated entries froze every replica the stamp reached. §9 now states the clock normatively: its update rule, the drift cap, `LOGICAL_LIMIT` = 65536, the acceptable stamp (a non-negative integer physical part, an integer counter from 0 to 65536, and a node), the refusal of any other remote stamp at every merge point (frames, self-introductions, folded states, gossiped claims, claims the ordered log commits) with a count and, where the node keeps strikes, a strike with the reason `logical counter above 65536`, and the carry that replaces saturation; it also states the frame-age window and the `(from, stamp)` replay filter that §5.6 already cited as the replay rule of §9, both measured against wall time. §7.3 refuses a state whose `issued` or `leaseStamp` is not acceptable, after its signatures verify, counting and striking once per distinct state, and asks the replica to acknowledge the refusal in its digest, §7.4 adds the acceptable stamp as a third claim-stamp rule, and §11 records the defence. The same day, an adversarial review of the first text found that lenient parsers in the clients adopted negative, fractional, and unreadable counters, and that a carry at a clamp of exactly `wall + MAX_DRIFT` landed one millisecond past the ceiling, where receivers struck the honest peer; the revision therefore defines the acceptable stamp and clamps one millisecond inside the ceiling. A third adversarial review (HLC canonical stamps, 2026-11-08) found that Java accepted leading zeros while the clients verified signatures over raw text, which split the claim lattice; that TypeScript capped the physical part at 2^53 and Ruby accepted byte-string stamps; that Python and Ruby counted a refused state or claim before verification and once per offer; that the clients ran the frame filters in another order; that the implementations judged `signedAt` and the `pdt.issued` attribute differently; that Python compared node parts by code point; that Java neither acknowledged a stamp refusal in its digest nor counted far-future drops and undecodable space deltas; and that a receiver running slightly behind struck an honest peer a hostile member had dragged to the ceiling until quarantine. The revision therefore defines the canonical stamp grammar (§9), the UTF-16 order of node parts, the order of the frame filters, the count after verification once per distinct state or claim under a bounded cache, the acknowledgement of a stamp refusal (§7.3), and the five-second skew margin of the far-future check. A fourth adversarial review (HLC round four, 2026-11-08) found that the implementations still judged an agent-signed state's `signedAt` differently, fell back differently from a malformed `pdt.issued` or `pdt.issuer`, accepted different CBOR items as a stamp (Java's parser repairs indefinite-length and tagged text and overlong UTF-8, and the TypeScript decoder replaced invalid UTF-8 with U+FFFD), let far-future records bring an honest taker a hostile member had dragged ahead to quarantine, and counted refusals under different keys and names. The owner's decisions D1 to D6 of that day settle each point, and the revision states them: §7.3 and §11a.4 judge `signedAt` like `issued` and `leaseStamp` (D1); §9 and annex H make `pdt.issued` and `pdt.issuer` advisory, with a fallback to the entry's own stamp and issuer together (D2); §9 fixes the stamp item, a definite-length, untagged CBOR text string in shortest-form UTF-8 (D3), and recorded until round five that Java alone also refused a longer length header; §7.3 and §7.4 defer a state or claim stamped up to one hour past `wall + MAX_DRIFT + SKEW_MARGIN` and refuse one further out (D4); §7.3 and §9 key every refusal on signed content, acknowledge refused states in digests, and name the counters after Java's (D5); and §9 has every peer validate its own node (D6). A fifth review round (HLC round five, 2026-11-08) ran a conformance probe through all four implementations and found them still split on the length header of a stamp item; on CBOR outside a stamp, where Java accepted tags and overlong UTF-8 while TypeScript and Ruby refused every indefinite-length text string, the form Java writes for any string over 3996 UTF-16 code units; on the far-future counters; on agent-signed records and claims, whose certificate check bounded the stamp before the far-future rule could defer it; on whether a refused far-future offer merged once the wall caught up; on the order of the revocation and stamp checks; on a state with no lease stamp; on the fallback from a null `pdt.issuer`; on a lone surrogate in a local node; on the bytes a claim's signature covers; on the eviction order of the refused-offer cache; and on the base of a ref merge between writers that share no version. The owner's decisions E1 to E17 of that day settle each point, and the revision states them: §9 binds the stamp item to its shortest length header (E1), names all seven counters in every implementation (E2), and fixes CBOR outside a stamp (E8), the lone surrogate of the node rule (E11), the place each implementation validates its node (E17), and the `BATCH` exception to striking nobody (E15); §4.2 and §7.3 let the far-future rule judge agent-signed records and claims (E4), judge every offer afresh (E5), and order signatures, revocation, and stamps (E6), and §7.3 lets a state without a lease stamp decode (E7) and sets the refused-offer cache everywhere to 4096 keys that evict the least recently offered key first (E14); §7.4 fixes a claim's signed bytes in `TakeClaim` order (E13); §9 and annex H make a null attribute fall back (E10, with the agent id of E3); and annex H makes the empty value the shared base of writers whose histories start from it (E16). The round also brought Python's frame decoder (E9) and TypeScript's acknowledgement of a revoked actor's state (E12) to rules §9 and §7.3 already stated. After the round the probe agrees in 175 of its 179 cases; the four that differ concern a claim's epoch sent as a float, a decimal, or text, or its bid sent as text, which no decision covers, and §9 records them with the related gaps. The Rust peer keeps a stamp with a counter above 65536 out of its clock, yet still dispatches the frame and folds the state or the claim that carries one, and it has adopted none of the other rules of this revision; `issues/ISSUE-HlcCanonicalStamps.md` gives the Rust team the exact changes. A canonical stamp at or below the limit encodes and orders exactly as before, so no golden vector changes.

**v0.1.31 (November 2026).** The wire-encoding revision (HLC round six, 2026-11-08). Round five left two questions to the owner: a claim number that arrives in another CBOR form than its holder signed, which Java's Jackson coerced and the clients refused or dropped, and an indefinite-length byte string, which Java and Python decoded and TypeScript and Ruby refused. The round-six definition stage found that the split ran deeper. Java verified a frame over its decoded envelope re-encoded in record order and decoded with Jackson's coercions, so it accepted 87 of the 108 vectors annex I refuses, most of them relabels of a signed carrier (a longer head, a field out of order or unknown, an integer sent as a decimal or as text, a duplicate key, bytes after the item), while the clients verified the envelope in arrival order; Java's encoder wrote some values in more than one form (a small `BigInteger` as a bignum, a `BigDecimal` with its scale, NaN with its raw bits, a `Set` in a per-JVM order); TypeScript failed to verify an honest Java record whose tags held an integer-like key; and Python and Ruby sorted a state's dots by code point where Java sorts by UTF-16 code units. The owner's rulings F1 to F4 and R1 to R12 of that day settle each point, and the revision states them. §9.1 defines the wire encoding: the item rules S1 to S12, the record rules P1 to P4 with every protocol record's schema in annex I, the exact types that hold an application value's big numbers and floats in each implementation, the item rules over every embedded item of a persistent-tree node, the receiver's procedure with its canonical-bytes check, and the order of the signed views. §9.2 states the rulings: wrong-typed protocol fields are undecodable (F1); tags 2, 3, and 4 stand in application values alone (F2); every carrier must re-encode to the bytes received (F3); an agent's `signedAt` meets the far-future rule (F4, with §4.2, §7.3, and §11a.4); `PRef.attrs` holds text, a head stamped past the reader's bound falls back, and every reader counts `headsIgnored` and walks bases as Java does (R1 to R4, with annex H); agent ids refuse a `:` in the local name (R5); Python adds Java's claim checks (R6); TypeScript acknowledges a revoked holder's claim (R7); Python drops a state with a null lease stamp uncounted (R8); TypeScript aligns its tickets, takes, and votes with Java (R9); every replica bounds its claim map (R10, with §7.4); and the TypeScript ref facade pulls its ref space as Java holds it (R11). §7.4 refuses a claim whose map arrives out of `TakeClaim` order, which E13 had verified, and §7.3 refuses a state that omits its lease stamp. Annex I lists the record schemas and the 155 vectors. Four accept rows of `issues/ISSUE-HlcCanonicalStamps.md` §14.8 (the six-unit indefinite text `7F 63 61 62 63 63 64 65 66 FF`, `C2 41 80`, `C3 41 FF`, and `C4 82 3A 7F FF FF FF 01`) become refusals. After Java's encoder changes, a `BigInteger` inside the `long` range, a `BigDecimal` with trailing zeros, and a NaN with a payload encode differently from what earlier 0.5.0 builds wrote; nothing at 0.5.0 is published, so no transition window applies, and Java still opens what a node persisted before through its lenient read. The HLC round six conformance harness ran Java, Python, TypeScript, and Ruby over 630 rows, and 627 agree with no disagreement (§9.3). The wire version stays 3, no golden vector changes its bytes, and `adv_agent_state_drift_delta` now defers where its label says refused (§9.2). *Round seven (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08).* The revision keeps its number. The seventh review closed four of the six gaps round six left, and two defects besides. A bignum's byte string holds at most 1000 bytes in every decoder and encoder (S9). A stamp holds at most 3996 UTF-16 code units and a clock node at most 3965, so a stamp is always one definite text, and every reader falls back from a `pdt.issued` too long for one (§9). Annex I lists the records of the aggregate, gossip-learning, semantic-discovery, Raft, and WebRTC signalling pipes, and the clients now read the ones they handle under P1 to P4, with any float64 in a `double` field while `TakeClaim` keeps its finite bid. A console's read-only and routing `Space` wrappers pass `undecodableEntries` through. TypeScript's `signal.decode` refuses an unknown key and a wrong type, and its `cbor.dumps` writes a `Map` as a map. Java's strict mapper refuses a null list or set element. The Ruby vector copy is byte-identical to the core file. A signed beacon document must arrive as exactly its canonical JSON, in Java's form, with its signature and key in padded standard base64 (§9.1). An adversarial review of the same day found that the clients signed a character past U+FFFF raw where Java escapes it, that Python and Ruby sorted map keys by code point and upper-cased a literal `\u00ab`, that TypeScript lost a ttl past 2^53 and read a numeric signature as none, that Python and Ruby crashed on hostile documents and accepted unsigned groups Java's `Group` record refuses, and that all four let the base64 text of a signature vary; the round fixed each. Annex I grows to 196 vectors with `doc-56` to `doc-62`, `frame-30`, `frame-31`, `claim-23`, and `beacon-01` to `beacon-31`. The round-seven harness ran 649 rows, of which 646 agree with no disagreement, and 86 round-seven rows, whose 240 client verdicts match Java's, none differing and 18 without a reader (§9.3). Two gaps remain: Java's content-swarm messages, and an unsigned beacon document whose group name arrives as a number. The wire version stays 3, and no golden vector changes its bytes.

**v0.1.32 (November 2026).** Two changes share this revision: the AppCard revision of ISSUE-AppCardOriginFormats (AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09; neutral vectors, 2026-11-09), written here, and PDT 2 (`srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md`, built 2026-11-08 and 09), whose text PDT 2 phase 8 wrote on 2026-11-09 (P2-O-01). The AppCard revision closes `kind` to four values that say how an app connects: `peer`, `fed`, `gateway`, and `assembled`, which now means a static app assembled from extracted data that reads nothing live; `realm` leaves the set, and an app a console drafts from its agents' ui declarations becomes `fed` with an origin. It adds `origin {system, ref, version, attrs}` and `formats`, open values listed in the new `agentspaces-spec/REGISTRY.md`, with the predicates `fromOrigin` and `handles` and three `fieldEquals` keys; it ties no kind to any origin. `host.signOn` and `Publish.signOn` become `idp` or `none`, default `idp`, and a reader refuses `tenant`. A reader refuses a closed AppCard value it does not know (§6.1b). The hint `wire.appcard=2` gates every new field on the whole view, as `wire.recordless=1` gates a null record. The 4 KiB sentence of §6.1a.1 and §6.1a.2 gives way to a byte budget on every advertisement cache (§6.3), and an `AppCard` or `AgentCard` above 65,536 bytes moves its large parts into a content-swarm document that readers fetch on demand, with a publisher warning past 57,344 bytes (§6.1a.5). `agentspaces-spec/REALM.md` states how a realm's apps reach a fleet. The action contract moves from the second §6.1b to §6.1c, so §6.1b names Unknown values alone. The golden vectors `agent_card_ui_cbor` and `app_card_host_cbor` take neutral values, 5 accept vectors, 2 value entries, and 18 refusal vectors join `golden.json`, and the platform's realistic examples move to the non-normative `golden-examples.json`. No implementation has built the AppCard revision yet; §9.3 still records v0.1.31. The core cancel events of 2026-11-09 add to §7.2 the events a cancel raises: EXPIRED once at every replica that held the entry, the cancelling one included, with no change on the wire. Java's `ReplicatedSpace` and the Python, TypeScript, and Ruby clients raise them, and PDT 2 phase 8 made the draft note normative. PDT 2 adds the `content-store` card (§6.1a.6); the reserved entries `StorePin#v1`, `SignedPin#v1`, and `StoreReceipt#v1` (§7.1); the content-swarm feature level `pdt` with its sending rule, the possession challenge `CHALLENGE` (tag 12), the `sketch` grammar on `TREE_WANT` with the appended `TREE_END.sketch`, and the appended `NODES.deltas` (§8); and, in annex H, the node kinds `db`, `dn`, `rr`, `rv`, `t`, `vg`, and `bo`, `PRef.v` 2 with the kind `db`, the reserved attributes `pdt.ancestry`, `pdt.blake3`, `pdt.bao`, and `pdt.push`, BLAKE3 identities with verified windows, the negentropy and RIBLT wire, the `dcz` rule, the text leaf, the version graph, eager push, and the rules of a pin. Annex G admits the table-less tree manifest and the identity attributes, annex I.1a gives the schemas of the content swarm and of PDT 2's records, and annex I.4 lists the 36 PDT 2 entries of `golden.json`, beside the negentropy conformance vectors. A new node kind lives only under a `PRef.v` 2 head or behind a reserved attribute, a new field on a record that every member reads goes into the record's open text map, a new message field is appended and gated by the feature level, and a new entry type travels ungated, so no codec changed and every record without a new field keeps its level-1 bytes. §9.3 closes the two gaps that round seven left: Java reads the content swarm through `fromWire` (P2-X-09), and every implementation refuses a beacon document, signed or not, that holds a member of another type than Java's field (the vectors `beacon-32` to `beacon-40`). §9.4 states as guidance the shape of every wait on another peer (swarm retries, 2026-11-09; `ROBUSTNESS.md` §10), and §5.3 times the graft by the clock.

## 16. Annexes (normative)

**Annex B. WebRTC formats.** A signal body is a CBOR map with `v` (1), `kind` (`OFFER`, `ANSWER`, `CAND`, `END`), `sid` (16 bytes, the session), and per kind `sdp` (at most 16 KiB) with `fp` (1 to 4 fingerprint strings, `sha-256 <hex>`), `cand` (at most 1 KiB; empty marks the end of trickle) with `mid`, or `code` (`END`: 0 normal, 1 refused, 2 timeout, 3 protocol, 4 failed); absent keys are omitted. A signal body is a protocol record under §9.1, so Java's `RtcSignalCapability` reads it through `fromWire` and refuses an unknown key (HLC round six, 2026-11-08); the TypeScript reader of `@agentspaces/webrtc` still ignores one (§9.3). A data-channel message is one chunk: a flags byte (`0x00` final, `0x01` more) then 1 to C payload bytes, C = min(16 KiB, the remote's maxMessageSize) − 1; a frame is the concatenation of consecutive payloads up to the first final chunk, at most 8 MiB, and a receiver closes the connection on a larger frame or a malformed chunk. A dial completes or fails within 15 s. The golden vectors `rtc_offer_cbor`, `rtc_answer_cbor`, `rtc_cand_cbor`, `rtc_cand_end_cbor`, `rtc_end_timeout_cbor`, and `relay_frame_body_cbor` pin the bodies.

**Annex C. The HTTP exchange.** `POST /open` → `201` with `Location: /s/<sid>`; `POST /s/<sid>/up` with the body media type `application/vnd.agentspaces.frames` (each frame a 4-byte big-endian length then the frame; at most 64 frames and 9 MiB per body) under `Aspace-Seq`, answered `204`; `GET /s/<sid>/down` under `Aspace-Ack` answered within 25 s with a batch or `204`; `DELETE /s/<sid>` ends the session and answers a parked poll `204`; an unknown session answers `410`. A body with a bad length prefix, too many frames, or an oversize frame is refused `400` before any copy.

**Annex F. Asset operations and the Workers convention.** The entries of §6.1a: `AssetInvocation(v, asset, operation, params, invocationId)` and `AssetInvocationResult(v, invocationId, asset, operation, ok, result, error)`, schema names `ai.badmonkey.agentspaces.connect.AssetInvocations$AssetInvocation#v1` and `...$AssetInvocationResult#v1`, pinned by the golden vectors `asset_invocation_cbor` and `asset_invocation_result_cbor` beside `data_query_cbor`, `data_result_cbor`, and `asset_change_cbor`. The `access` keys a Workers connector publishes: `read-ops`, `invoke-ops`, `runtime` (`celld`, `workerd`, `cloudflare`), and per kind `prefixes` (KV, R2), `consistency=single-writer` and `object-param` (Durable Objects), `result=block` (R2 bodies); the card URI `workers://<application>/<kind>/<name>` with kinds `do`, `kv`, `d1`, `r2`, `queue`, `workflow`, and `route`.

**Annex G. Content swarm formats (v0.1.23).** `Manifest(v, root, size, pieceSize, pieces, table, tableRef, tableRefs, name, mediaType, sealing, keyEpoch, hashing, origin, created, attrs)`: `v` 1; `root` the CIDv1 string; `table` the piece hashes (32 bytes each, in order) when `pieces ≤ 64`, else null with `tableRef` the block CID of the table; `tableRefs` reserved (null); `name` at most 256 bytes; `sealing` `none` or `group-key` with `keyEpoch` when sealed; `hashing` `plain` (hashes over plaintext pieces) or `sealed`; `origin` the first publisher's PeerID; `attrs` at most 32 entries and 4 KiB. `Holding(v, root, holder, complete, have, count, via, cost)`: `have` the run-length bitfield (alternating runs of zeros and ones as unsigned varints, 7 bits per byte, zeros first, from piece 0), null when complete or above 8 KiB; `via` the relays the holder is attached to. Pipe payloads, each a CBOR map with the integer tag `t` first: `PIECE_WANT(t=1, root, index, offset, length, req)`, `PIECE(t=2, root, index, offset, bytes, req)`, `HAVE(t=3, root, have, complete)`, `CANCEL(t=4, root, index, req)`, `REJECT(t=5, root, index, reason)`, `MANIFEST_WANT(t=6, root)`, `MANIFEST(t=7, root, record)` with `record` the CBOR of the manifest value. A range is at most 4 MiB and, except at a piece's end, a power-of-two multiple of 64 KiB at an aligned offset. The relay signal payload is `RelaySignal(target, failedHop, reason)` inside the pipe body `(capability, payload)`. Golden vectors to be added: `content_manifest_inline_cbor`, `content_manifest_tableref_cbor`, `content_holding_partial_cbor`, `content_holding_complete_cbor`, `content_piece_want_cbor`, `content_piece_cbor`, `content_have_cbor`, `content_cancel_cbor`, `content_reject_cbor`, `content_manifest_want_cbor`, `content_manifest_cbor`, `content_root_cidv1`, `relay_frame_with_path_cbor`, `relay_signal_unreachable_cbor`, `peer_ad_with_via_hint_cbor`.

*Tree manifests and identities (v0.1.32; srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md P2-X-01, P2-B-06, §6.10.3 rule 11).* A manifest whose `layout` is `tree` names a blob tree whose root, a dag-cbor CID over sha2-256, names every piece, so it carries no piece table: a reader accepts a tree manifest with neither `table` nor `tableRef`, refuses one that carries both, and refuses a tree manifest whose root is no dag-cbor CID over sha2-256, so a tree manifest never stands for a flat root (`pdt_manifest_tree_cbor`). A flat fetch never asks for a table that a tree manifest lacks. A manifest MAY name its artifact's BLAKE3 identity in `attrs["pdt.blake3"]` and the root of its Bao outboard in `attrs["pdt.bao"]` (annex H); `Manifest` gains no field, so a level-1 reader passes both through. Every message above, and the manifest record that `MANIFEST` carries, reads under its annex I.1a schema (§8, §9.3).

**Annex H. Persistent data trees (v0.1.29; SRAD agentspaces-pdt §3 and §10.2).** A *persistent value* is a map, set, vector, or blob held as a wide tree of content-addressed nodes, after Clojure's collections: an update copies the path from the root to the change and shares every other node, so a *delta* (the nodes reachable from a new root that a receiver lacks) is what moves. The module is `agentspaces-pdt`; the wire is the content swarm of §8 and the entry record of §7.1.

*Nodes and CIDs.* A node is a canonical DAG-CBOR map with text keys: RFC 8949 deterministic encoding, definite lengths, shortest integer forms, keys ordered by length then bytes (the same order as §9 for these short ASCII keys), no floats and no indefinite forms in the node's own layer, and a *link* encoded as CBOR tag 42 over a byte string of `0x00` followed by the binary CID. Application keys and values (map entries, vector elements, spilled values) are embedded as raw CBOR items in the form the entry codec gives them (an indefinite-length map, a 32-bit float), spliced in and skipped over by a decoder, and each one is an application value under §9.1: it obeys rules S1 to S12 over its own bytes, tags 2, 3, and 4 included, and a decoder refuses a node whose embedded item breaks them (HLC round six, 2026-11-08, owner ruling F2); a strict DAG-CBOR decoder therefore reads every node whose embedded items are scalars, strings, or definite arrays of those (`pdt_node_dagcbor`) and refuses one with a map-valued or float-valued item (`pdt_node_embedded`). A node's CID is CIDv1 as a string: `"b"` + base32lower(`0x01`, codec, `0x12`, `0x20`, sha2-256 of the bytes), with codec `dag-cbor` (0x71) for every structured node and `raw` (0x55) for a blob chunk, which is raw bytes with no map (in `sealed-nodes` mode a sealed chunk is an `sn` map that keeps the raw codec in its CID, so a parent's link reads as before). Every node carries `k`, its kind: `m` (map node), `mc` (map collision node), `vr` (vector root), `v` (vector node), `b` (blob interior node), `x` (a spilled value), and `sn` (the sealed envelope of the `sealed-nodes` mode, below); `s` (sorted map) is reserved, and PDT 2 gives `rr` its schema beside six more kinds (v0.1.32, below). Bounds: a node is at most 1 MiB and carries at most 1,024 links (spilled-value links counted); a tree is at most 64 levels deep. A decoder refuses a node over a bound, an unknown kind, codec, or multihash, a key outside the kind's schema, or a bare link item in a key position, and names the CID and the rule. A value slot that is exactly a tag-42 link item always names a spilled node, so an application value or key is never one (hold a CID as text). Every node is verified against its CID before any use: inline delta, `NODES` answer, store read after a restart, escalation read.

*Maps and sets.* `PMap` is a CHAMP trie. The hash of a key is sha2-256 of the key's canonical CBOR; level d uses bits 5d to 5d+4 of the digest read as a big-endian bit string (bit 0 the most significant bit of digest byte 0). A node is `{k:"m", d: datamap uint32, n: nodemap uint32, e: [k0, v0, k1, v1, ...], c: [link, ...]}`: `d` marks the slots that hold an entry inline, `n` the slots that hold a child, `e` lists entries in ascending slot order, `c` children in ascending slot order; every `m` node writes all four fields, empty lists included, and the root alone adds `cnt`, the total entries. After a removal a child with one entry and no children inlines into its parent, so the layout depends only on the key set and equal maps have equal roots. Past level 51 the remaining keys sit in `{k:"mc", e:[k, v, ...]}` sorted by key bytes (level 52). A set is a map whose every value is null. The empty map root is `{c:[], d:0, e:[], k:"m", n:0, cnt:0}` (`pdt_map_empty`).

*Vectors.* `PVector` is Clojure's trie with the tail: the root is `{k:"vr", n: count, s: shift, r: link or null, t: [tail items, 1 to 32]}` (`s` = 5 and `r` null while the trie is empty), an interior node `{k:"v", c:[links]}`, a leaf `{k:"v", e:[items]}` exactly 32 wide; five index bits per level; tailoff = n < 32 ? 0 : ((n-1) >>> 5) << 5. The shape is a function of the count, so the layout is canonical (`pdt_vector_tail`, `pdt_vector_deep`).

*Blobs.* A `PBlob` is cut by FastCDC in the per-byte 2016 form with normalized chunking level 2 and cut-point skipping: minimum 16 KiB, average 64 KiB, maximum 256 KiB. The Gear table is derived, GEAR[i] = the big-endian uint64 of the first 8 bytes of sha2-256(the single byte i) for i in 0..255 (GEAR[0] = 0x6e340b9cffb37a98, GEAR[255] = 0xa8100ae6aa1940d0); the rolling hash is h = (h << 1) + GEAR[b] over unsigned 64 bits; MaskS = 0xFFFFC00000000000 applies before the average size and MaskL = 0xFFFC000000000000 after it; a cut falls at the first position at or past the minimum where (h & mask) == 0, else at the maximum (`pdt_fastcdc_cuts`, the cut points of a seeded 8 MiB stream). Each chunk is a raw node (0x55). A blob of zero or one chunk is that chunk, and its CID is the root. Otherwise the chunks are level 0 of a prolly tree: level L+1 groups the entries of level L into nodes `{k:"b", h: height (1 just above the chunks), c:[links], z:[bytes under each child]}`, closing a node after an entry whose sha2-256 digest's first four bytes, read as an unsigned big-endian integer, are below 0x08000000 (average fanout 32), with at least 4 and at most 256 entries per node, until one node remains (`pdt_blob_small`). The boundary test reads the digest itself; the CID prefix bytes play no part. A reader seeks by the `z` sums and verifies any chunk by the path of CIDs from the root. A directory is a `PMap` from relative path to a value holding the blob root as text, the size, the mode, and the media type.

*Spill.* An embedded entry value whose CBOR exceeds 1 KiB is held in its own `{k:"x", v: <item>}` node and linked from the slot; a map, array, or byte string above 4 KiB becomes a sub-tree of kind `m`, `vr`, or blob and is linked. Only an indefinite-length map or a definite array spills to a sub-tree, so reassembly is byte-identical to the entry codec's output; keys never spill; a value whose `x` node would exceed 1 MiB is refused on the way in. The same rules encode an escalated payload (§7.1).

*The ref.* `ai.badmonkey.agentspaces.agent.pdt.PRef#v1` is a reserved entry type (§7.1): `v` 1, or 2 under PDT 2 (below); `name` text, at most 256 bytes, also a tag; `kind` one of `map`, `set`, `vector`, `blob`, `tree`, and under PDT 2 `db`; `root` the CID, also a tag; `base` 0 to 8 CIDs, the roots this version was computed from; `seq` unsigned, max(seq of the bases) + 1, at most 2^53 and saturating there; `size` unsigned, entries or bytes; `nodes` optional, a list of node byte strings totalling at most 32 KiB (the inline delta); `mode` `multi` or `linear`; `sealing` `none`, `group-key`, or `sealed-nodes`; `keyEpoch` optional; `attrs` optional, a map of text to text of at most 32 entries and 4 KiB, counted as the UTF-8 bytes of its keys and text values, with `pdt.issued` and `pdt.issuer` reserved for the stamp and issuer a rewrite reproduces. The pair is advisory and only orders heads (§9, the advisory head stamp; HLC round four, 2026-11-08): a reader orders a head by `pdt.issued` only when it is text holding a canonical stamp with a counter of at most 65536 and a physical part at most the reader's `wall + MAX_DRIFT + SKEW_MARGIN`, and `pdt.issuer` is absent or text that parses as an agent id, pairing it with `pdt.issuer` or, when that attribute is absent, with the entry's own issuer; in every other case, a null or non-text value included, it orders the head by the entry's own `issued` stamp and the entry's own issuer together, and it refuses, counts, strikes, and drops nothing for the values of the pair (HLC round five, 2026-11-08; HLC round six, 2026-11-08, R1 and R2). A null or non-text value under any other key makes the `PRef` undecodable, and a reader ignores the head and counts the entry once in `headsIgnored`, as it counts an entry whose head breaks a bound, names another kind, or names another ref than its tag (R1, R3). `nodes`, `keyEpoch`, and `attrs` are omitted when absent; `mode` and `sealing` are at most 32 bytes each and are open sets a reader passes through, ignoring a head whose value it does not know; `root` and every `base` must be a 59-character base32 CIDv1 of codec 0x71 or 0x55 with a sha2-256 multihash. The largest well-formed head stays under 37 KiB. A ref is the set of live `PRef` entries with one name in one space; its heads are the entries no other live entry of that name with a larger `seq` lists in `base`, equal roots folded into one. A reader of one head returns its value; of several, the deterministic merge: key-wise three-way against a common base found by walking `base` links breadth first over known versions (at most 64 steps, a missing base fetched from the head's issuer first). The base is the first version on the latest head's walk that every other head's walk also reaches. When the walks share no version, yet every walk reaches only versions the reader knows and every version a walk reaches at its 64th step is a first version (an empty `base`), so that no walk leaves a base link unfollowed, the empty value is the shared base, and the merge keeps every key of every writer (HLC round five, 2026-11-08). Such walks end at first versions or close a revert cycle within the 64 steps. A key changed on both sides takes the head with the later issued HLC, ties by issuer PeerID bytes, and vectors and blobs take the later head whole. When the walks share no version and a walk meets a version the reader never saw, or reaches a version at its 64th step that still lists a base, the heads have no base, and the reader takes the later head whole. A swap writes the new nodes, then a head whose `base` lists the heads it read, and cancels its own superseded heads after a grace of two gossip rounds; in `linear` mode it takes the head entry first and completes it with the successor, the take claim serving as the compare-and-set. A head lease is 10 minutes by default, renewed at half-life; any member that holds a lapsed head's root complete rewrites it under its own signature with the same root, base, and seq. Vectors `pdt_ref_cbor` (two inline nodes) and `pdt_ref_min_cbor` (the nine required fields).

*The messages.* `NODE_WANT` (8), `NODES` (9), `TREE_WANT` (10), and `TREE_END` (11) as §8 states them, each a CBOR map with the integer tag `t` first so a receiver routes a frame before decoding it; `Manifest.layout` (`pieces` by default and omitted, `tree` for a blob root, any other value a validation failure). Vectors `content_node_want_cbor`, `content_nodes_cbor` (the plaintext form), `content_tree_want_cbor`, `content_tree_end_cbor`, `content_manifest_tree_cbor`. PDT 2 adds `CHALLENGE` (tag 12) and the level-2 fields of §8.

*Sealing.* Three modes, carried on `PRef.sealing` and chosen per facade. `group-key`: nodes are stored in plaintext and named by plaintext CIDs; a holder seals the canonical CBOR of the node list of each `NODES` under the current write epoch with the associated data of §8, a fetcher opens it with the epoch's opening keys (§11a.3), and only key holders hold or serve; members learn that two nodes are equal, outsiders see nothing. `sealed-nodes`: each node is sealed deterministically so keyless members and relays verify, store, and serve it. The per-epoch key is HKDF-SHA256(the group content key of the epoch, salt empty, info `aspace:pdt:node`), 32 bytes; the cipher is AES-256-GCM-SIV (RFC 8452) with a 12-byte nonce equal to the first 12 bytes of HMAC-SHA256(the per-epoch key, the plaintext node bytes) and the associated data `aspace:pdt:node|` + the epoch as decimal text (the nonce is keyed so that, in the clear, it confirms no guessed plaintext). The stored and transported bytes are the envelope, a node of kind `sn`: `{k:"sn", ct: ciphertext and tag, ep: epoch, ln: [the links of the plaintext node, in order], nc: nonce}` in the canonical form of this annex; its CID keeps the plaintext's codec (`dag-cbor` for a structured node, `raw` for a chunk), so links and the chunk rule read as before, and an `sn` node may reach 1 MiB plus 48 KiB (a 1 MiB plaintext, the tag, the nonce, and up to 1,024 links in the clear) where every other kind keeps the 1 MiB bound. A reader opens with the epoch's opening keys and refuses an envelope whose links in the clear differ from the links of the plaintext it opened to; equal plaintext nodes of one epoch seal to equal bytes and share, and nodes of an older epoch stay shared by reference. Vector `pdt_sealed_node` over a fixed test key (the bytes `0x00..0x1f`, epoch 3). `none`: for public data and groups without a content key. In every mode CIDs, sizes, `name`, and `attrs` are visible to members; in `sealed-nodes` mode a keyless member also sees the link graph of a tree (its shape, node count, node sizes, and epoch) and the equality of sealed nodes of one epoch, and nothing of their contents; node contents are visible only to key holders.

*Golden vectors.* `pdt_map_empty`, `pdt_map_small`, `pdt_map_deep`, `pdt_vector_tail`, `pdt_vector_deep`, `pdt_blob_small`, `pdt_fastcdc_cuts`, `pdt_node_dagcbor`, `pdt_node_embedded`, `pdt_ref_cbor`, `pdt_ref_min_cbor`, `content_manifest_tree_cbor`, `content_node_want_cbor`, `content_nodes_cbor`, `content_tree_want_cbor`, `content_tree_end_cbor`, `pdt_sealed_node`, `state_delta_recordless_cbor` (shipped; the sealed vector carries no `_cbor` suffix because it is a definite-length node, which the clients' wire-form round-trip loops would re-encode in the indefinite Jackson form); `pdt_map_collision` cannot be produced, since an `mc` node needs two keys with one sha2-256 digest. Every port (Python, TypeScript, Ruby where it reads trees) passes every `pdt_*` and `content_*` tree vector and cuts `pdt_fastcdc_cuts` at the same offsets; each decodes `pdt_sealed_node` as an `sn` envelope with its links in the clear, Python and Java open it under the test key, and TypeScript and Ruby, which carry no AES-GCM-SIV, check the keyed nonce (TypeScript) or the envelope alone (Ruby).

*PDT 2 (v0.1.32, normative; srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md §3 and §6.10).* PDT 2 adds a DataScript database on a ref, relaxed vectors, text, a persisted version graph, BLAKE3 identities with verified windows, intra-chunk deltas, set reconciliation, eager push, and content stores. It needs no change to the shared codec: a new node kind lives only under a `PRef.v` 2 head or behind a reserved attribute, a field added to a record that every member reads goes into the record's open text map, a field added to a message is appended and gated by the feature level of §8, and a new entry type travels ungated (§7.1). The paragraphs below give each form.

*Levels and kinds.* The level-1 kinds stay `m`, `mc`, `v`, `vr`, `b`, `x`, and `sn`, the set every 0.5.0 member reads. PDT 2 adds seven kinds. The kinds `db`, `dn`, `rr`, `rv`, and `t` live only in trees under a `PRef.v` 2 head, and `vg` and `bo` only behind the reserved attributes `pdt.ancestry` and `pdt.bao`, so no value root links a `vg` or `bo` node and no head carries one inline. The kinds `s` (sorted map), `tm` (text marks), and `ec` (erasure-coded stripe) stay reserved, and a decoder refuses them by name. A level-1 member verifies a node of a new kind by its CID, stores it, and serves it, and never reads it as a value. An escalated payload (§7.1) keeps the level-1 kinds, with `b` under its annex H keys, so every member of an escalating space reads it.

*The ref at level 2.* `PRef.v` holds 1 or 2, and `kind` gains `db`. A writer writes `v` 2 for a head whose tree holds a node of a level-2 kind and `v` 1 for every other head, so a vector ref writes `v` 1 heads again once its writer stores the packed form. The schema keeps its twelve fields, so the record decodes everywhere. A level-1 reader ignores a `v` 2 head and counts it once in `headsIgnored` (R3), so it shows the last `v` 1 head of a ref; a level-2 reader reads both versions. The attributes `pdt.ancestry`, `pdt.blake3`, `pdt.bao`, and `pdt.push` join `pdt.issued` and `pdt.issuer` as reserved keys of `attrs`. Each is advisory: a reader uses one only when its text has the form given below, otherwise treats the head as a head without it, and refuses, counts, and strikes nothing for its value. A reader judges a head live by its entry's present dots and the entry's lease register (§7.3), so a head its writer renewed stays live past the lease its record was written with, and a head its writer cancelled is gone. A writer cancels its own superseded head after the grace of two gossip rounds unless a fork still walks through it, and it renews its live heads at half their lease. Vectors `pdt_ref_v2_cbor` (a `v` 2 head of kind `db`) and `pdt_ref_ancestry_cbor` (a `v` 1 head with `pdt.ancestry`).

*The version graph.* `pdt.ancestry` holds the CID text of a `vg` node `{k:"vg", r: the head's root as CID text, b: [each base root as CID text], i: the issuer's agent id, t: the issued stamp, p: [links to the vg nodes of the bases, in the order of b]}`. The writer stores and holds the node beside the head's delta. A reader uses the attribute only when its text is a CIDv1 of codec `dag-cbor` over a sha2-256 multihash whose node decodes as a `vg` node of the head's root. A merge's base walk reads a version from the entries the reader has seen and otherwise from the version's chain node, under the same 64-step bound, and fetches a missing chain node by `NODE_WANT` from the heads' issuers, a store, or any holder, so a late joiner merges past versions whose entries are gone. A writer never cancels a version that a fork still names on the chain. Vectors `pdt_vg_chain` and `pdt_vg_merge`.

*The DataScript database.* A `db` ref holds a DataScript database as three sorted indexes and a tail. The root is `{k:"db", v: 1, s: the schema as an embedded map with keyword keys as text, me: the largest entity id, mt: the largest transaction, bf: the branching factor (128), i: {aevt: link, avet: link, eavt: link}, t: a link to the tail or null}`. An index node is `{k:"dn", l: its level (0 for a leaf), x: "eavt", "aevt", or "avet", e: [datom, ...], c: [link, ...]}`, with `c` on a branch alone and one link per child in the order of `e`; the tail is a leaf whose `x` is `"tail"` and whose datoms stand in transaction order. A datom is `[e, a, v, tx]`: an integer entity, the attribute as a keyword's print form `":ns/name"`, a value, and a non-zero integer transaction, negative for a retraction. A value is an integer, text, a boolean, a byte string, a finite float64, a keyword as text that starts with `:`, a link to a spilled value, or a tagged pair: `["u", 16 bytes]` a UUID, `["i", milliseconds]` an instant, or `["n", bytes]` a big integer, which may sit inside the long range. A string that starts with `:` or with a backslash carries one more backslash in front, and a datom holds no other embedded item. The root links the tail even when the tail travels inline on the head, and every head of a `db` ref is `v` 2. A reader compares datoms by DataScript's comparator for each index. Two `db` heads merge by datoms since their lowest common base: the assertions and retractions of both unite, a cardinality-one or unique clash keeps the later head's value and raises a conflict, and the largest transaction becomes the larger of the two plus one. Vectors `pdt_db_root`, `pdt_dn_leaf`, `pdt_dn_branch`, `pdt_dn_tail`, `pdt_datom_values`, `pdt_db_sample`, and `pdt_db_sample_answers`.

*Relaxed vectors.* `{k:"rr", n, s, r, t}` is the root of a relaxed (RRB) vector of branching 32, with the fields of `vr`. A relaxed interior node, one whose children other than the last are not all full, is `{k:"rv", c: [links], z: [cumulative sizes]}`, and every `rv` node carries `z`. A dense interior node stays a `v` node, so a dense subtree that a relaxed vector shares with a `vr` vector keeps its CID. Under an `rr` root a leaf holds 1 to 32 elements and the tail 1 to 32. A writer commits the tree as its edits left it: a tree with the `vr` shape writes the `vr` root of its elements, and any other tree writes an `rr` root, so an edit ships its path and the root of a relaxed vector depends on the edits that made it. The packed form, the leftwise-dense `vr` vector of the same elements, has one root per sequence of elements, and equality and hashing compare packed roots. A reader indexes an `rv` node by a binary search of `z` and a `v` node by radix. It refuses by name an `rv` node without `z`, a `z` under which radix indexing finds the same children, an `rv` child that holds more elements than its level holds, an `rv` node at the leaf level, a leaf where an interior node belongs, a node that does not hold the count its parent gives it, and a shift past 30, and it reads an `rr` root whose tail another writer left empty. Vectors `pdt_rr_relaxed`, `pdt_rr_packed`, and `pdt_rr_splice`.

*Text.* `{k:"t", r: [run, ...], v: 1}` holds a Fugue sequence as runs `[replica, counter, parent, side, length, deleted, text]`: the replica id as text, the counter of the run's first character, the parent as the character id `[replica, counter]` of that character's parent or null for the document root, the side `"L"` or `"R"`, the length in code points, the deleted flag, and the text, empty for a deleted run. A run is a maximal sequence of one replica's characters with consecutive counters, each the right child of the one before it, under one deleted flag. Runs sort by the id of their first character, the replica's UTF-8 bytes unsigned and then the counter, and a child of the document root sits on side `"R"`. The order reads a character's left children, then the character, then its right children, with same-side siblings in id order. A writer emits the canonical leaf, maximal runs with deleted runs stripped of their text, and a reader refuses a leaf of more than 2^20 characters, live and deleted, before it expands a run. A replica id derived from an agent id is the first five bytes of the SHA-256 of the id's text as eight lower-case base32 characters. Two texts merge by the union of their characters, deleted where either side deleted them, and raise no conflict; a character that the two sides give two parents, sides, or texts, or a union past the node bound, goes to the resolver. As a value inside a map or a vector a text is the wire encoding of its leaf's map, an indefinite map (rule S8), and a text value above 1 KiB spills to a `t` node that holds the canonical leaf. A writer puts a value that holds a text under a `v` 2 head. Vectors `pdt_text_runs`, `pdt_text_merge`, `pdt_text_edits`, `pdt_text_value`, `pdt_text_spill`, and `pdt_text_replica`.

*BLAKE3 identities and verified windows.* A blob's identity is the BLAKE3 hash of its whole content as a CIDv1 of codec `raw` (0x55) with multihash 0x1e over 32 bytes, whatever the chunking. Every head of a blob ref names the identity in `pdt.blake3` and the root of the blob's Bao outboard in `pdt.bao`, both as CID text, except over a store in the `sealed-nodes` mode, where the identity would name plaintext the group seals; the `b` node keeps its schema. An outboard node is `{k:"bo", g: 4 (16 KiB groups of 1 KiB chunks), n: the bytes it covers, p: one byte string of the subtree's parent chaining-value pairs in pre-order, 64 bytes a pair, c: [links to child bo nodes]}`. A node over at most 512 groups is a leaf that holds every parent pair of its subtree, 8 MiB in 511 pairs and a full leaf of 32,728 bytes. A larger node takes the smallest j ≥ 1 for which its groups number at most 512 × 16^j, links one child for each aligned block of 512 × 16^(j−1) groups, and holds the parent pairs above that cut, so an upper node links at most 16 children. A reader that wants bytes `[off, off+len)` fetches the chunks that cover the window and the `bo` nodes on the window's paths, and it delivers each group only when the group's chaining value verifies up to the identity, so a corrupt group refuses itself and the groups around it still arrive. A flat artifact's manifest names the same pair in its `attrs` when its publisher holds a tree store and its group holds no content key (annex G). A reader treats either attribute as absent unless its text is the canonical CID of the expected codec and hash. Structured nodes keep their sha2-256 names, and a node link over BLAKE3 stays refused. Vectors `pdt_blake3_identity`, `pdt_bo_small`, and `pdt_bo_split`.

*Intra-chunk deltas.* An entry of `NODES.deltas` (§8) is `{cid, base, dcz}`. `dcz` is a format byte, `d` for zstd, then the 40-byte header that RFC 9842 gives the `dcz` content encoding (the skippable-frame magic `5e 2a 4d 18 20 00 00 00` and the base chunk's sha-256), then exactly one zstd frame (RFC 8878) compressed with the base as a raw-content dictionary. The format byte `v` (VCDIFF) is reserved, and a reader refuses it by name. Before it decompresses, a reader refuses a frame without a content size, a content size or a window past 256 KiB, a dictionary id, a reserved header bit, a second frame or trailing bytes, a base past 256 KiB, and a base that starts with the zstd dictionary magic. It then decompresses into exactly the declared size, verifies the result against `cid`, and stores the chunk, never the frame. A writer computes its frames at edit time and keeps them only while another member advertises level 2. Frames need not match across implementations, since zstd's bytes depend on the encoder. Vectors `pdt_dcz_frame` and `content_nodes_deltas_cbor`.

*Reconciliation.* A sketch round of §8 reconciles two sets of CIDs, such as the roots two stores hold. Negentropy (leading byte 0x61) follows the reference protocol exactly: its varints, 32-byte ids equal to the sha2-256 digests of the CIDs, the modes Skip, Fingerprint, and IdList, the fingerprint as the sha-256 of the sum of the ids and the count, 16 buckets, id lists below 32 items, and a frame limit of 64 KiB. Every item stands at timestamp 0, so the items sort by digest alone, and an implementation codes timestamps as 64-bit varints; a set whose items carry a time that every member shares uses epoch seconds. A negentropy version byte other than 0x61 or 0x62 gets the reference protocol's version reply, and a reader refuses any other byte by name. RIBLT (leading byte 0x62) is the one-round form for small differences. The asker sends its 16-byte session key, the index of its first symbol, the count, and coded symbols, each the sum of its ids, the SipHash-2-4 checksum under the key as 8 little-endian bytes, and the count as a negentropy varint, under the mapping with α = 0.5 that takes at least one step and reads the state's top 53 bits as a double. It streams up to twice the expected difference and at least 64 symbols, and then continues by negentropy. The holder answers 0x62 and a status: more, decoded (the asker's extra ids, then the holder's), or too many. A holder refuses a stream that peels more ids than it has symbols, holds at most 4 RIBLT sessions of 16,384 symbols, and drops a session idle for 30 s. A round yields two lists of digests. A digest names the raw CID whose manifest the content space holds, else the dag-cbor CID, and the member then fetches by the tree exchange, `NODE_WANT`, or the piece swarm. Vectors `content_tree_want_sketch_cbor`, `content_tree_end_sketch_cbor`, and `pdt_riblt_symbols`, and the negentropy conformance vectors of `agentspaces-spec/negentropy-vectors.json`: 10 cases with every message of every round, 2 measurements, and the 2 protocol-version vectors of the reference suite.

*Eager push.* A writer that pushes a delta above the inline bound first writes the head with `pdt.push`, the canonical decimal count of its bundles, and then publishes the delta's nodes as rumours on the stream `pdt:<space>:<name>` in `PushBundle {v: 1, root, seq, part, parts, nodes}` payloads of at most 1 MiB of nodes in at most 64 parts. By default a writer pushes at most 8 MiB and 52,428 nodes of one delta (the byte bound over 160, the heap a follower spends on each node it holds aside) at 2 MiB/s, and it pushes no ref sealed with a group key, since every member relays a rumour. A member that does not follow the ref relays each bundle by item id without decoding it. A follower reads a bundle strictly and holds its nodes aside within budgets per stream and per root. It stores a node only once a live head announces a push of the node's root, only when that root reaches the node through stored nodes, and only after the node verifies against its CID. A follower that finds the root complete sends no request; past its grace (§9.4) it fetches what is still missing, and it still stores bundles that come late. It forwards only the bundles it took. No member strikes the sender of a bad bundle, since that sender is often an honest relay; the receiver drops the bundle and counts it. A follower fetches a head without `pdt.push` at once. Vector `pdt_push_bundle_cbor`.

*Stores.* A member asks the content stores of §6.1a.6 to hold a root by writing a `SignedPin` entry into the content space. The entry carries the `StorePin` terms `{v: 1, root, kind ("tree" or "artifact"), lease in seconds, copies (1 to a store's copiesMax), requester, haves (at most 8 bases the store may already hold), attrs (at most 32 entries and 4 KiB, omitted when null)}`, then `left`, the copies still to place, `notAfter` in epoch milliseconds, `key`, the requester's raw Ed25519 key, and `sig`, the requester's signature over the canonical CBOR of `{purpose: "agentspaces.store-pin", group, pin, notAfter}` (`SignedPin.Consent`, annex I.1a). The attribute `pdt.stores` names the stores the requester chose, comma separated, and a store takes a pin that names it or that names none. A store takes a signed pin only when the key hashes to the pin's requester, the signature verifies for this group, `notAfter` has not passed, and `notAfter` lies at most one hour plus a clock-skew allowance of 10 minutes ahead, whoever wrote the entry. It takes one signature once, refuses any other pin, counts the refusal, and strikes nobody. A store takes under the space's conflict strategy, fetches the root (a tree by the tree exchange with the pin's haves, an artifact by the piece swarm), pins it, writes a holding, and completes the entry with a `StoreReceipt {v: 1, root, holder, bytes, until in epoch milliseconds, blind}`. For each further copy it writes the requester's own signed pin again with `left` one fewer, and only before the deadline. A requester renews by writing a freshly signed pin with the same terms, and a store releases a root once its lease has lapsed for a holding interval with no renewal. A requester proves possession by `CHALLENGE` (§8) with picks from a cryptographic random source. It strikes a store that fails two challenges in a row on one root, stops counting that store's receipts, and replaces every root it pinned there; a replacement that finds no fitting store writes no pin, so every signed pin names its stores. A blind store holds no content key: it holds a tree only when the tree's root is a sealed node (the `sealed-nodes` mode), holds only artifacts whose `hashing` is `sealed`, refuses a `group-key` tree, and proves holding without the key. Stores reconcile their held roots with one another by sketch rounds and fetch again only the roots they hold a pin for. The `pin`, `unpin`, `status`, and `challenge` invokes (annex F) offer the same to a host that prefers a call, and an invocation's signature stands as the requester's consent. Vectors `pdt_store_pin_cbor`, `pdt_signed_pin_cbor`, `pdt_signed_pin_consent_cbor`, `pdt_store_receipt_cbor`, `content_challenge_cbor`, and `asset_card_content_store_cbor`.

*PDT 2 vectors.* The 36 entries above join `golden.json` after the first design's and change none of its entries, and they pass in Java, Python, TypeScript, and Ruby (§9.3). `pdt_manifest_tree_cbor` gives the table-less manifest of annex G for the blob of `pdt_blob_small`.

**Annex I. The wire encoding: record schemas and vectors (v0.1.31; HLC round six, 2026-11-08; HLC round seven, 2026-11-08).** This annex holds the two tables §9.1 cites.

*I.1 Protocol record schemas.* Every protocol record a carrier reaches, with its fields in wire order, generated on 2026-11-08 from Java's own serializers. `?null` marks a field omitted when null, `?empty` a field omitted when null or empty, and every other field is always present (P1). Ids, stamps, and `AgentId` are text (P2). `PRef.attrs` reads as `map<String,String>`, and R1 refines it for `pdt.issued` and `pdt.issuer`. The records of the aggregate, gossip-learning, semantic-discovery, Raft, and WebRTC signalling capabilities travel inside `PipeFrame.payload` and follow the same rules; Java reads them through `fromWire`, Python through `records.py`, TypeScript through `records.ts`, `packages/ordered/src/raft.ts` and `packages/webrtc/src/signal.ts`, and Ruby through `schema.rb`, and the table lists them last, generated the same way from those roots (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08). No client reads `GossipLearner.Exchange`, and Python and Ruby read no `RtcSignal`. The beacon document is JSON and has no row here; §9.1 gives its canonical form. Four of their byte fields stay opaque to the record rules: `GossipLearner.Exchange.inline` holds model bytes the learner's own codec reads, `RaftMessages.LogEntry.command` and `RaftMessages.ClientSubmit.command` hold an `OrderedTakes.ClaimCommand` document its own reader decodes, and `SemanticDiscovery.SemHit.adBytes` holds signed advertisement bytes read on the advertisement path. *The v0.1.32 rows (AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09).* `AgentCard.spill`, `AgentCard.Parts`, `AppCard.origin`, `AppCard.formats`, `AppCard.spill`, `AppCard.Origin`, `AppCard.Parts`, and `CardSpill` come from ISSUE-AppCardOriginFormats §9.1 ahead of the build, and the generator writes them from Java's serializers when the build lands. `AppCard.Parts` and `AgentCard.Parts` are the content-swarm documents of §6.1a.5, which a reader decodes under P1 to P4 like any carrier. The value rules of `kind`, `signOn`, `origin`, `formats`, and `spill` sit in §6.1a.2 to §6.1a.5, beyond the kinds P2 checks. *The static app rows (static apps, 2026-11-09).* `PublishStatic`, `StaticBundle`, and `StaticFile` come from `agentspaces-a2ui` and serve the owner decision on static apps in ISSUE-AppCardOriginFormats: an app of kind `assembled` whose files an agent publishes and a host serves as is. `PublishStatic` is a new record type beside `Publish`, because P1 refuses a field `Publish` does not name. An agent writes it into the group's `views` space. It names the bundle by its content root (annex G, the form of `CardSpill.root`) and its length in bytes (`size`, at least 1), the file a person opens first (`entry`, `index.html` by default and always one of `files`), and each file's path with the media type a host serves it under (`files`, 1 to 256 entries). A path holds 1 to 256 characters in segments joined by `/`, and each segment starts with a letter or digit followed by letters, digits, `.`, `_`, `~`, or `-`, so no path climbs out of the app or names a hidden file. A media type is RFC 6838's `type/subtype` in lower case with at most a `charset` parameter. `signOn` takes the values of `Publish.signOn` (§6.1a.4) with the same default `idp`. `StaticBundle` is the content-swarm document the root names: every file in path order, each path once. A host fetches it with the stated length, decodes it under P1 to P4 with the canonical-bytes check, and serves a file only when the bundle holds exactly the files `PublishStatic.files` names. The golden vectors `publish_static_cbor` and `static_bundle_cbor` in `agentspaces-a2ui/src/test/resources/vectors` pin the bytes.

*I.1a The content swarm and PDT 2 records (v0.1.32; srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md §6.10.4).* The records of the content swarm, which Java reads through `fromWire` since PDT 2 phase 1, and the records PDT 2 adds, in the notation of I.1, from Java's serializers on 2026-11-09. Every content-swarm message keeps its integer tag `t` first. The fields marked `?null` on `Messages.Nodes`, `Messages.TreeEnd`, and `Messages.Pick` are the level-2 fields of §8, which a writer omits when null, so every message without them keeps its level-1 bytes. `StorePin.lease` counts seconds, and `SignedPin.notAfter` and `StoreReceipt.until` hold epoch milliseconds, as `Manifest.created` does. A `Pick` without `offset` and `length` names a node, and a pick with both names a chunk group of a BLAKE3 blob. The `PRef` schema of I.1 does not change: `v` holds 1 or 2, and `kind` gains `db`. `TREE_WANT` keeps its schema, and a level-2 holder accepts `root` `""` only when `sketch` is set.

```
Messages.Challenge { t: int, req: int, root: String, picks: list<Messages.Pick> }
Messages.Pick { cid: String, offset: Long ?null, length: Integer ?null }
StorePin { v: int, root: String, kind: String, lease: long, copies: int, requester: PeerId, haves: list<String>, attrs: map<String,String> ?null }
SignedPin { v: int, pin: StorePin, left: int, notAfter: long, key: bytes, sig: bytes }
SignedPin.Consent { purpose: String, group: String, pin: StorePin, notAfter: long }
StoreReceipt { v: int, root: String, holder: PeerId, bytes: long, until: long, blind: boolean }
PushBundle { v: int, root: String, seq: long, part: int, parts: int, nodes: list<bytes> }
```

*I.2 Vectors.* 205 vectors (HLC round seven, 2026-11-08; nine beacon documents added 2026-11-09, v0.1.32). 165 are CBOR carriers, 51 to accept and 114 to refuse as undecodable (drop, count as undecodable, strike nobody): 31 frames, 36 space deltas (13 states and 23 claims), 12 `PRef` documents, 6 signed peer advertisements, and 80 application documents. 40 are beacon documents (carrier `beacon-document`), 7 to accept and 33 to refuse, whose `hex` holds the JSON bytes. Java signed the base carriers with the golden fixture key (`private_key_pkcs8` and `public_key_raw` of `golden.json`). A relabel vector keeps Java's signature over another form of the same value, so a receiver that verified over a repaired re-encoding accepted it before v0.1.31. In `frame-23` to `frame-31`, `claim-20` to `claim-23`, `delta-13`, and `ad-01` to `ad-06` the sender signed the bytes the vector holds, which shows that an honest signature does not rescue a non-canonical carrier and gives every accept case a signature each implementation verifies. The vectors ship as `vectors.json` beside the core repository's golden vectors (`agentspaces/tools/wire-encoding/vectors.json`), with byte-identical copies in `agentspaces-python/tests/wire_encoding_vectors.json`, `agentspaces-typescript/test/wire-encoding-vectors.json`, and `agentspaces-rb/gems/agentspaces/test/wire-encoding-vectors.json` (the Ruby copy since HLC round seven, 2026-11-08, with a test that compares it). Each entry carries `id`, `carrier`, `hex`, `verdict`, `rule`, `why`, and `structural`, the verdict of the item rules S1 to S12 alone, which Java's `WireEncodingTest` checks (`json` on a beacon document). A beacon document also carries `signer`, the PeerID a reader verifies for an accepted signed document, and null otherwise. The probe fields `java_today` on every entry and `python_today`, `typescript_today`, and `ruby_today` on the 80 documents and on `beacon-32` to `beacon-40` record what each implementation did before the changes of the round that added the vector, and no test reads them. The table gives a vector's bytes when they hold at most 20 bytes, and its length otherwise. The column Rule names the rule of §9.1 or §9.2 that decides the vector: `base` marks an honest carrier the relabels start from, `S1 to S12` a document Java's codec wrote, `signed views` the dot order of §9.1, and `F3` on a beacon document its canonical JSON (§9.1).

| `beacon-23` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 294 bytes | a signature that is a number; Jackson reads it as the text 123, so the document is not its canonical JSON |
| `beacon-24` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 380 bytes | an issuerKey without its base64 padding, signed over that text: the key is not the canonical text of its bytes |
| `beacon-25` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 397 bytes | a ttl of 2^63, signed: a long does not hold it |
| `beacon-26` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 390 bytes | a seed priority of 2^31, signed: an int does not hold it |
| `beacon-27` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 383 bytes | a ttl of 1e400, past every number type |
| `beacon-28` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 283 bytes | a group that is text |
| `beacon-29` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 70 bytes | an unsigned document with a parameter by a forbidden name; Java's Group record refuses it signed or not |
| `beacon-30` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 46 bytes | an unsigned document with a seed that is null |
| `beacon-31` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 31 bytes | an unsigned document with a group that has no name |
| `beacon-32` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 40 bytes | an unsigned document whose group name is a JSON number; Jackson coerced it to the text 7, Python and Ruby kept the number, and TypeScript refused it (SPEC 9.3, v0.1.31) |
| `beacon-33` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 54 bytes | an unsigned document with ttl as the text "300" |
| `beacon-34` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 103 bytes | an unsigned document with a seed priority of 1.0, a JSON number with a fraction where an int belongs |
| `beacon-35` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 56 bytes | an unsigned document whose issuer is a boolean |
| `beacon-36` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 68 bytes | an unsigned document with a parameter whose value is a number |
| `beacon-37` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 63 bytes | an unsigned document with a wire hint as the text "1" |
| `beacon-38` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 40 bytes | an unsigned document whose groups member is an object where an array belongs |
| `beacon-39` | beacon document | refuse | F3 | 44 bytes | an unsigned document with v as the text "1" |
| `beacon-40` | beacon document | accept | F3 | 68 bytes | an unsigned document with its members out of order, a null seeds member, and unknown members of other types: an unsigned document stays lenient in form, so the reader reads it and names no signer |

*I.3 The v0.1.32 entries (AppCard origin and formats, 2026-11-09; neutral vectors, 2026-11-09).* The AppCard revision changes two entries of `golden.json` and adds 25; ISSUE-AppCardOriginFormats §9.6 gives each one's inputs. `agent_card_ui_cbor` and `app_card_host_cbor` take neutral values, the second with `signOn` `idp`. The accept entries are `app_card_origin_formats_cbor`, `app_card_spill_cbor`, `app_card_parts_cbor`, `agent_card_spill_cbor`, and `agent_card_parts_cbor`; each parts document's content root, computed over 65,536-byte pieces, equals its stub's `spill.root`, and its length equals `spill.size`. The value entries `card_spill_threshold_bytes` (65536) and `card_spill_warn_bytes` (57344) pin the two constants of §6.1a.5. The 18 refusal entries, each undecodable by §6.1a.2 or §6.1a.5, are `adv_app_card_kind_realm_cbor`, `adv_app_card_sign_on_tenant_cbor`, `adv_app_card_formats_empty_cbor`, `adv_app_card_origin_attrs_empty_cbor`, `adv_app_card_formats_bound_cbor`, `adv_app_card_formats_upper_cbor`, `adv_app_card_formats_duplicate_cbor`, `adv_app_card_formats_parameter_cbor`, `adv_app_card_origin_system_cbor`, `adv_app_card_origin_attrs_prefix_cbor`, `adv_app_card_origin_ref_bound_cbor`, `adv_app_card_spill_placeholder_cbor`, `adv_app_card_spill_parts_order_cbor`, `adv_app_card_spill_parts_unknown_cbor`, `adv_app_card_spill_parts_empty_cbor`, `adv_app_card_spill_root_codec_cbor`, `adv_app_card_spill_size_zero_cbor`, and `adv_agent_card_spill_actions_cbor`. The entries live in `golden.json` alone, whose suite every implementation runs; `vectors.json` keeps its six carriers, none of which is an advertisement, and I.2's counts stay as they are. Every entry holds neutral values only: example catalogs (`example-widgets`), the `example.org` namespace, and the registered systems of `REGISTRY.md`. The non-normative `agentspaces-spec/golden-examples.json` holds the realistic examples of one console in the same format; no conformance claim rests on it, and the neutrality check reads `golden.json`, the core sources, and this specification, and leaves that file alone.

*I.4 The PDT 2 entries (v0.1.32; srad/agentspaces-pdt2.md §10).* PDT 2 adds 36 entries to `golden.json` after `pdt_sealed_node` and changes no earlier entry, so the file holds 293 entries: `pdt_db_root`, `pdt_dn_leaf`, `pdt_dn_branch`, `pdt_dn_tail`, `pdt_datom_values`, `pdt_store_pin_cbor`, `pdt_store_receipt_cbor`, `pdt_blake3_identity`, `pdt_bo_small`, `pdt_bo_split`, `pdt_rr_relaxed`, `pdt_rr_packed`, `pdt_text_runs`, `pdt_text_merge`, `pdt_vg_chain`, `pdt_ref_ancestry_cbor`, `pdt_vg_merge`, `pdt_ref_v2_cbor`, `pdt_db_sample`, `pdt_db_sample_answers`, `pdt_signed_pin_cbor`, `pdt_signed_pin_consent_cbor`, `pdt_manifest_tree_cbor`, `pdt_rr_splice`, `pdt_text_edits`, `pdt_text_value`, `pdt_text_spill`, `pdt_text_replica`, `pdt_dcz_frame`, `content_nodes_deltas_cbor`, `content_challenge_cbor`, `asset_card_content_store_cbor`, `content_tree_want_sketch_cbor`, `content_tree_end_sketch_cbor`, `pdt_push_bundle_cbor`, and `pdt_riblt_symbols`. Annex H says what each holds, and the generators `golden/PdtVectors`, `golden/SequenceVectors`, `golden/BlobVectors`, and `golden/PushVectors` of `agentspaces-pdt` and `golden/StoreVectors` of `agentspaces-store` produce them, written by `tools/golden/Pdt2GoldenVectors.java` and `tools/golden/GoldenVectors.java`, which only add entries. `pdt_bo_split` covers 8 MiB and 5,000 bytes of the `java.util.Random(42)` stream since the 8 MiB outboard leaf of 2026-11-09. The negentropy conformance vectors ship apart, in `agentspaces-spec/negentropy-vectors.json` with a byte-identical copy in `agentspaces-typescript/test/negentropy-vectors.json`. The nine beacon vectors `beacon-32` to `beacon-40` of I.2 (2026-11-09) pin the closing of the second gap of §9.3: eight unsigned documents with a member of another type than Java's field, each refused, and one unsigned document in a lenient form with unknown members of other types, which a reader accepts and for which it names no signer.

*End of specification v0.1.32.*
